# Paediatrics — Bookmark-Derived Pearls

*Distilled from bookmarked INI-CET / NEET-PG MCQs. Only high-yield, revisable learning points were kept; points already covered by the existing pearl collection were dropped.*

---

## 1. Chlamydial Pneumonia of Infancy

**PEARL:** An infant of **1–3 months with a preceding CONJUNCTIVITIS who develops an AFEBRILE, INSIDIOUS pneumonia with a STACCATO COUGH, TACHYPNOEA, RALES, NO WHEEZE and PERIPHERAL EOSINOPHILIA (>400/µL), with hyperinflation and diffuse infiltrates on X-ray, has *CHLAMYDIA TRACHOMATIS* PNEUMONIA.** Treat with **ORAL ERYTHROMYCIN or azithromycin for 14 days — TOPICAL therapy is useless.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The organism is acquired from the mother's genital tract at delivery and **colonises the conjunctiva and nasopharynx first**, descending to the lungs weeks later — which is why the **conjunctivitis (day 5–14) precedes the pneumonia (week 4–12)** and why the two are part of one illness. The **ABSENCE OF FEVER AND WHEEZE is the key discriminator from RSV bronchiolitis**, and the eosinophilia is the laboratory fingerprint.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Because the nasopharynx is colonised, SYSTEMIC (oral) therapy is mandatory — topical eye treatment neither cures the conjunctivitis nor prevents the pneumonia. TREAT THE MOTHER AND HER PARTNER.**
- **Erythromycin in infants under 6 weeks carries a small risk of INFANTILE HYPERTROPHIC PYLORIC STENOSIS — parents should be warned.**
- **Contrast the neonatal pneumonias: GROUP B STREPTOCOCCUS (early-onset, within 24 hours, with sepsis and shock — the commonest cause of early neonatal sepsis in the West); *S. AUREUS* (pneumatoceles, empyema); RSV (wheeze, fever, seasonal, hyperinflation); and *PNEUMOCYSTIS* (in HIV-exposed infants, with severe hypoxia).**
- **Ophthalmia neonatorum timing repeats here: CHEMICAL <24 hours; GONOCOCCAL 2–5 days (hyperacute, purulent, sight-threatening — ceftriaxone); CHLAMYDIAL 5–14 days (mucopurulent, needs oral erythromycin).**
- **In India, the WHO/IMNCI approach classifies pneumonia by RESPIRATORY RATE and CHEST INDRAWING — fast breathing is ≥60/min under 2 months, ≥50/min at 2–12 months and ≥40/min at 1–5 years.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Conjunctivitis then afebrile pneumonia with eosinophilia → ***Chlamydia trachomatis***.

**REMEMBER:** *No fever, no wheeze, but eosinophils and a preceding sticky eye — that is chlamydia.*

`Q9050`

---

## 2. Transposition of the Great Arteries

**PEARL:** An **"EGG-ON-STRING" (egg-on-side) cardiac silhouette with a NARROW VASCULAR PEDICLE, INCREASED pulmonary blood flow and pulmonary venous hypertension = TRANSPOSITION OF THE GREAT ARTERIES.** The chamber that enlarges is the **LEFT ATRIUM (from increased pulmonary venous return), NOT the right atrium.** Immediate management is **PROSTAGLANDIN E1 to keep the ductus open, then BALLOON ATRIAL SEPTOSTOMY (RASHKIND) and definitive ARTERIAL SWITCH in the first 2 weeks.**

**UNDERSTAND:** In TGA the two circulations run in **PARALLEL rather than in series** — deoxygenated blood returns to the body and oxygenated blood returns to the lungs — so **survival depends entirely on MIXING** through the foramen ovale, a VSD or the ductus. That is why the baby is **profoundly cyanosed from birth, deteriorates as the duct closes, and why PGE1 is life-saving.** The narrow pedicle results from the great vessels lying anteroposteriorly rather than side by side.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **TGA is the commonest cyanotic heart disease presenting IN THE FIRST 24 HOURS, and it is the lesion MOST SPECIFICALLY associated with an INFANT OF A DIABETIC MOTHER. Cyanosis is severe and does NOT improve with oxygen (the HYPEROXIA TEST).**
- **Radiological signatures worth memorising: "EGG ON STRING" — TGA; "BOOT-SHAPED" heart (coeur en sabot) with a right aortic arch and DECREASED pulmonary flow — TETRALOGY OF FALLOT; "SNOWMAN"/figure-of-8 — total anomalous pulmonary venous connection (supracardiac); "BOX-SHAPED" massive cardiomegaly with a "wall-to-wall" heart — EBSTEIN'S ANOMALY.**
- **CYANOTIC lesions with INCREASED pulmonary blood flow: TGA, TAPVC, truncus arteriosus, single ventricle. With DECREASED flow: Fallot, tricuspid atresia, pulmonary atresia. The "5 Ts" mnemonic maps to the number of letters — Truncus (1 vessel), TGA (2), Tricuspid atresia (3), TAPVC (4), Tetralogy... (with pentalogy 5).**
- **DUCT-DEPENDENT lesions need PGE1: TGA, pulmonary atresia, critical pulmonary stenosis, tricuspid atresia, hypoplastic left heart, critical coarctation and interrupted arch. Watch for APNOEA as a side effect of prostaglandin.**
- **ALL cyanotic newborns need pre- and post-ductal saturations, a hyperoxia test and urgent ECHOCARDIOGRAPHY; PULSE OXIMETRY SCREENING at 24–48 hours detects critical congenital heart disease before collapse.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Egg-on-string X-ray → **TGA, with LEFT (not right) atrial enlargement**.

**REMEMBER:** *Two circulations in parallel — the baby lives only as long as something mixes.*

`Q9051`

---

## 3. Fetal Lung Maturity and the Fetal Adrenal

**PEARL:** Indicators of **FETAL LUNG MATURITY: LECITHIN:SPHINGOMYELIN RATIO ≥2, the presence of PHOSPHATIDYLGLYCEROL, a POSITIVE SHAKE (Clements' bubble) TEST, a LAMELLAR BODY COUNT >50,000/µL, and the NILE BLUE test showing >50% ORANGE (not blue) CELLS.** The **major steroid product of the FETAL ADRENAL is DHEA/DHEA-S**, the substrate for placental oestrogen synthesis.

**UNDERSTAND:** The L:S ratio works because **sphingomyelin stays constant through pregnancy while LECITHIN rises sharply from ~34 weeks as type II pneumocytes mature** — so the ratio is a clock rather than a concentration. **Phosphatidylglycerol appears LAST (~35–36 weeks) and is therefore the most reliable single marker, and it is unaffected by blood or meconium contamination.** The shake test simply asks whether enough surfactant is present to stabilise a ring of bubbles against ethanol.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **ANTENATAL CORTICOSTEROIDS (betamethasone 12 mg × 2, 24 hours apart, or dexamethasone 6 mg × 4) accelerate lung maturity when given between 24 and 34 weeks, with maximum benefit 24 hours to 7 days after the course — they reduce RDS, intraventricular haemorrhage, necrotising enterocolitis and neonatal death.**
- **The FETAL ADRENAL is proportionally huge, dominated by the FETAL ZONE, which lacks 3β-hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase and therefore makes DHEA-S rather than cortisol; the placenta converts DHEA-S to oestriol — which is why LOW maternal OESTRIOL suggests fetal adrenal or placental compromise, anencephaly or a sulfatase deficiency.**
- **PLACENTAL CRH drives the fetal adrenal to make DHEA-S and cortisol; cortisol then stimulates more placental CRH — a POSITIVE feedback loop that is central to the onset of labour and to lung maturation.**
- **SURFACTANT (dipalmitoyl phosphatidylcholine) is made by TYPE II PNEUMOCYTES from ~24 weeks, reaching adequate levels by 34–36 weeks; deficiency causes RESPIRATORY DISTRESS SYNDROME with a "GROUND-GLASS" reticulogranular pattern and AIR BRONCHOGRAMS on X-ray, treated with surfactant replacement and CPAP.**
- **Conditions ACCELERATING lung maturity: chronic intrauterine stress, pre-eclampsia, IUGR, prolonged rupture of membranes and maternal steroids. DELAYING it: MATERNAL DIABETES (insulin antagonises cortisol), hydrops and male sex.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Not an indicator of lung maturity" → **Nile blue showing >50% BLUE cells** (it is >50% ORANGE).

**REMEMBER:** *Lecithin climbs and phosphatidylglycerol arrives last — and orange cells, not blue, mean a mature lung.*

`Q9052, Q9053`

## 4. Hurler Syndrome and the Mucopolysaccharidoses

**PEARL:** A child of **6–24 months with COARSE FACIES, MACROGLOSSIA, thick lips, prominent forehead, HEPATOSPLENOMEGALY, CORNEAL CLOUDING, joint stiffness, short stature and PERSISTENT COPIOUS NASAL DISCHARGE with noisy breathing = HURLER SYNDROME (MPS I)** — autosomal recessive **α-L-IDURONIDASE deficiency** with accumulation of **DERMATAN and HEPARAN SULPHATE.** The skeletal signature is **DYSOSTOSIS MULTIPLEX.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Glycosaminoglycans accumulate in **every connective tissue**, which is why the disease is so multisystem and why the facial and airway features dominate: **thickened mucosa and adenotonsillar tissue cause the chronic nasal discharge, noisy breathing and OBSTRUCTIVE SLEEP APNOEA**, while GAG deposition in the cornea, valves, joints and meninges produces clouding, valvular disease, contractures and hydrocephalus. **The child is normal at birth and deteriorates progressively — a "storage" pattern.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **DYSOSTOSIS MULTIPLEX: thick oar-shaped RIBS, OVOID/beaked vertebral bodies with anterior inferior beaking and gibbus, a J-SHAPED SELLA, macrocephaly, wide flared iliac wings, and expanded diaphyses with irregular metaphyses.**
- **Distinguish within the group: HURLER (MPS I, autosomal recessive) — corneal clouding PRESENT, severe intellectual disability, death in the first decade. HUNTER (MPS II) — X-LINKED RECESSIVE, NO CORNEAL CLOUDING, with pebbly ivory skin lesions and a milder course. SCHEIE is the attenuated form of MPS I. MORQUIO (MPS IV) — normal intelligence with severe skeletal dysplasia and ATLANTOAXIAL INSTABILITY (a critical anaesthetic risk). SANFILIPPO (MPS III) — severe behavioural/neurological disease with MILD somatic features.**
- **Diagnosis: urinary GAG screening, then ENZYME ASSAY in leucocytes/fibroblasts (definitive) and molecular confirmation. Treatment: ENZYME REPLACEMENT THERAPY (laronidase) and HAEMATOPOIETIC STEM CELL TRANSPLANTATION, which — if performed EARLY (before 2 years and before cognitive decline) — preserves neurodevelopment because donor-derived cells cross into the CNS. Enzyme replacement does NOT cross the blood–brain barrier.**
- **The differential of COARSE FACIES: mucopolysaccharidoses, mucolipidoses (I-cell disease — coarse features and gingival hyperplasia AT BIRTH), GM1 gangliosidosis, CONGENITAL HYPOTHYROIDISM (which must be excluded first, as it is treatable), and Beckwith–Wiedemann syndrome (macroglossia, omphalocele, hemihypertrophy, hypoglycaemia).**
- **Anaesthesia in MPS is hazardous — a difficult airway, cervical instability and cardiac involvement.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Coarse facies + macroglossia + hepatosplenomegaly + copious nasal discharge → **Hurler syndrome**.

**REMEMBER:** *Normal at birth, then coarsening everywhere — and Hunter is the X-linked one with clear corneas.*

`Q9054`

---

## 5. Paediatric Resuscitation and the Apgar Score

**PEARL:** **In children, cardiac arrest is usually the END RESULT OF RESPIRATORY INSUFFICIENCY AND HYPOXIA, not a primary cardiac arrhythmia** — hypoxia, hypercapnia and acidosis cause bradycardia, hypotension and then asystole. **VENTRICULAR arrhythmias are UNCOMMON in children; SVT is the commonest tachyarrhythmia needing treatment.** The **APGAR score** grades appearance, pulse, grimace, activity and respiration 0–2 each at 1 and 5 minutes: **0–3 = severe distress, 4–6 = moderate, 7–10 = good.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Because the arrest is **respiratory in origin, the airway and breathing take absolute priority** — which is why paediatric CPR uses a **higher ratio of ventilations (15:2 with two rescuers, 30:2 single rescuer) and why the FIRST intervention in a collapsed child is 5 RESCUE BREATHS**, in contrast to the compression-first, defibrillation-focused adult algorithm. It also explains why **BRADYCARDIA in a child is treated with OXYGEN AND VENTILATION first**, and chest compressions started if the heart rate stays below 60 with poor perfusion.

**MUST KNOW:**
- ***THE APGAR SCORE IS NOT USED TO GUIDE RESUSCITATION*** — resuscitation begins before the 1-minute score. Its value is in documenting the RESPONSE to resuscitation; if the 5-minute score is <7, repeat every 5 minutes for 20 minutes.
- **Worked examples: pink body with blue extremities (1) + heart rate 80 (1) + grimace (1) + some flexion (1) + irregular respiration (1) = 5. Central cyanosis (0) + heart rate 60 (1) + grimace (1) + some flexion (1) + irregular gasping (1) = 4.**
- **Causes of a LOW/false-positive Apgar despite a healthy fetus: PREMATURITY, maternal DRUGS (opioids, sedatives, magnesium sulphate), acute cerebral trauma, precipitate delivery, airway obstruction (choanal atresia), congenital pneumonia/sepsis and hypovolaemia. FALSE-NEGATIVE (a good score despite acidosis): maternal acidosis and high fetal catecholamines.**
- **The NEONATAL RESUSCITATION sequence: the three questions at birth (term? crying/breathing? good tone?) → warmth, position, clear airway, dry and stimulate → if apnoeic or heart rate <100, POSITIVE PRESSURE VENTILATION (the single most important step) → if heart rate <60 despite effective ventilation, CHEST COMPRESSIONS at 3:1 with ventilation → ADRENALINE. Start term babies on ROOM AIR and preterms on 21–30% oxygen, titrated by saturation targets.**
- **The four phases of cardiac arrest: PRE-ARREST, NO-FLOW (untreated arrest), LOW-FLOW (CPR in progress) and POST-RESUSCITATION — the "no-flow" phase FOLLOWS, not precedes, the onset of arrest.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "True about paediatric resuscitation" → **dysrhythmias are usually due to respiratory insufficiency**; Apgar calculations are straight arithmetic.

**REMEMBER:** *Children arrest because they stop breathing — so breathe for them first.*

`Q9055, Q9065, Q9072`

---

## 6. Juvenile Myoclonic Epilepsy

**PEARL:** An adolescent who **DROPS OBJECTS IN THE MORNING with PRESERVED CONSCIOUSNESS, often with a family history and generalised tonic–clonic seizures on awakening, has JUVENILE MYOCLONIC EPILEPSY (JANZ SYNDROME)** — the commonest generalised epilepsy of adolescence. **EEG shows 4–6 Hz POLYSPIKE-AND-WAVE discharges with PHOTOSENSITIVITY.** Precipitants: **SLEEP DEPRIVATION, ALCOHOL and PHOTIC STIMULATION.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The **morning timing is diagnostic** — myoclonic jerks cluster within 1–2 hours of waking because generalised epileptiform discharges are facilitated during the **transition from sleep to wakefulness**, when thalamocortical synchronisation is greatest. This is also why sleep deprivation is such a potent trigger, and why "the patient spills her tea at breakfast" is the classic history that patients themselves never think worth reporting.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Treatment: VALPROATE is most effective but is AVOIDED IN GIRLS OF CHILDBEARING AGE (teratogenicity and neurodevelopmental harm) — use LEVETIRACETAM or lamotrigine (though lamotrigine can worsen myoclonus). ***CARBAMAZEPINE, PHENYTOIN, OXCARBAZEPINE AND GABAPENTIN WORSEN myoclonic and absence seizures*** and must be avoided. Treatment is usually LIFELONG — relapse after withdrawal is the rule.**
- **Distinguish the childhood epilepsy syndromes: CHILDHOOD ABSENCE EPILEPSY (4–8 years, brief blank spells with 3 Hz SPIKE-AND-WAVE, provoked by HYPERVENTILATION, treated with ETHOSUXIMIDE or valproate — excellent prognosis). BENIGN ROLANDIC EPILEPSY (CENTROTEMPORAL SPIKES — nocturnal focal seizures with facial twitching and drooling in a 5–10-year-old; benign, remits by adolescence, often needs no treatment). LENNOX–GASTAUT (multiple seizure types, SLOW 1–2.5 Hz spike-and-wave, intellectual disability, refractory). WEST SYNDROME (infantile spasms in clusters, HYPSARRHYTHMIA, developmental regression — treat with ACTH/vigabatrin, the latter especially in TUBEROUS SCLEROSIS).**
- **The three cardinal seizure types of JME are myoclonic jerks (always present), generalised tonic–clonic seizures (~90%) and absences (~30%).**
- **Neuroimaging is normal — JME is a genetic generalised epilepsy, so MRI is not required if the history and EEG are typical.**
- **Counselling on sleep hygiene, alcohol avoidance and driving regulations matters as much as the drug.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Morning jerks with retained consciousness in a teenager → **juvenile myoclonic epilepsy**.

**REMEMBER:** *She drops her toothbrush every morning, stays fully aware, and the EEG shows polyspikes.*

`Q9056`

---

## 7. Growing Skull Fracture

**PEARL:** A child **UNDER 3 YEARS with a PROGRESSIVELY ENLARGING skull swelling or palpable defect MONTHS TO YEARS after a skull fracture — most often PARIETAL — has a GROWING SKULL FRACTURE (leptomeningeal cyst, traumatic encephalocele).** It requires **SURGICAL REPAIR OF THE DURA AND CRANIOPLASTY** — it will not heal spontaneously.

**UNDERSTAND:** The essential lesion is a **DURAL TEAR beneath a diastatic fracture.** The **pulsating brain, arachnoid or dilated ventricle herniates through the tear** and, with each cardiac and respiratory pulsation, progressively erodes the fracture edges apart — so the defect **widens instead of healing.** It occurs almost exclusively under 3 years because that is when the **brain is growing fastest and the dura is thin and adherent.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **It may present LATE with headache, SEIZURES or a focal neurological deficit rather than a lump — so any child with a post-traumatic seizure years later and a skull defect needs imaging.**
- **CT shows the widened fracture with scalloped margins; MRI defines the herniated contents and any encephalomalacia.**
- **Distinguish the scalp swellings of infancy: CAPUT SUCCEDANEUM (present AT BIRTH, CROSSES suture lines, pitting, resolves in days); CEPHALHAEMATOMA (appears HOURS after birth, subperiosteal so it does NOT cross suture lines, resolves over weeks and may calcify or cause jaundice); SUBGALEAL HAEMORRHAGE (a boggy swelling crossing sutures that can hold the ENTIRE BLOOD VOLUME — a cause of hypovolaemic shock, especially after ventouse).**
- **The other late complications of paediatric head injury: post-traumatic seizures, hydrocephalus, chronic subdural collection and second-impact syndrome.**
- **In any infant with an unexplained skull fracture or intracranial bleed, ***CONSIDER NON-ACCIDENTAL INJURY*** — look for retinal haemorrhages, fractures of different ages, and an inconsistent history.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Enlarging parietal swelling a year after a fracture in a toddler → **growing skull fracture**.

**REMEMBER:** *A torn dura lets the brain pulse through — the fracture grows instead of healing.*

`Q9057`

---

## 8. Neonatal Seizures

**PEARL:** **HYPOXIC–ISCHAEMIC ENCEPHALOPATHY is the commonest cause of neonatal seizures overall; HYPOGLYCAEMIA is the commonest METABOLIC cause. HYPONATRAEMIA, HYPERNATRAEMIA, HYPOCALCAEMIA AND HYPOMAGNESAEMIA ALL cause them.** Seizures unresponsive to conventional anticonvulsants should prompt a trial of **PYRIDOXINE (vitamin B6) — PYRIDOXINE-DEPENDENT EPILEPSY, usually from ANTIQUITIN (ALDH7A1) deficiency, with raised α-aminoadipic semialdehyde and pipecolic acid.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Pyridoxal-5-phosphate is the **cofactor for GLUTAMIC ACID DECARBOXYLASE, which converts glutamate (excitatory) to GABA (inhibitory)** — so deficiency leaves the neonatal brain flooded with excitation and starved of inhibition. No conventional anticonvulsant can correct a missing cofactor, which is exactly why the seizures are refractory and why **giving 50–100 mg of pyridoxine intravenously (with EEG and resuscitation facilities, as apnoea may follow) is both the diagnostic test and the treatment.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Causes by AGE OF ONSET: DAYS 1–4 — HIE (commonest), hypoglycaemia, hypocalcaemia, intracranial haemorrhage, drug withdrawal, pyridoxine dependency, galactosaemia. DAYS 4–14 — bacterial MENINGITIS, herpes ENCEPHALITIS, hypocalcaemia (cow's-milk/high-phosphate feeds), inborn errors (urea cycle, aminoacidurias), kernicterus. WEEKS 2–8 — infection, child abuse/subdural haematoma, malformations of cortical development, tuberous sclerosis, Sturge–Weber.**
- **Neonatal seizures are often SUBTLE — lip-smacking, cycling or boxing movements, eye deviation, apnoea — rather than generalised tonic–clonic, because the immature cortex cannot propagate a generalised discharge. AMPLITUDE-INTEGRATED EEG is the diagnostic standard.**
- **JITTERINESS mimics seizures but is STIMULUS-SENSITIVE, STOPS ON GENTLE RESTRAINT, has no autonomic or eye changes, and is a tremor rather than a clonic jerk.**
- **Immediate work-up: BEDSIDE GLUCOSE FIRST, then calcium, magnesium, sodium, sepsis screen and LUMBAR PUNCTURE, followed by cranial ultrasound/MRI. Correct the metabolic cause before reaching for anticonvulsants — PHENOBARBITONE remains first-line, with levetiracetam and phenytoin as alternatives.**
- **THERAPEUTIC HYPOTHERMIA (33.5 °C for 72 hours, started within 6 hours) improves outcome in moderate-to-severe HIE in babies ≥35 weeks.**
- **In an infant of a diabetic mother whose glucose is normal, the next cause to check is CALCIUM (and magnesium) — hypocalcaemia occurs in the first 24–72 hours from delayed parathyroid responsiveness.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Vitamin deficiency causing neonatal seizures" → **pyridoxine**; the causes list → **all four electrolyte abnormalities**.

**REMEMBER:** *Check the sugar first, the calcium next — and if nothing works, give pyridoxine.*

`Q9064, Q9080, Q9058`

---

## 9. The High-Risk Infant

**PEARL:** A **HIGH-RISK NEONATE (AIIMS criteria) is one with a BIRTH WEIGHT <1500 g, GESTATION <32 WEEKS, or — at ≥1500 g/≥32 weeks — any of: intrauterine growth <3rd centile, MENINGITIS, MECHANICAL VENTILATION ≥48 hours, HIE stage 2 or more, major malformation, inborn error/chromosomal disorder, intrauterine infection, symptomatic hypoglycaemia or polycythaemia, an HIV-positive mother, hyperbilirubinaemia needing EXCHANGE TRANSFUSION, abnormal neurological examination or seizures, and major morbidities (chronic lung disease, IVH grade III+, periventricular leukomalacia).**

**UNDERSTAND:** The list is not arbitrary — every item marks a baby at **high risk of NEURODEVELOPMENTAL IMPAIRMENT**, and the purpose of the register is to enrol them in **structured FOLLOW-UP: growth monitoring, developmental assessment, vision and HEARING screening, and early intervention.** The rationale is that **neuroplasticity is greatest in the first two years**, so identifying deficits early converts an inevitable disability into a treatable one.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Low birth weight <2500 g; VERY low <1500 g; EXTREMELY low <1000 g. PRETERM <37 weeks; very preterm <32; extremely preterm <28. Small-for-gestational-age is below the 10th centile for gestation — SGA is a weight statement, prematurity a maturity statement, and the two must not be confused.**
- **Follow-up schedule: at discharge, then at 1, 2, 3, 6, 9, 12 and 18 months and 2 years corrected age — with CORRECTED AGE used for developmental assessment until 2 years in preterms.**
- **Screening obligations in preterm infants: RETINOPATHY OF PREMATURITY screening (all <34 weeks or <2000 g, or with oxygen exposure — first screen by 4 weeks of age or 31 weeks post-menstrual age), HEARING screening (OAE/BERA), cranial ultrasound for IVH, and anaemia and osteopenia of prematurity.**
- **Preterm nutritional needs: human milk with FORTIFIER, iron from 2–4 weeks, vitamin D and calcium/phosphate supplementation.**
- **KANGAROO MOTHER CARE — continuous skin-to-skin contact with exclusive breastfeeding and early discharge — reduces mortality, hypothermia, infection and improves growth and bonding: the highest-value low-cost intervention in newborn care.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Infant at risk" → **premature birth at <32 weeks**.

**REMEMBER:** *Under 1500 grams or under 32 weeks — or anything that injures the brain — earns lifelong follow-up.*

`Q9061`

---

## 10. Vaccination in the Immunosuppressed Child

**PEARL:** In a child on **steroids for nephrotic syndrome, ALL KILLED/INACTIVATED VACCINES may be given normally.** **LIVE vaccines are permitted only if the prednisolone dose is BELOW 2 mg/kg/day (or <20 mg/day) and for LESS THAN 14 DAYS — and are CONTRAINDICATED on high-dose steroids or on steroid-sparing agents (cyclophosphamide, ciclosporin, tacrolimus, rituximab).** ***HOUSEHOLD CONTACTS SHOULD BE FULLY VACCINATED, INCLUDING WITH LIVE VACCINES*** — this protects the immunosuppressed child ("cocooning").

**UNDERSTAND:** Killed vaccines cannot replicate, so they are **safe but may be less immunogenic** — which is why they are given and, where possible, timed for periods of lower immunosuppression. Live vaccines can **replicate unchecked and cause vaccine-strain disease.** The apparent paradox about siblings resolves once you realise that **the risk of the child catching WILD measles or varicella from an unvaccinated sibling far exceeds the tiny risk of vaccine-strain transmission** — the only practical precaution being to avoid contact with the stool of an OPV recipient and with a vesicular rash after varicella vaccine.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Live vaccines should be given at least 4 WEEKS BEFORE starting immunosuppression, and not until 3 MONTHS AFTER stopping it (6 months after rituximab or chemotherapy).**
- **Extra vaccines specifically indicated in nephrotic syndrome and asplenia: PNEUMOCOCCAL (PCV13/15 followed by PPSV23), annual INFLUENZA, MENINGOCOCCAL and Hib — because these children are at high risk of encapsulated-organism infection (SPONTANEOUS BACTERIAL PERITONITIS from *S. pneumoniae* in nephrotic syndrome, from urinary loss of immunoglobulin and complement factor B).**
- **VARICELLA vaccine should be given during a period of remission on low-dose alternate-day steroids, and VZIG (or acyclovir) given after significant exposure in a non-immune child on immunosuppression.**
- **In HIV, live vaccines depend on immune status: BCG is CONTRAINDICATED in symptomatic HIV; MMR and varicella may be given if the CD4 percentage is adequate; OPV is replaced by IPV.**
- **The general rule: ***THE MORE IMMUNOSUPPRESSED THE CHILD, THE MORE IMPORTANT THE KILLED VACCINES AND THE MORE DANGEROUS THE LIVE ONES*** — and the more important it is to vaccinate everyone around them.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Steroid-dependent nephrotic syndrome → **all killed vaccines under the national schedule can be given**.

**REMEMBER:** *Killed vaccines for the patient, live vaccines for the family.*

`Q9063`

---

## 11. Cutaneous Markers of Spinal Dysraphism

**PEARL:** A **TUFT OF HAIR over the LUMBOSACRAL region in a newborn indicates OCCULT SPINAL DYSRAPHISM — which may be SPINA BIFIDA OCCULTA, an INTRASPINAL LIPOMA, a DORSAL DERMAL SINUS, diastematomyelia or a tethered cord.** Other markers: a **dimple ABOVE the gluteal crease, a subcutaneous LIPOMA, a haemangioma, a skin tag or "tail", and an ATYPICAL/deviated gluteal cleft.** **ULTRASOUND is the initial investigation under 6 months (before the posterior elements ossify); MRI thereafter.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The skin and the neural tube share an **ECTODERMAL origin and close together at the same time**, so a failure of neural tube closure leaves a **visible footprint in the overlying skin.** That is the entire diagnostic value of these markers — they are the only clue in a child whose neurological examination is normal, which it usually is in infancy. **The neurological deficit appears LATER, as the tethered cord is stretched by growth.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **The TETHERED CORD SYNDROME presents in later childhood with progressive gait disturbance, leg length or muscle bulk asymmetry, HIGH-ARCHED FEET or claw toes, back and leg pain, sensory loss in the perineum, and NEW-ONSET BLADDER OR BOWEL DYSFUNCTION — the last is often the earliest and most easily missed. Untethering surgery prevents progression but does not reverse established damage.**
- **A DERMAL SINUS is the most dangerous of these, because it is a direct conduit for infection — a cause of RECURRENT MENINGITIS (especially with unusual organisms) and intraspinal abscess. It must NEVER be probed or injected.**
- **A SIMPLE SACRAL DIMPLE that is small (<5 mm), within 2.5 cm of the anus, in the midline and with a visible base is a NORMAL variant needing no imaging; dimples that are large, high, off-midline or accompanied by other markers require imaging.**
- **Open dysraphism (MYELOMENINGOCELE) is obvious at birth and needs closure within 24–72 hours, with lifelong management of hydrocephalus (85% need a shunt), the CHIARI II malformation, neurogenic bladder and orthopaedic deformity — plus LATEX PRECAUTIONS, since latex allergy is common.**
- **Prevention: PERICONCEPTIONAL FOLIC ACID, 400 µg routinely and 4–5 mg where the risk is high.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Tuft of hair over the lumbosacral spine → **any of spina bifida occulta, spinal lipoma or dermal sinus** — i.e. occult spinal dysraphism.

**REMEMBER:** *Skin and neural tube close together — so a mark on the back means look at the cord.*

`Q9068`

---

## 12. Hydrocephalus in the Newborn

**PEARL:** **AQUEDUCTAL STENOSIS is the commonest cause of congenital hydrocephalus/ventriculomegaly.** VENTRICULOMEGALY is dilatation of the ventricles; when accompanied by RAISED CSF PRESSURE it is HYDROCEPHALUS. Other congenital causes: **DANDY–WALKER malformation, CHIARI malformations, neural tube defects and agenesis of the corpus callosum**; acquired causes include **intraventricular haemorrhage, meningitis, congenital infection (CMV, toxoplasmosis) and tumours.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The cerebral aqueduct is the **narrowest point of the entire CSF pathway (about 1–2 mm)**, so it is the site where even minor narrowing obstructs flow — producing **NON-COMMUNICATING (obstructive) hydrocephalus with dilated LATERAL AND THIRD ventricles but a NORMAL FOURTH ventricle**, a pattern that identifies the level of obstruction on imaging without any further test.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **In an infant with open sutures, hydrocephalus presents with EXCESSIVE HEAD GROWTH crossing centiles, a TENSE BULGING FONTANELLE, widely separated sutures, "CRACKED-POT" (Macewen's) sign, prominent scalp veins, and the "SETTING-SUN" SIGN (impaired upgaze from tectal compression). After the sutures fuse, the picture becomes classical raised intracranial pressure — headache, vomiting, papilloedema and altered consciousness.**
- **SERIAL HEAD CIRCUMFERENCE MEASUREMENT plotted on a chart is the single most useful bedside tool; cranial ULTRASOUND through the anterior fontanelle is the first-line investigation in infants.**
- **Treatment: VENTRICULOPERITONEAL SHUNT, or ENDOSCOPIC THIRD VENTRICULOSTOMY (which is ideal for aqueductal stenosis, as it bypasses the obstruction and avoids hardware). SHUNT COMPLICATIONS — blockage, infection, over-drainage with subdural collections, disconnection — are the commonest reason such children re-present, and any shunted child with headache, vomiting or drowsiness must be assumed to have shunt malfunction.**
- **DANDY–WALKER malformation: cystic dilatation of the fourth ventricle with cerebellar vermian hypoplasia and an enlarged posterior fossa. CHIARI II: downward displacement of the cerebellar vermis and medulla, almost always with myelomeningocele.**
- **Normal head circumference at birth is ~35 cm, growing ~2 cm/month in the first 3 months, then 1 cm/month to 6 months, then 0.5 cm/month to 1 year (~47 cm at 1 year); 85% of adult head size is reached by 2 years.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Commonest cause of neonatal ventriculomegaly → **aqueductal stenosis**.

**REMEMBER:** *The aqueduct is the narrowest channel in the system — so it blocks first, and the fourth ventricle stays small.*

`Q9069`

---

## 13. Cerebral Palsy

**PEARL:** **CEREBRAL PALSY is a NON-PROGRESSIVE disorder of movement and posture from an insult to the DEVELOPING brain — it is an UPPER MOTOR NEURONE disorder, so FLACCID PARALYSIS IS NOT A FEATURE.** Hypotonia (in the dyskinetic/athetoid form and in early "hypotonic" CP), MICROCEPHALY and ATAXIA all occur. **SPASTIC CP is the commonest type overall; SPASTIC DIPLEGIA is characteristically associated with PREMATURITY and PERIVENTRICULAR LEUKOMALACIA.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Spastic diplegia affects the **legs more than the arms** because of the **somatotopic arrangement of the corticospinal tract in the periventricular white matter — the descending fibres to the LEGS lie CLOSEST to the lateral ventricles**, and are therefore damaged first by periventricular ischaemia in the preterm brain. The same anatomical logic explains every clinical pattern in CP: the site of injury dictates the distribution of the deficit.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Types: SPASTIC (~70–80%) — DIPLEGIA (prematurity, PVL, scissoring gait, commando crawl, good intellect), HEMIPLEGIA (vascular insult/infarct, EARLY HAND PREFERENCE before 1 year, arm > leg, seizures in ~33%), QUADRIPLEGIA (the most severe — term asphyxia, with seizures, intellectual disability, microcephaly, feeding difficulty and hip dislocation). DYSKINETIC/athetoid — basal ganglia injury from KERNICTERUS or severe asphyxia, with hypotonia early and involuntary movements later, and INTELLECT OFTEN PRESERVED. ATAXIC — cerebellar. MIXED.**
- **The insult is STATIC but the CLINICAL PICTURE EVOLVES with growth — spasticity produces progressive contractures, hip subluxation and scoliosis, which is why the child needs lifelong orthopaedic and physiotherapy input even though the brain lesion is fixed.**
- **Associated problems must be sought actively: EPILEPSY (~35%), intellectual disability, visual (squint, refractive error, cortical visual impairment) and HEARING deficits, speech and swallowing difficulty with ASPIRATION and malnutrition, GERD, drooling, constipation and behavioural problems.**
- **Management is MULTIDISCIPLINARY: physiotherapy and occupational therapy, orthoses, BOTULINUM TOXIN for focal spasticity, oral baclofen/tizanidine or INTRATHECAL BACLOFEN, SELECTIVE DORSAL RHIZOTOMY, orthopaedic surgery for contractures and hips, nutrition (often gastrostomy), seizure control and communication aids. GMFCS grades functional severity I–V.**
- **A PROGRESSIVE course, loss of previously acquired skills, or a purely lower-motor-neurone picture EXCLUDES cerebral palsy — investigate for a neurodegenerative or metabolic disorder.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Not a feature of cerebral palsy" → **flaccid paralysis**.

**REMEMBER:** *A fixed injury to a growing brain — spastic, never flaccid, and never progressive.*

`Q9071`

---

## 14. Necrotising Enterocolitis

**PEARL:** **NECROTISING ENTEROCOLITIS is the commonest life-threatening gastrointestinal emergency of the newborn**, typically in the **2nd–3rd week of life in a PRETERM, FORMULA-FED infant**, presenting with **abdominal distension and tenderness, FEED INTOLERANCE with bilious aspirates, BLOODY STOOLS, ERYTHEMA OF THE ABDOMINAL WALL and an abdominal mass, plus lethargy, apnoea, temperature instability and shock.** It causes **METABOLIC (and respiratory) ACIDOSIS — NOT alkalosis.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The pathogenesis is a triad: **PREMATURITY (an immature gut barrier and immune system — the single greatest risk factor), ENTERAL FEEDING with formula (substrate for bacterial proliferation), and BACTERIAL COLONISATION**, acting on a mucosa made vulnerable by ischaemia. The acidosis reflects **sepsis, lactic acid from a hypoperfused gut, loss of bicarbonate from the damaged intestine, and impaired respiratory compensation from apnoea** — which is why a falling pH is one of the earliest warnings of deterioration.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **The DISTAL ILEUM and PROXIMAL COLON are most often affected. Radiological signs: dilated bowel loops, a fixed "persistent loop", ***PNEUMATOSIS INTESTINALIS (intramural gas — pathognomonic)***, PORTAL VENOUS GAS (a grave sign), and PNEUMOPERITONEUM indicating PERFORATION.**
- **BELL'S STAGING: I suspected; II definite (with pneumatosis); III advanced with shock or perforation.**
- **Management: STOP ENTERAL FEEDS, nasogastric decompression, IV fluids and total parenteral nutrition, BROAD-SPECTRUM ANTIBIOTICS, and supportive intensive care. ***SURGERY (laparotomy or peritoneal drainage) IS INDICATED FOR PERFORATION*** or for clinical deterioration despite maximal medical therapy.**
- **Prevention: ANTENATAL CORTICOSTEROIDS, ***EXCLUSIVE HUMAN (breast) MILK*** — the strongest protective factor — cautious standardised feeding advancement, and PROBIOTICS in selected units. Avoid prolonged empirical antibiotics and H2 blockers, which increase risk.**
- **Long-term sequelae: intestinal STRICTURE (the commonest, presenting weeks later with obstruction), SHORT BOWEL SYNDROME after resection, cholestasis from TPN, and adverse neurodevelopmental outcome.**
- **Incidence and mortality rise steeply with FALLING birth weight and gestational age.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "All are features of NEC EXCEPT" → **metabolic alkalosis**.

**REMEMBER:** *Preterm, fed on formula, second week — distension, blood in the stool and gas in the bowel wall.*

`Q9074`

---

## 15. Mother-to-Child Transmission of HIV

**PEARL:** **The commonest route of HIV infection in a newborn is PERINATAL (INTRAPARTUM) transmission — 70–80% of vertically infected children acquire the virus DURING LABOUR AND DELIVERY**, through mucosal exposure to infected blood and cervicovaginal secretions and micro-transfusions during contractions. **In utero transmission accounts for most of the rest, and BREASTFEEDING is the least common route** (though it adds a continuing risk).

**UNDERSTAND:** The intrapartum predominance explains the entire prevention strategy: **maternal ART to suppress viral load (the single most effective measure), avoidance of prolonged rupture of membranes and invasive fetal monitoring, and — where the viral load is high — CAESAREAN SECTION before labour.** With full intervention, transmission falls from **15–45% to under 2%.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Risk factors for vertical transmission: HIGH MATERNAL VIRAL LOAD (the strongest), low CD4 count, seroconversion during pregnancy or lactation, PRETERM delivery (<34 weeks), PROLONGED RUPTURE OF MEMBRANES (>4 hours), chorioamnionitis, low birth weight, vitamin A deficiency, and mixed feeding.**
- **India's PPTCT programme: universal antenatal HIV testing, LIFELONG ART for every pregnant woman regardless of CD4 count (Option B+), institutional delivery, INFANT NEVIRAPINE prophylaxis (6 weeks, extended to 12 weeks in high-risk infants), and COTRIMOXAZOLE prophylaxis from 6 weeks until infection is excluded.**
- **INFANT DIAGNOSIS REQUIRES VIROLOGICAL TESTING (DNA/RNA PCR) at 6 weeks, 6 months and 12 months — ***ANTIBODY TESTS ARE UNRELIABLE UNTIL 18 MONTHS*** because maternal IgG crosses the placenta and persists.**
- **FEEDING: in India, ***EXCLUSIVE BREASTFEEDING FOR 6 MONTHS with maternal ART*** is recommended, because the risks of formula feeding (diarrhoea, malnutrition) outweigh the residual transmission risk. ***MIXED FEEDING CARRIES THE HIGHEST TRANSMISSION RISK OF ALL*** — it damages the gut mucosa while exposing it to virus.**
- **AVOID invasive procedures where possible — amniocentesis, fetal scalp electrodes, artificial rupture of membranes and instrumental delivery all increase exposure.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Most common cause of HIV in a newborn" → **perinatal (intrapartum) transmission**.

**REMEMBER:** *Most babies are infected during the passage, not the pregnancy — which is why viral suppression and delivery care matter most.*

`Q9075`

---

## 16. Chemoprophylaxis of Contacts

**PEARL:** For **MENINGOCOCCAL disease, close contacts receive ANTIBIOTIC PROPHYLAXIS (RIFAMPICIN, CIPROFLOXACIN, CEFTRIAXONE or azithromycin) WITHIN 24 HOURS — not vaccination as the primary measure.** For **DIPHTHERIA, ALL household contacts receive ANTIBIOTIC PROPHYLAXIS (erythromycin or benzathine penicillin) REGARDLESS OF IMMUNISATION STATUS, plus throat and nose cultures and 7 days of surveillance — and a BOOSTER of diphtheria toxoid ONLY IF the last dose was MORE THAN 5 YEARS AGO.** So a fully vaccinated 3-year-old boostered 16 months earlier needs **ERYTHROMYCIN ONLY.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The logic is that **prophylaxis must act FASTER than the incubation period.** Antibiotics eradicate nasopharyngeal carriage immediately, whereas a vaccine takes **1–2 weeks to generate antibody — far too slow to prevent a case incubating now.** Vaccination is therefore an ADJUNCT for longer-term protection in outbreak settings, never the emergency measure.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Who counts as a "close contact" for meningococcus: household members, childcare/school contacts sharing a room, anyone directly exposed to the patient's oral secretions (kissing, sharing utensils), and healthcare workers who performed mouth-to-mouth resuscitation, intubation or suction WITHOUT protection. ***ROUTINE PROPHYLAXIS IS NOT GIVEN TO OTHER HEALTHCARE STAFF OR CASUAL SCHOOL CONTACTS.***
- **Treatment of the case itself: PENICILLIN or a third-generation cephalosporin; note that penicillin does NOT eradicate carriage, so a patient treated with penicillin also needs clearance therapy before discharge (ceftriaxone does eradicate it).**
- **Other standard contact prophylaxis: *H. INFLUENZAE* type b — RIFAMPICIN for household contacts if there is an unimmunised/incompletely immunised child under 4. TUBERCULOSIS — ISONIAZID (or 3HP) for contacts under 5 and for HIV-positive contacts after excluding active disease. PERTUSSIS — azithromycin for household contacts. LEPROSY — SINGLE-DOSE RIFAMPICIN. MENINGOCOCCAL vaccine is added for serogroup-preventable outbreaks and for asplenia/complement deficiency.**
- **In diphtheria, unimmunised contacts or those with fewer than 3 doses should receive an immediate dose and complete the primary series; ***ANTITOXIN IS NOT GIVEN TO CONTACTS***, only to cases.**
- **Chemoprophylaxis works only if given EARLY — beyond about 14 days after exposure its value in meningococcal disease is negligible.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** School contacts of meningococcal meningitis → **antibiotic prophylaxis**; vaccinated diphtheria contact boostered 16 months ago → **erythromycin only**.

**REMEMBER:** *Antibiotics work today, vaccines work in a fortnight — the incubating case cannot wait.*

`Q9076, Q9077`

---

## 17. Growth, Nutrition and the Growth Chart

**PEARL:** A child reaches **HALF OF ADULT HEIGHT AT 2 YEARS (20–24 months)**; birth length ~50 cm → **75 cm at 1 year → 85–90 cm at 2 years**, then ~6 cm/year until puberty. **Height DOUBLES by 4 years and TRIPLES by 12.** On the WHO **WEIGHT-FOR-AGE chart, between −2 SD and −3 SD is MODERATE malnutrition — the mother is TAUGHT HOW TO FEED (home management); BELOW −3 SD is SEVERE malnutrition requiring referral.** **Energy from SATURATED FAT should be <10% of total intake.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The growth chart's power is that it tracks **VELOCITY, not a single point** — a child moving along a low centile is usually normal, while one **CROSSING centiles downwards is in trouble even if still "within normal limits".** That is the whole rationale of plotting serial weights in the mother-and-child protection card, and why **weight gain, not attained weight, is the best measure of a good response to nutritional treatment.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Weight landmarks: birth ~3 kg, with 10% physiological loss regained by 10–14 days; DOUBLES by 5 months, TRIPLES by 1 year, QUADRUPLES by 2 years; thereafter ~2 kg/year until puberty. Head circumference ~35 cm at birth, 47 cm at 1 year, 49 cm at 2 years.**
- **SEVERE ACUTE MALNUTRITION (WHO): weight-for-height below −3 SD, MUAC <11.5 cm, or bilateral pitting OEDEMA. Management has 10 STEPS in two phases — treat/prevent HYPOGLYCAEMIA, HYPOTHERMIA, DEHYDRATION (with ***ReSoMal***, which has LESS SODIUM and MORE POTASSIUM than standard ORS), correct ELECTROLYTES, treat INFECTION, correct MICRONUTRIENTS (***WITHHOLDING IRON UNTIL THE REHABILITATION PHASE***), cautious feeding (F-75), catch-up growth (F-100), sensory stimulation, and follow-up.**
- ***REFEEDING SYNDROME*** — hypophosphataemia, hypokalaemia and hypomagnesaemia with cardiac failure — is why feeding starts LOW and slow.
- **Macronutrient distribution (AMDR): ages 1–3 years — fat 30–40%, protein 5–20%, carbohydrate 45–65%; ages 4–18 — fat 25–35%, protein 10–30%, carbohydrate 45–65%. Dietary cholesterol <300 mg/day and NO trans fat.**
- **The Indian growth reference is the WHO standard up to 5 years and the IAP charts thereafter; STUNTING (height-for-age) reflects CHRONIC undernutrition, WASTING (weight-for-height) ACUTE undernutrition, and UNDERWEIGHT (weight-for-age) a composite of both.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Half of adult height → **20–24 months**; weight between −2 and −3 SD → **moderate malnutrition, counsel the mother on feeding**.

**REMEMBER:** *Half your height by two, triple your birth weight by one — and it is the crossing of centiles that matters.*

`Q9079, Q9082, Q9078`

---

## 18. Febrile Seizures and When to Suspect Meningitis

**PEARL:** **FEBRILE SEIZURES occur between 6 and 60 MONTHS (peak 12–18 months) with a temperature ≥38 °C, WITHOUT intracranial infection or a prior unprovoked seizure.** **MAJOR risk factors for RECURRENCE: AGE UNDER 1 YEAR, a LOW peak temperature (38–39 °C), a SHORT duration of fever before the seizure (<24 hours), and a family history of febrile seizures.** But an **infant with fever, seizures, POOR FEEDING, LETHARGY and a BULGING FONTANELLE has MENINGITIS until proved otherwise — febrile seizure is a diagnosis of exclusion.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The recurrence risk factors all describe a **LOW SEIZURE THRESHOLD**: a child who fits at a modest temperature, early in the illness, and at a young age is one whose brain convulses easily — so any future fever is likely to do it again. The corollary is reassuring and is what parents need to hear: **recurrence does NOT mean epilepsy, and simple febrile seizures do NOT cause brain damage.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **SIMPLE febrile seizure — GENERALISED, lasting <15 minutes, NOT recurring within 24 hours, in a neurologically normal child; risk of later epilepsy ~1–2% (near the background rate). COMPLEX — FOCAL, PROLONGED (>15 minutes), or RECURRING within 24 hours; epilepsy risk rises to ~6% and higher with multiple complex features.**
- **LUMBAR PUNCTURE should be performed if there are MENINGEAL SIGNS or the child looks toxic, and CONSIDERED in infants UNDER 12 MONTHS (especially under 6 months), in those PRETREATED WITH ANTIBIOTICS (which mask meningitis), and after a complex seizure or slow recovery. ***EEG AND NEUROIMAGING ARE NOT INDICATED after a simple febrile seizure.***
- **Management: reassurance and parental education — first aid (left lateral position, nothing in the mouth, time the seizure), and RESCUE benzodiazepine (buccal midazolam or rectal diazepam) if the seizure exceeds 5 minutes. ***ANTIPYRETICS DO NOT PREVENT RECURRENCE*** (they are given for comfort), and continuous prophylactic anticonvulsants are NOT recommended.**
- **Bacterial meningitis beyond the neonatal period: *S. PNEUMONIAE*, *N. MENINGITIDIS* and *H. INFLUENZAE* type b — treat with a third-generation cephalosporin plus vancomycin, with DEXAMETHASONE BEFORE OR WITH the first antibiotic dose (reducing deafness, especially in Hib). Suspect it in an infant from NON-SPECIFIC signs — poor feeding, lethargy, irritability, vomiting, bulging fontanelle — as neck stiffness is often ABSENT under 18 months.**
- **The overall recurrence rate of febrile seizures is ~30%, rising to 50% if the first occurred under 1 year and higher still with multiple risk factors.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Recurrence risk factors → **age <1 year, temperature 38–39 °C, fever <24 hours**; bulging fontanelle with fever and fits → **meningitis**.

**REMEMBER:** *Young, mildly febrile, and fitting early — that brain will fit again. But feel the fontanelle before you call it febrile.*

`Q9083, Q9059`

---

## 19. Thalassaemia and the Mentzer Index

**PEARL:** A **9-month-old with progressive PALLOR, HEPATOSPLENOMEGALY and a MENTZER INDEX (MCV ÷ RBC count) <13 has THALASSAEMIA.** The investigations needed are **PERIPHERAL SMEAR and HPLC (showing reduced HbA with raised HbA2 and HbF)** — **bone marrow examination and coagulation studies have NO role.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The Mentzer index works because of what the marrow is doing in each disease. In **THALASSAEMIA the marrow produces MANY small red cells** (the RBC count is normal or high while the MCV is very low), so **MCV/RBC is LOW (<13)**. In **IRON DEFICIENCY the marrow cannot make enough cells at all** (both MCV and RBC count are low), so the ratio is **>13**. One arithmetic ratio therefore separates the two commonest causes of microcytosis in Indian children.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Peripheral smear in thalassaemia major: severe HYPOCHROMIC MICROCYTIC anaemia with TARGET CELLS, basophilic stippling, fragmented cells, anisopoikilocytosis and NUCLEATED RED CELLS — with a normal or raised RBC count and a low RDW (versus the HIGH RDW of iron deficiency).**
- **β-thalassaemia major presents at 3–6 MONTHS as HbF declines, with failure to thrive, massive hepatosplenomegaly from extramedullary haematopoiesis, and — if untransfused — the classic "CHIPMUNK/thalassaemic FACIES" with frontal bossing, maxillary hypertrophy and a "HAIR-ON-END" skull X-ray from marrow expansion.**
- **Management: regular TRANSFUSION to keep pre-transfusion Hb ≥9–10 g/dL (which suppresses ineffective erythropoiesis and prevents the bony changes), with IRON CHELATION (deferasirox, deferiprone, desferrioxamine) started after ~10–20 transfusions or a ferritin >1000 — because IRON OVERLOAD causes the cardiac failure, cirrhosis, diabetes and hypogonadism that kill these patients. ***CARDIAC T2* MRI*** monitors myocardial iron. HAEMATOPOIETIC STEM CELL TRANSPLANTATION is the only cure. Give folic acid; AVOID IRON SUPPLEMENTS.
- **Prevention is the real answer: CARRIER SCREENING (HbA2 >3.5%), genetic counselling and PRENATAL DIAGNOSIS by chorionic villus sampling — the basis of national thalassaemia control programmes.**
- **Distinguish the causes of microcytic anaemia — "TAILS": Thalassaemia, Anaemia of chronic disease, Iron deficiency, Lead poisoning, Sideroblastic anaemia.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Mentzer index <13 with pallor and organomegaly → **HPLC and peripheral smear**.

**REMEMBER:** *Many tiny cells means thalassaemia; too few cells means iron deficiency — thirteen is the dividing line.*

`Q9084`

---

## 20. Scarlet Fever

**PEARL:** **Fever, SORE THROAT and a diffuse erythematous SANDPAPER RASH with a STRAWBERRY TONGUE = SCARLET FEVER, caused by the PYROGENIC (ERYTHROGENIC) EXOTOXIN of GROUP A STREPTOCOCCUS.** It usually follows pharyngitis but can follow **inoculation through a wound or burn (as after a thorn prick).** Treat with **PENICILLIN or AMOXICILLIN for 10 DAYS** (amoxicillin–clavulanate empirically when a wound is involved).

**UNDERSTAND:** The rash is a **DELAYED-TYPE HYPERSENSITIVITY response to the exotoxin in a previously sensitised child** — which is why it appears 24–48 hours after the fever, requires prior exposure, and does not recur with every streptococcal infection (there are three toxin types). The toxin is **phage-encoded**, so only lysogenised strains cause it.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **The rash is a fine, blanching, punctate erythema that feels like SANDPAPER, begins on the neck and trunk and spares the palms and soles, with CIRCUMORAL PALLOR, accentuation in the skin folds (PASTIA'S LINES), and DESQUAMATION of the hands and feet during convalescence. The tongue is first white-coated with red papillae ("white strawberry") and then bright red ("red strawberry").**
- ***TREATING PHARYNGITIS PREVENTS RHEUMATIC FEVER BUT NOT POST-STREPTOCOCCAL GLOMERULONEPHRITIS*** — and only PHARYNGEAL infection causes rheumatic fever, while both skin and throat infections cause nephritis.
- **Confirm with a RAPID ANTIGEN DETECTION TEST or THROAT CULTURE before treating where possible; ASO titre rises after throat infection and ANTI-DNase B after skin infection.**
- **Group A *Streptococcus* also causes IMPETIGO, ERYSIPELAS, cellulitis, NECROTISING FASCIITIS and STREPTOCOCCAL TOXIC SHOCK SYNDROME (a superantigen-mediated illness treated with penicillin PLUS CLINDAMYCIN to switch off toxin production, and IVIG).**
- **Differential of a childhood rash with fever: KAWASAKI DISEASE (fever ≥5 days, conjunctivitis, mucositis, rash, extremity changes and cervical node — treat with IVIG and aspirin to prevent CORONARY ANEURYSMS), measles, rubella, drug eruption, staphylococcal scalded skin syndrome and toxic shock.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Fever, sore throat and rash after a thorn prick → **amoxicillin–clavulanate (Group A streptococcal infection)**.

**REMEMBER:** *Sandpaper rash, strawberry tongue, peeling hands — the toxin of a strep the child has met before.*

`Q9085`

---

## 21. Status Epilepticus in Children

**PEARL:** The order is: **(1) AIRWAY AND BREATHING (with oxygen, glucose check and IV access) → (2) IV LORAZEPAM (a benzodiazepine, repeated once) → (3) IV FOSPHENYTOIN (or levetiracetam/valproate) → (4) ANAESTHETIC INFUSION — MIDAZOLAM, PROPOFOL or THIOPENTONE — with intubation.** Status epilepticus for generalised tonic–clonic seizures is defined at **t1 = 5 MINUTES (start treatment) and t2 = 30 MINUTES (risk of neuronal injury).**

**UNDERSTAND:** The escalation follows **receptor pharmacology over time.** Early in a seizure, **GABA-A receptors are internalised progressively — so benzodiazepines become LESS effective the longer the seizure runs**, while **NMDA receptors are recruited to the membrane**, making the seizure self-sustaining. That is precisely why **early benzodiazepine is the highest-yield intervention** and why delay converts a treatable seizure into refractory status.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **REFRACTORY status = failure of a benzodiazepine PLUS one second-line agent; SUPER-REFRACTORY = continuing or recurring ≥24 hours despite anaesthetic infusion.**
- **The immediate parallel actions ("the first five minutes"): check CAPILLARY GLUCOSE, correct hypoglycaemia, take blood for electrolytes, calcium, magnesium, cultures and anticonvulsant levels, and consider PYRIDOXINE in an infant, antibiotics/acyclovir if infection is possible, and imaging once stable.**
- **Out-of-hospital or without IV access: BUCCAL MIDAZOLAM, intranasal midazolam or RECTAL DIAZEPAM.**
- **Doses to know: lorazepam 0.1 mg/kg IV (max 4 mg), midazolam 0.2 mg/kg buccal, fosphenytoin 20 mg PE/kg, levetiracetam 40–60 mg/kg, valproate 20–40 mg/kg (AVOID valproate if a metabolic/mitochondrial disorder is suspected, and in hepatic disease).**
- **Complications of prolonged status: hypoxia, aspiration, rhabdomyolysis, hyperthermia, cerebral oedema and permanent neuronal loss — mortality and morbidity rise sharply after 30 minutes.**
- **Paediatric BASIC LIFE SUPPORT sequence: ensure safety → CHECK RESPONSIVENESS → check BREATHING AND PULSE (≤10 seconds) → 5 RESCUE BREATHS/bag-and-mask ventilation → CHEST COMPRESSIONS if pulseless or heart rate <60 with poor perfusion (15:2 with two rescuers) — reflecting the respiratory aetiology of paediatric arrest.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Sequence → **airway/breathing → lorazepam → fosphenytoin → anaesthetic agents**.

**REMEMBER:** *GABA receptors disappear as the seizure runs — so the benzodiazepine must be given early or not at all.*

`Q9086, Q9087`

---

## 22. Estimating GFR in Children

**PEARL:** The **MODIFIED SCHWARTZ FORMULA: eGFR (mL/min/1.73 m²) = 0.413 × HEIGHT (cm) ÷ SERUM CREATININE (mg/dL).** A 4-year-old of 100 cm with a creatinine of 1 mg/dL therefore has an eGFR of **41 ≈ 40 mL/min/1.73 m²** — a serum creatinine that would be entirely normal in an adult represents **severe renal impairment in a small child.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Creatinine is generated by **MUSCLE**, and a child's muscle mass is small — so the "normal" creatinine rises with age and size. **HEIGHT is used as the surrogate for muscle mass**, which is why the paediatric formula needs only height and creatinine. This also explains the commonest clinical error: **interpreting a child's creatinine against adult reference ranges and missing significant kidney disease.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Normal GFR is only ~20–30 mL/min/1.73 m² AT BIRTH, doubles by 2 weeks and reaches adult values (~120) by 1–2 YEARS — so neonatal drug dosing must account for immature clearance.**
- **In adults, use the CKD-EPI (preferred) or MDRD equations; CREATININE CLEARANCE by 24-hour urine and inulin/iothalamate clearance are the reference methods but impractical. CYSTATIN C-based equations avoid the muscle-mass problem entirely and are useful in malnutrition and neuromuscular disease.**
- **CKD staging by eGFR: G1 ≥90, G2 60–89, G3a 45–59, G3b 30–44, G4 15–29, G5 <15 (kidney failure) — with albuminuria staged separately (A1–A3).**
- **Creatinine is an INSENSITIVE early marker — it does not rise until GFR has fallen by about half ("the creatinine blind range"), which is why eGFR and albuminuria are used instead.**
- **In children, always look for a CONGENITAL ANOMALY OF THE KIDNEY AND URINARY TRACT (CAKUT) — the commonest cause of CKD in childhood — and for reflux nephropathy and glomerulonephritis.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Pure calculation — **0.413 × height ÷ creatinine**.

**REMEMBER:** *Height stands in for muscle mass — so a "normal" adult creatinine can mean half a child's kidney function is gone.*

`Q9088`

---

## 23. Congenital Cytomegalovirus

**PEARL:** **CMV is the commonest congenital infection. Only ~10% of infected newborns are SYMPTOMATIC at birth, and those ASYMPTOMATIC at birth have a LOWER risk of late sequelae.** Transmission to the fetus occurs in **~30–40% of PRIMARY maternal infections but only 1–2% of REACTIVATION** — yet because reactivation/reinfection is far commoner, **most congenitally infected babies in developing countries are born to mothers with NON-PRIMARY infection.** **Diagnosis requires URINE (or saliva) PCR/culture WITHIN THE FIRST 3 WEEKS OF LIFE.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The **3-week rule** exists because after that age a positive test cannot distinguish **congenital infection from perinatal acquisition through breast milk or the birth canal** — and the distinction matters entirely, since only congenital infection causes hearing loss and neurological damage. This single timing constraint is the most examined practical point in congenital CMV.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Clinical features: intrauterine growth restriction, MICROCEPHALY, PERIVENTRICULAR intracranial CALCIFICATION, hepatosplenomegaly, jaundice with conjugated hyperbilirubinaemia, thrombocytopenia with a "BLUEBERRY MUFFIN" rash, and CHORIORETINITIS.**
- ***SENSORINEURAL HEARING LOSS IS THE COMMONEST LONG-TERM SEQUELA AND THE COMMONEST NON-GENETIC CAUSE OF DEAFNESS*** — it can be PROGRESSIVE AND OF LATE ONSET, so every congenitally infected child (even the asymptomatic) needs SERIAL AUDIOLOGICAL FOLLOW-UP for years.
- **Treatment: symptomatic congenital CMV (especially with CNS involvement) is treated with 6 MONTHS OF ORAL VALGANCICLOVIR, which improves hearing and neurodevelopmental outcomes; monitor for NEUTROPENIA.**
- **Distinguish the calcification patterns: CMV — PERIVENTRICULAR; TOXOPLASMOSIS — DIFFUSE/scattered throughout the parenchyma, with hydrocephalus and chorioretinitis. RUBELLA — cataract, deafness and PDA. Congenital VARICELLA — cicatricial dermatomal scarring and limb hypoplasia after infection at 13–20 weeks. Congenital SYPHILIS — snuffles, rash on palms and soles, and later Hutchinson's teeth.**
- **There is no vaccine; prevention is hand hygiene around toddlers' saliva and urine, especially for pregnant women working with young children.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** True statements → **asymptomatic neonates have fewer late sequelae**, and **transmission is far higher with primary maternal infection**.

**REMEMBER:** *Test the urine in the first three weeks, and follow the hearing for years.*

`Q9089`

---

## 24. Urinary Tract Infection in Children

**PEARL:** ***E. COLI* causes ~80% of childhood UTI** (not *S. pneumoniae*). **BOWEL AND BLADDER DYSFUNCTION (constipation, infrequent voiding, holding) markedly increases the risk of RECURRENCE, and a MICTURATING CYSTOURETHROGRAM (MCU) is indicated for RECURRENT UTI, atypical UTI or an abnormal ultrasound** — chiefly to detect **VESICOURETERIC REFLUX.** Cotrimoxazole is a perfectly acceptable agent where sensitivities allow.

**UNDERSTAND:** The reason childhood UTI is taken so seriously is not the acute illness but **RENAL SCARRING**: infection ascending through a refluxing ureter into the renal parenchyma during the first years of life produces **permanent scars, and enough scarring causes hypertension and chronic kidney disease decades later.** That is why the work-up hunts for reflux and obstruction rather than merely treating the infection.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Presentation is NON-SPECIFIC in infants — fever without focus, vomiting, poor feeding, failure to thrive, jaundice, irritability — so ***URINE MUST BE TESTED IN EVERY INFANT WITH UNEXPLAINED FEVER.*** In older children, dysuria, frequency, urgency and loin pain appear.
- **Sampling and counts: CLEAN-CATCH midstream ≥10⁵ CFU/mL; CATHETER specimen ≥5×10⁴; ***ANY growth from a SUPRAPUBIC ASPIRATE is significant.*** Bag samples are useful only if negative. Dipstick nitrite is specific but insensitive (infants void frequently, and some organisms do not reduce nitrate).
- **Imaging: ULTRASOUND for all young children/atypical infection; DMSA SCAN detects acute pyelonephritis and, at 4–6 months, permanent SCARS (the gold standard); MCU demonstrates and grades REFLUX and shows POSTERIOR URETHRAL VALVES in boys.**
- **Treatment: oral antibiotics for uncomplicated cystitis; parenteral therapy for infants under 3 months, toxicity, vomiting or complicated UTI (high fever >39 °C, dehydration, renal angle tenderness, raised creatinine). Duration 7–10 days for upper tract infection.**
- **Prevention of recurrence: treat CONSTIPATION, encourage regular complete voiding and fluids, and consider antibiotic PROPHYLAXIS in high-grade reflux — plus circumcision in boys with recurrent UTI and high-grade reflux.**
- **Risk factors for recurrence: age <6 months, female sex, vesicoureteric reflux, obstructive uropathy, bladder–bowel dysfunction and repeated catheterisation.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** True statements → **bladder–bowel dysfunction increases recurrence** and **MCU is done for recurrent UTI**.

**REMEMBER:** *The infection is treatable; the scar is not — so image the child who keeps getting infected.*

`Q9090`

---

## 25. Developmental Milestones — Fine Motor and Handedness

**PEARL:** **HANDEDNESS (established hand preference) appears at about 3 YEARS.** ***HAND PREFERENCE BEFORE 1 YEAR IS ABNORMAL and suggests HEMIPLEGIC CEREBRAL PALSY*** on the opposite side. Drawing milestones: **copies a LINE at 2, CIRCLE at 3, CROSS at 4, SQUARE at 4–5, TRIANGLE at 5–6, DIAMOND at 6–7, CROSS (Doctor's cross)/CYLINDER at ~9, and CUBE at ~11 years.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Hand dominance requires **maturation of interhemispheric connections and lateralisation of motor control**, which is not complete until the preschool years — so an infant should use both hands equally. **Early preference means the non-preferred hand is not working**, which is the earliest sign of hemiplegic CP long before spasticity is obvious. Forcing a change of handedness later causes frustration and stuttering and should be avoided.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Core fine motor milestones: 3–4 months — hands open, reaches for objects; 5 months — bidextrous (ulnar) grasp; 6–7 months — transfers objects, unidextrous/palmar grasp; 9 months — immature PINCER; 12 months — mature pincer grasp and releases voluntarily; 15 months — tower of 2 cubes; 18 months — tower of 3–4; 2 years — tower of 6, turns pages singly; 3 years — tower of 9, copies a circle, rides a tricycle.**
- **Gross motor: head holding 3 months, rolls over 5, sits with support 6 and without support 8, stands with support 9, walks with support 12, walks alone 13–15, runs 18 months, climbs stairs 2 years (one foot per step at 3), hops at 4.**
- **Language: coos 2–3 months, babbles 6, monosyllables 9, 1–2 words at 12, 10 words at 18, 2-word sentences at 2 years (with 50% of speech intelligible to strangers), 3-word sentences at 3 years (75% intelligible).**
- **Social: social smile 2 months, stranger anxiety 6–9 months, waves bye-bye 9, feeds self with a spoon 15–18 months, dry by day 2 years, dresses with help 3, plays cooperatively 3–4.**
- **RED FLAGS for immediate referral: no social smile by 3 months, no head control by 4 months, EARLY HAND PREFERENCE before 1 year, not sitting by 9 months, not walking by 18 months, no words by 18 months, no 2-word phrases by 2 years, and ***ANY LOSS OF PREVIOUSLY ACQUIRED SKILLS (regression), which always demands urgent investigation.***

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Handedness → **3 years**; cylinder → **~9 years**.

**REMEMBER:** *Both hands until three — a favourite hand in infancy means the other one is weak.*

`Q9091, Q9653`

---

## 26. The First Void and the First Stool

**PEARL:** **A healthy neonate should pass URINE by 24 HOURS and MECONIUM by 48 HOURS.** A well baby who has **not voided in the first 24 hours needs only CONTINUED BREASTFEEDING AND OBSERVATION** — delay this early is usually benign and reflects low intake. **Failure to pass urine beyond 24 hours, or a poor stream with a palpable bladder, demands evaluation for OBSTRUCTION.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The newborn's urine output in the first day is small because **milk intake is small and the kidney is still concentrating poorly** — so early "anuria" is usually a supply problem, not a kidney problem. The dangerous alternative is **outflow obstruction — POSTERIOR URETHRAL VALVES in a boy** — which is why the examination looks specifically for a **palpable bladder, a weak dribbling stream and a palpable kidney.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Causes of delayed voiding: inadequate intake, renal agenesis/dysplasia, POSTERIOR URETHRAL VALVES (boys — the commonest obstructive cause, with antenatal hydronephrosis, a distended bladder and a "KEYHOLE" sign; diagnosed by MCU and treated by valve ablation), ureteropelvic junction obstruction, ureterocele and neurogenic bladder from spinal dysraphism.**
- **Delayed passage of MECONIUM beyond 48 hours suggests HIRSCHSPRUNG DISEASE (with abdominal distension and an explosive gush of stool on rectal examination — the "SQUIRT SIGN"; diagnosed by RECTAL SUCTION BIOPSY showing ABSENT GANGLION CELLS with hypertrophied nerve trunks), meconium plug syndrome, meconium ileus (think CYSTIC FIBROSIS), imperforate anus, ILEAL ATRESIA, prematurity, HYPOTHYROIDISM and sepsis.**
- ***ALWAYS EXAMINE THE ANUS AT BIRTH*** — imperforate anus is easily missed and presents as failure to pass meconium with progressive distension and vomiting.
- **Bilious (green) vomiting in a neonate is INTESTINAL OBSTRUCTION UNTIL PROVED OTHERWISE — and MALROTATION WITH VOLVULUS is the emergency that must be excluded by an urgent UPPER GI CONTRAST STUDY, since it strangulates the entire midgut within hours.**
- **Normal urine output thereafter is 1–3 mL/kg/hour; 6 or more wet nappies a day indicates adequate breastfeeding.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Well 1-day-old who has not voided → **continue breastfeeding and observe**.

**REMEMBER:** *Urine by a day, meconium by two — and green vomit is a surgical emergency.*

`Q9094`

---

## 27. Tumour Lysis Syndrome

**PEARL:** After starting chemotherapy (typically 12–72 hours), **HYPERURICAEMIA, HYPERKALAEMIA, HYPERPHOSPHATAEMIA and HYPOCALCAEMIA with rising creatinine and oliguria = TUMOUR LYSIS SYNDROME. The FIRST and most important intervention is AGGRESSIVE HYDRATION**, alongside RASBURICASE (or allopurinol) and management of the electrolytes.

**UNDERSTAND:** The syndrome is simply **the intracellular contents of billions of lysed tumour cells appearing in the blood at once** — potassium, phosphate and nucleic acids (which become uric acid). Renal failure follows from **crystallisation of uric acid and CALCIUM PHOSPHATE in the tubules**, so the entire aim of treatment is to **keep the tubules flushed and the crystals dissolved** — which is why hydration precedes every drug, and why hypocalcaemia is a *consequence* of the hyperphosphataemia, not a separate problem.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **RASBURICASE (recombinant urate oxidase) converts uric acid to the highly soluble allantoin and works within HOURS — it is used for established or high-risk TLS. ***IT IS CONTRAINDICATED IN G6PD DEFICIENCY*** (haemolysis and methaemoglobinuria). ALLOPURINOL blocks xanthine oxidase and prevents NEW uric acid formation but does not remove what is already there, so it is prophylactic and takes days.**
- ***URINARY ALKALINISATION IS NO LONGER ROUTINELY RECOMMENDED*** — it increases uric acid solubility but promotes CALCIUM PHOSPHATE precipitation and worsens hypocalcaemia.
- **DO NOT GIVE CALCIUM for asymptomatic hypocalcaemia — it precipitates with the high phosphate and worsens renal injury; treat only symptomatic hypocalcaemia (tetany, arrhythmia).**
- **HYPERKALAEMIA is the immediate killer — monitor ECG and treat with calcium gluconate (for cardiac protection), insulin–dextrose, salbutamol and potassium binders; ***DIALYSIS*** is indicated for refractory hyperkalaemia, severe hyperphosphataemia, fluid overload or uraemia.
- **Highest risk: BURKITT LYMPHOMA, acute lymphoblastic leukaemia with a high white cell count, and other bulky, rapidly proliferating, chemosensitive tumours — in whom prophylaxis (hydration plus allopurinol or rasburicase) begins BEFORE chemotherapy.**
- **CAIRO–BISHOP criteria define laboratory and clinical TLS.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Post-chemotherapy oliguria with hyperuricaemia and hyperkalaemia → **hydration first**.

**REMEMBER:** *Flush the tubules before you give any drug — the crystals are what kill the kidney.*

`Q9095`

---

## 28. Congenital Hypothyroidism

**PEARL:** A newborn with **TSH >100 mIU/L on screening has CONGENITAL HYPOTHYROIDISM — the commonest PREVENTABLE cause of intellectual disability.** After confirming with serum TSH and free T4, the next investigation is a **RADIONUCLIDE SCAN (TECHNETIUM-99m or I-123) to determine whether a thyroid gland is present, absent or ectopic** — but ***TREATMENT WITH LEVOTHYROXINE MUST NEVER BE DELAYED FOR IMAGING.*** **THYROID DYSGENESIS (agenesis/ectopia) causes ~65–85% (non-goitrous); DYSHORMONOGENESIS ~10–30% (goitrous).**

**UNDERSTAND:** The urgency is developmental: **thyroid hormone is essential for myelination and cortical development in the first months**, and every week of delay costs IQ points. Because the neonate is **protected in utero by maternal T4 crossing the placenta**, the baby looks normal at birth and the classical signs appear only after weeks — by which time damage has begun. **This is exactly why universal newborn SCREENING exists**, and why the diagnosis is made by a laboratory value rather than by clinical suspicion.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Early clinical clues (often subtle): PROLONGED PHYSIOLOGICAL JAUNDICE (an early sign, from delayed glucuronyl transferase maturation), lethargy and poor feeding, CONSTIPATION, hoarse cry, umbilical hernia, macroglossia, dry mottled skin (cutis marmorata), hypotonia, WIDE OPEN ANTERIOR AND POSTERIOR FONTANELLES, and delayed bone age with an absent distal femoral epiphysis at term.**
- **Screening in India is by CORD BLOOD or heel-prick TSH at 48–72 HOURS — sampling BEFORE 48 hours gives false positives because of the physiological TSH SURGE after birth.**
- **Treatment: LEVOTHYROXINE 10–15 µg/kg/day started as early as possible (ideally within 2 weeks), aiming to normalise T4 within 2 weeks and TSH within a month; monitor 2-weekly initially. Give the tablet crushed in water — NOT with soy, iron or calcium, which impair absorption.**
- **TRANSIENT hypothyroidism occurs with maternal antithyroid drugs, iodine excess (povidone-iodine antiseptics) or deficiency, and maternal blocking antibodies — hence re-evaluation off treatment at 3 years if the scan showed a normal gland.**
- **CRETINISM (untreated congenital hypothyroidism) causes severe intellectual disability, deaf-mutism, spasticity and short stature — entirely preventable.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** TSH >100 in a newborn → **technetium/radionuclide uptake scan** to define the aetiology (while starting treatment).

**REMEMBER:** *Treat first, image second — every week without thyroxine costs the brain.*

`Q9096`

---

## 29. West Syndrome (Infantile Spasms)

**PEARL:** The triad of **INFANTILE SPASMS (sudden flexion "SALAAM" or jack-knife attacks IN CLUSTERS, usually on waking), DEVELOPMENTAL REGRESSION, and HYPSARRHYTHMIA on EEG (high-voltage, chaotic, disorganised slow waves with multifocal spikes) = WEST SYNDROME**, presenting at **3–12 months.** The treatment of choice is **ACTH (or high-dose prednisolone); VIGABATRIN is first-line when the cause is TUBEROUS SCLEROSIS.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Spasms are frequently dismissed as colic or startles, and the **developmental REGRESSION is the clue that this is not benign** — the epileptic encephalopathy itself damages the developing brain, so the EEG abnormality is not merely a marker but a cause of the deterioration. This is why treatment is urgent and why **SHORTER LEAD TIME TO TREATMENT PREDICTS BETTER DEVELOPMENTAL OUTCOME** — one of the few epilepsies where days matter.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Causes: SYMPTOMATIC in ~2/3 — TUBEROUS SCLEROSIS (the classic), hypoxic–ischaemic encephalopathy, cortical malformations, intracranial haemorrhage, congenital infection and inborn errors of metabolism; the rest are idiopathic/cryptogenic (with a better prognosis). ***EXAMINE THE SKIN WITH A WOOD'S LAMP FOR ASH-LEAF MACULES*** in every infant with spasms.**
- **Vigabatrin's dose-limiting toxicity is IRREVERSIBLE PERIPHERAL VISUAL FIELD CONSTRICTION, requiring visual monitoring.**
- **Many children evolve into LENNOX–GASTAUT SYNDROME (multiple seizure types, SLOW 1–2.5 Hz spike-and-wave, intellectual disability, refractory to treatment).**
- **Distinguish from BENIGN SLEEP MYOCLONUS (occurs only in sleep, stops on waking, normal EEG and development) and from infantile colic.**
- **Work-up: EEG (ideally including sleep), MRI BRAIN, and metabolic/genetic testing where no cause is found.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Spasms + regression + hypsarrhythmia → **ACTH**.

**REMEMBER:** *Clusters of salaams with lost milestones and a chaotic EEG — treat within days, not weeks.*

`Q9097`

---

## 30. Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy

**PEARL:** A boy of **3–5 years with difficulty running, climbing stairs and RISING FROM THE FLOOR (GOWERS' SIGN — "climbing up his own body"), a waddling gait and CALF PSEUDOHYPERTROPHY has DUCHENNE MUSCULAR DYSTROPHY** — X-linked recessive, from a mutation in the **DYSTROPHIN gene at Xp21**, with a hugely raised **CREATINE KINASE.** ***DEATH OCCURS IN THE LATE TEENS TO TWENTIES (THIRD DECADE)***, usually from respiratory failure or CARDIOMYOPATHY.

**UNDERSTAND:** Dystrophin **anchors the muscle cytoskeleton (actin) to the extracellular matrix through the dystrophin–glycoprotein complex**, so without it the sarcolemma tears with every contraction — hence **repeated cycles of necrosis and regeneration, a leaking CK, and eventual replacement of muscle by FAT AND FIBROUS TISSUE** (the pseudohypertrophied calf that is weak, not strong). The **proximal, symmetrical, progressive weakness** follows directly.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Diagnosis: markedly raised CK (10–100× normal, present from birth), then GENETIC TESTING (deletion/duplication analysis, then sequencing) — ***MUSCLE BIOPSY IS NOW RARELY NEEDED***, though it shows variable fibre size, necrosis, regeneration, fatty replacement and ABSENT dystrophin on immunostaining.
- **BECKER muscular dystrophy is the same gene with an IN-FRAME mutation producing reduced/abnormal but PRESENT dystrophin — later onset, slower course, and survival into middle age (the "READING FRAME rule").**
- **Associated features: intellectual impairment (mean IQ ~1 SD below average, non-progressive), DILATED CARDIOMYOPATHY in almost all by the late teens, scoliosis and progressive restrictive lung disease.**
- **Management: ***CORTICOSTEROIDS (prednisolone/deflazacort) PROLONG AMBULATION BY 2–3 YEARS***, physiotherapy and contracture prevention, scoliosis surgery, ***CARDIAC SURVEILLANCE WITH ACE INHIBITORS/beta-blockers STARTED BEFORE symptoms***, non-invasive ventilation, and newer exon-skipping/gene therapies for specific mutations. Genetic counselling and carrier testing are essential — carrier mothers may have a raised CK and cardiomyopathy.
- **Loss of ambulation typically occurs at 9–13 years untreated; scoliosis then accelerates and respiratory function declines.**
- **DMD is the COMMONEST inherited neuromuscular disease of childhood.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Gowers' sign with fatty replacement on biopsy → **DMD; death in the third decade**.

**REMEMBER:** *No dystrophin means the membrane tears with every step — big calves, weak boy, early death.*

`Q9098`

---

## 31. Diabetic Ketoacidosis in Children

**PEARL:** Per **ISPAD guidelines: manage AIRWAY, BREATHING AND CIRCULATION, give an initial FLUID BOLUS of 10–20 mL/kg NORMAL SALINE, and START THE INSULIN INFUSION (0.05–0.1 U/kg/hour) ONLY AFTER 1–2 HOURS OF FLUID REPLACEMENT.** ***INSULIN IS NEVER GIVEN AS A BOLUS IN CHILDREN.***

**UNDERSTAND:** The delay before insulin is the crucial paediatric difference, and its purpose is to avoid **CEREBRAL OEDEMA — the leading cause of death in paediatric DKA.** Starting insulin immediately causes a **rapid fall in glucose and therefore in serum osmolality**, drawing water into brain cells already adapted to a hyperosmolar state. Fluids alone lower glucose substantially, restore perfusion and correct acidosis, so waiting costs nothing and prevents the one complication that kills.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Diagnostic criteria: hyperglycaemia >200 mg/dL, venous pH <7.3 or bicarbonate <15, and ketonaemia/ketonuria. Severity by pH: mild <7.3, moderate <7.2, severe <7.1.**
- **Rules that prevent cerebral oedema: rehydrate SLOWLY over 48 HOURS, avoid excessive fluid boluses, do NOT let glucose fall faster than ~50–100 mg/dL per hour, ***ADD DEXTROSE to the fluid once glucose reaches ~250–300 mg/dL rather than stopping insulin*** (insulin is needed to clear ketones), and AVOID BICARBONATE (which worsens cerebral oedema and paradoxical CNS acidosis) except for life-threatening hyperkalaemia or pH <6.9.**
- **POTASSIUM: total body potassium is always DEPLETED despite a normal or high serum level; add potassium to the fluids once the child passes urine and potassium is <5.5. Watch for hypokalaemia as insulin drives potassium intracellularly.**
- **Warning signs of CEREBRAL OEDEMA (typically 4–12 hours into treatment): headache, recurrence of vomiting, BRADYCARDIA WITH RISING BLOOD PRESSURE, falling consciousness, incontinence and cranial nerve palsies. ***TREAT IMMEDIATELY WITH MANNITOL OR HYPERTONIC SALINE — do not wait for a CT scan.***
- **Precipitants: new-onset type 1 diabetes (in ~a third of children), infection, insulin omission (the commonest in known diabetics) and pump failure. DKA remains the commonest cause of death in childhood diabetes.**
- **After resolution (pH >7.3, bicarbonate >15, ketones cleared), transition to subcutaneous insulin ***WITH AN OVERLAP*** before stopping the infusion.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Severe paediatric DKA → **ABC, 20 mL/kg normal saline, insulin after 1 hour**.

**REMEMBER:** *Fluids first, insulin an hour later, and never drop the osmolality fast — cerebral oedema is what kills children.*

`Q9100`

---

## 32. Biliary Atresia
