- **Secondary FSGS: HIV, heroin, obesity, sickle cell disease, reflux nephropathy, reduced nephron mass and hypertension — always looked for before labelling it primary. Familial forms (NPHS1 nephrin, NPHS2 podocin) are steroid-resistant from the start.**
- **FSGS can RECUR IN A TRANSPLANTED KIDNEY (a circulating permeability factor) — a favourite exam contrast with MCD.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Steroid-unresponsive proteinuria with segmental sclerosis and hyalinosis → **FSGS**.

**REMEMBER:** *Minimal change loses only function; FSGS loses podocytes — and scars never respond to steroids.*

`Q11782`

---

## 94. Micronutrients and Monitoring in Severe Acute Malnutrition

**PEARL:** On **day 1 of SAM management give FOLIC ACID 5 mg (then 1 mg daily), VITAMIN A by age (2 lakh IU over 1 year, 1 lakh IU at 6–12 months, 50,000 IU under 6 months), ZINC 2 mg/kg/day, COPPER 0.2–0.3 mg/kg/day — and give all vitamins and minerals at TWICE the recommended daily allowance. ***IRON (3 mg/kg/day) IS WITHHELD UNTIL THE CHILD IS GAINING WEIGHT, AFTER THE STABILISATION PHASE.*** **ACUTE malnutrition (wasting) is assessed by WEIGHT-FOR-HEIGHT.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The delay in giving iron is the most examinable and the most physiological point. In the stabilisation phase, **transferrin and other iron-binding proteins are depleted**, so supplemental iron circulates as **FREE IRON — which fuels bacterial growth and generates FREE RADICALS in tissues already depleted of antioxidants.** Withholding it until anabolism begins converts a poison into a nutrient.

**MUST KNOW:**
- ***PRIMARY FAILURE OF RECOVERY (inpatient): failure to REGAIN APPETITE by DAY 4, failure to START LOSING OEDEMA by DAY 4, OEDEMA STILL PRESENT ON DAY 10, and failure to gain at least 5 g/kg/day by day 10. SECONDARY FAILURE: failure to gain 5 g/kg/day for 3 CONSECUTIVE DAYS during rehabilitation, or REAPPEARANCE OF OEDEMA after 4 weeks*** (admission day = day 0).
- **Feeding phases: STABILISATION with F-75 (75 kcal/100 mL, low protein, low sodium) for the first days — ***DELIBERATELY NOT ENOUGH FOR GROWTH***, to avoid REFEEDING SYNDROME and heart failure — then transition and REHABILITATION with F-100 or ready-to-use therapeutic food (RUTF) at 150–220 kcal/kg/day for catch-up growth.
- **Failure to respond should trigger a search for an unrecognised problem: TUBERCULOSIS, HIV, urinary infection, severe anaemia, vitamin/mineral deficiency (especially potassium and magnesium), malabsorption, inadequate feeding at night, or a wrongly calculated diet.**
- **Discharge/transition criteria: appetite returned, oedema resolved, infections treated, weight gain established, and the mother trained in feeding; follow-up must continue in the community.**
- **A child is admitted for INPATIENT care if there are complications — anorexia, severe oedema, hypoglycaemia, hypothermia, dehydration, shock, severe anaemia, high fever or a failed APPETITE TEST; uncomplicated SAM is managed in the community with RUTF.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Folic acid on day 1 of SAM → **5 mg**; best parameter for acute malnutrition → **weight-for-height**; primary failure of recovery → **the day 4/day 10 criteria**.

**REMEMBER:** *Everything is doubled except iron — and iron waits for the appetite to come back.*

`Q11591, Q11590, Q11786`

---

## 95. Neonatal Meningitis — the Organisms

**PEARL:** **Neonatal meningitis and sepsis (0–2 months): WORLDWIDE, GROUP B STREPTOCOCCUS (*Streptococcus agalactiae*) is commonest; ***IN INDIA, GRAM-NEGATIVE ORGANISMS — *E. COLI* AND *KLEBSIELLA* — PREDOMINATE.*** *LISTERIA MONOCYTOGENES* is the classic third. From **2 months to 2 years, *STREPTOCOCCUS PNEUMONIAE* then *H. INFLUENZAE*; over 2 years, *S. PNEUMONIAE* then *NEISSERIA MENINGITIDIS*.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The neonatal organisms are the **flora of the maternal genital tract and gut** — the baby is colonised during passage through the birth canal — which is why **early-onset (<72 hours) sepsis is MATERNALLY acquired and vertical, while LATE-onset (>72 hours) sepsis is NOSOCOMIAL or community-acquired** (*Staphylococcus*, *Klebsiella*, *Pseudomonas*, *Candida*). The Indian difference reflects a **higher burden of gram-negative colonisation and hospital-acquired infection**, and is a favourite "in India" exam qualifier.

**MUST KNOW:**
- ***A NEONATE WITH MENINGITIS DOES NOT HAVE NECK STIFFNESS***: early features are temperature INSTABILITY (hypo- or hyperthermia), lethargy, poor feeding, vomiting, bradycardia and apnoea; late features are seizures, a BULGING FONTANELLE and nuchal rigidity. ***"THE NEONATE WITH SEPSIS JUST LOOKS WRONG" — a low threshold for lumbar puncture and blood culture is essential***, and up to a third of neonates with sepsis have meningitis.
- **Empirical therapy: AMPICILLIN (covering *Listeria* and enterococci) PLUS an AMINOGLYCOSIDE (gentamicin/amikacin) or a third-generation CEPHALOSPORIN (cefotaxime — ***ceftriaxone is AVOIDED in neonates: it displaces bilirubin and precipitates with calcium***). Treat meningitis for 14 days (gram-positive) to 21 days (gram-negative), guided by culture and repeat CSF.
- **Prevention: intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis for maternal GBS colonisation or risk factors, clean delivery practices, hand hygiene, exclusive breastfeeding and KANGAROO CARE.**
- **BLOOD CULTURE remains the gold standard for neonatal sepsis and for enteric fever; the "septic screen" (CRP, total leucocyte count, band:total neutrophil ratio, micro-ESR) only supports the decision.**
- **Complications: ventriculitis, hydrocephalus, cerebral abscess (especially *Citrobacter* and *Proteus*), subdural effusion, deafness and neurodevelopmental impairment — hence follow-up hearing tests and imaging.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Commonest organism of neonatal meningitis in India → ***E. coli***; worldwide → **group B *Streptococcus***.

**REMEMBER:** *Early sepsis comes from the mother, late sepsis from the ward — and in India it is gram-negative.*

`Q11812, Q11890`

---

## 96. Dengue in Children — Fluid Management

**PEARL:** **DENGUE WITH WARNING SIGNS and NO hypotension is treated with an ISOTONIC CRYSTALLOID (RINGER LACTATE or normal saline) at 5–7 mL/kg/HOUR, tapering as the patient improves. With DOCUMENTED HYPOTENSION/shock, give 10–20 mL/kg as a BOLUS.** ***A PLATELET COUNT OF 50,000 WITHOUT BLEEDING IS NOT AN INDICATION FOR PLATELET TRANSFUSION.***

**UNDERSTAND:** Dengue kills through **PLASMA LEAKAGE, not bleeding.** During the critical phase, **capillary permeability increases for 24–48 hours**, so intravascular volume falls while the haematocrit rises — the entire therapy is **judicious fluid replacement across that window, then STOPPING before the leaked fluid returns to the circulation and causes pulmonary oedema.** Platelets fall as a bystander phenomenon and **transfused platelets are consumed within hours without preventing bleeding**, which is why prophylactic transfusion is discouraged.

**MUST KNOW:**
- ***WARNING SIGNS (the trigger for admission): ABDOMINAL PAIN OR TENDERNESS, PERSISTENT VOMITING, CLINICAL FLUID ACCUMULATION (ascites, pleural effusion), MUCOSAL BLEEDING, LETHARGY OR RESTLESSNESS, LIVER ENLARGEMENT >2 cm, and a RISING HAEMATOCRIT WITH A RAPIDLY FALLING PLATELET COUNT.***
- **Three phases: FEBRILE (2–7 days, high fever, myalgia, retro-orbital pain, flushing, positive tourniquet test), ***CRITICAL (around defervescence, days 3–7 — THE DANGEROUS PHASE, when the fever falls and the patient LOOKS BETTER while leaking)***, and RECOVERY (fluid reabsorption, "convalescent rash" of ISLES OF WHITE IN A SEA OF RED, bradycardia — and the risk of FLUID OVERLOAD if infusion continues).
- **Diagnosis: NS1 ANTIGEN in the first 5 days, IgM from day 5, and paired IgG; leucopenia, thrombocytopenia and a rising haematocrit are the classic laboratory triad.**
- ***USE PARACETAMOL ONLY — ASPIRIN AND NSAIDs ARE CONTRAINDICATED*** (bleeding risk and Reye syndrome), and avoid intramuscular injections.
- **Monitor haematocrit, urine output (aim ≥0.5–1 mL/kg/hour), vital signs and NARROWING PULSE PRESSURE (≤20 mmHg indicates shock). Colloids are reserved for refractory shock; blood transfusion for significant bleeding.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** NS1-positive child with warning signs and no hypotension → **Ringer lactate 7 mL/kg/hour** (not a platelet transfusion).

**REMEMBER:** *Dengue leaks before it bleeds — replace the plasma, not the platelets.*

`Q11785`

---

## 97. Why Paediatric Resuscitation Differs from Adult

**PEARL:** ***IN CHILDREN, CARDIAC ARREST AND DYSRHYTHMIAS ARE ALMOST ALWAYS THE RESULT OF RESPIRATORY INSUFFICIENCY OR HYPOXIA — NOT PRIMARY CARDIAC DISEASE***, unlike adults. Hence the rhythm is usually **BRADYCARDIA PROGRESSING TO ASYSTOLE OR PULSELESS ELECTRICAL ACTIVITY (NON-shockable), ventricular dysrhythmias are UNCOMMON, and VENTILATION IS WEIGHTED MORE HEAVILY** — 15:2 with two rescuers (3:1 in the newborn) rather than the adult 30:2.

**UNDERSTAND:** An adult arrest is typically a **sudden electrical event on a diseased coronary circulation — so defibrillation is the priority and compressions preserve the brain until the shock arrives.** A child arrests at the **end of a long period of respiratory or circulatory deterioration**, so by the time the heart stops, the whole body is already hypoxic and acidotic. That is why **outcomes are far worse for paediatric out-of-hospital arrest, why RECOGNISING THE PRE-ARREST PHASE is the highest-value intervention of all, and why OXYGEN AND VENTILATION are the therapy.**

**MUST KNOW:**
- ***BRADYCARDIA WITH POOR PERFUSION IN A CHILD IS PRE-ARREST: START CHEST COMPRESSIONS IF THE HEART RATE IS BELOW 60 DESPITE ADEQUATE OXYGENATION AND VENTILATION*** — you do not wait for the pulse to disappear.
- **The four phases of cardiac arrest: PRE-ARREST (the phase where paediatric lives are actually saved), NO-FLOW (untreated arrest before recognition), LOW-FLOW (during CPR) and POST-RESUSCITATION (targeted temperature management, avoiding hyperoxia, hypotension and hyperthermia).**
- **SUPRAVENTRICULAR TACHYCARDIA is the commonest arrhythmia needing treatment in children (narrow complex, rate typically >220 in infants and >180 in children, with no beat-to-beat variability) — managed with vagal manoeuvres, then ADENOSINE, and SYNCHRONISED CARDIOVERSION if unstable.**
- **Paediatric BLS sequence: check response → check breathing and pulse (≤10 s) → 5 rescue breaths → compressions at 100–120/min, one-third of chest depth, 15:2 (two rescuers) or 30:2 (single rescuer).**
- **The reversible causes ("4 Hs and 4 Ts") skew differently in children: HYPOXIA above all, then hypovolaemia (dehydration, sepsis, trauma), hypo/hyperkalaemia and hypothermia; tension pneumothorax, tamponade, toxins and thrombosis.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "True about the difference between adult and paediatric resuscitation" → **dysrhythmias are most often due to respiratory insufficiency in children**.

**REMEMBER:** *Adults arrest from the heart and children from the lungs — so give them air.*

`Q11781, Q11574, Q11567`

---

## 98. Ciliopathies with Renal Cysts

**PEARL:** **Bardet–Biedl** (renal cysts + obesity + **retinitis pigmentosa** + **polydactyly** + intellectual disability + hypogonadism) and **Meckel–Gruber** (renal cysts + **occipital encephalocele** + **polydactyly** — lethal) are **defects of ciliary proteins**.

**UNDERSTAND:** The **primary cilium (9+0, non-motile)** is a sensory antenna present on almost every cell — in the renal tubule it senses flow, in the photoreceptor it forms the connecting cilium, and in the limb bud it transduces **Sonic hedgehog** signalling. One defective protein therefore hits kidney, retina and digits together — which is why **cystic kidneys + retinal dystrophy + polydactyly** is such a recognisable cluster.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Denys–Drash is NOT a ciliopathy** — it is a ***WT1*** mutation: **progressive renal failure (diffuse mesangial sclerosis) + ambiguous genitalia + WILMS TUMOUR**. (**WAGR** = Wilms, Aniridia, GU anomalies, Retardation — an 11p13 deletion.)
- **Other ciliopathies:** **ADPKD and ARPKD** (polycystin/fibrocystin), **nephronophthisis** (commonest genetic cause of ESRD in children/adolescents), **Joubert syndrome** (molar tooth sign), **Alström**, **Kartagener/primary ciliary dyskinesia** (that one affects **motile** cilia).
- **Meckel–Gruber is the commonest LETHAL ciliopathy** and is a differential for **antenatally detected enlarged echogenic kidneys with oligohydramnios**.
- **Bardet–Biedl vs Prader–Willi:** both give obesity and hypogonadism, but **BBS has retinitis pigmentosa, polydactyly and renal disease**; PWS has neonatal hypotonia and 15q11 imprinting.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Renal cyst is a component of" → both Meckel–Gruber and Bardet–Biedl (i.e. "both A and B"); Denys–Drash is the distractor.

**REMEMBER:** *Cysts + retina + extra digits = a cilium problem. Cysts + ambiguous genitalia + Wilms = WT1.*

`Q10759`

---

## 99. Anterior Fontanelle

**PEARL:** Closes at **12–24 months**; the **posterior fontanelle closes by 2–3 months**. Diamond-shaped, at the junction of the coronal and sagittal sutures.

**UNDERSTAND:** The fontanelles exist to allow **moulding during birth** and **rapid brain growth** afterwards. Because the anterior fontanelle is a soft window over the subarachnoid space, its **tension directly reflects intracranial pressure** — the only place in the body where ICP can be assessed by palpation.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Bulging, tense fontanelle = raised ICP** — meningitis, hydrocephalus, intracranial bleed (assess with the baby **upright and calm**; it bulges normally when crying). **Sunken = dehydration**.
- **DELAYED closure (>18–24 months):** **congenital HYPOTHYROIDISM**, **rickets**, **Down syndrome**, **achondroplasia**, osteogenesis imperfecta, raised ICP, IUGR. Also — **a large posterior fontanelle at birth is a classic early clue to congenital hypothyroidism**.
- **Early closure (craniosynostosis)** → abnormal skull shape: **sagittal suture → scaphocephaly** (commonest), **coronal → brachycephaly**, all sutures → **oxycephaly** with raised ICP.
- The anterior fontanelle is the **acoustic window for cranial ultrasound in neonates** — the imaging of choice for **intraventricular haemorrhage** in preterms (no radiation, done at the cot side).
- **All other fontanelles (posterior, two sphenoidal, two mastoid)** close within the first few months.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Straight recall of closure age, and vignette-based ("delayed closure + constipation + prolonged jaundice" → congenital hypothyroidism).

**REMEMBER:** *Posterior closes by 2–3 months, anterior by 18 months — late closure means think thyroid or rickets.*

`Q11641`

---

## 100. Fontanelle (Ventricular) Tap

**PEARL:** The needle passes **scalp → dura → arachnoid** into the lateral ventricle — **no bone is crossed**, which is the entire point of using the open fontanelle.

**UNDERSTAND:** At the fontanelle the skull bones have not yet met, so only soft tissue and meninges separate the needle from the ventricle. This is the only period of life when the ventricle can be reached percutaneously without a burr hole.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Enter at the ANTEROLATERAL angle of the anterior fontanelle, away from the midline** — the **superior sagittal sinus runs in the midline** and puncturing it is catastrophic.
- Use a **short 25 G butterfly/spinal needle**; **do not aspirate forcibly** and remove only what is needed — rapid decompression risks **subdural haematoma from tearing bridging veins**.
- **Indications:** urgent decompression in **post-haemorrhagic hydrocephalus** without shunt access, or CSF sampling for **ventriculitis/shunt infection** when LP is contraindicated or non-diagnostic.
- Note the contrast with **lumbar puncture**, which passes **skin → supraspinous → interspinous → LIGAMENTUM FLAVUM → epidural space → dura → arachnoid**.
- In infants, LP should be at **L4–L5 or lower** because the **cord ends at L3 at birth**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Structures punctured while piercing the marked structure" → **scalp, dura, arachnoid** (options adding "epicranium/pericranium" or "synchondral membrane" are the traps).

**REMEMBER:** *Anterolateral, never midline — the sagittal sinus runs down the middle.*

`Q11790`

---

## 101. Calcium Tells You the Syndrome: DiGeorge vs Williams

**PEARL:** **Low calcium (~6.5 mg/dL) + immunodeficiency + conotruncal heart defect =
DiGeorge (22q11.2 deletion).** **High calcium (>11 mg/dL) + friendly "cocktail-party"
personality + supravalvular aortic stenosis = Williams (7q11.23 deletion).**

**UNDERSTAND:** Both are contiguous gene deletion syndromes, and in both the calcium
level is a direct read-out of what tissue was lost. DiGeorge deletes the developmental
programme of the **3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouches** — no parathyroids (hypocalcaemia)
and no thymus (T-cell deficiency). Williams deletes the **elastin (ELN)** gene, which
explains the arteriopathy; the hypercalcaemia is idiopathic but reliably present and
causes the constipation, weakness and lethargy in the stem.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **DiGeorge — CATCH-22:** **C**ardiac (truncus arteriosus, interrupted aortic arch, TOF), **A**bnormal facies (hypertelorism, notched/low-set ears, short philtrum, small mouth), **T**hymic aplasia (recurrent viral/fungal infection), **C**left palate, **H**ypocalcaemia; **22**q11.2. Diagnose by **FISH/MLPA**. Absent thymic shadow on CXR. Give **irradiated, CMV-negative blood** — live vaccines contraindicated if T cells are very low.
- **Williams:** elfin facies, full cheeks, wide mouth, upturned nose, stellate iris, broad forehead; **overfriendly, loquacious**, intellectual disability with relatively preserved language; **supravalvular AS** and peripheral pulmonary stenosis; hypercalcaemia. Diagnose by **FISH for ELN**.
- Recurrent infection + hypocalcaemia in a child = think thymus + parathyroid = **one pouch defect**.
- Both are usually **de novo** deletions.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The stem hands you a serum calcium and expects you to jump straight
to the syndrome. **6.5 → DiGeorge, >11 → Williams.** Supravalvular AS is essentially
pathognomonic for Williams in an exam.

**REMEMBER:** *DiGeorge lost its parathyroids (low Ca); Williams keeps too much (high Ca)
and is too friendly.*

`Q9539, Q9540`

---

## 102. Trisomy 18 vs Trisomy 13

**PEARL:** **Clenched hands with overlapping fingers + rocker-bottom feet + small mouth
and micrognathia = Edward syndrome (Trisomy 18).** **Holoprosencephaly + cleft lip/palate
+ polydactyly + cutis aplasia = Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13).**

**UNDERSTAND:** Both are lethal autosomal trisomies with severe failure to thrive, so the
exam separates them entirely on the **hand and midline findings**. Trisomy 18 is the
**hand/limb-posture** syndrome; Trisomy 13 is the **midline-defect** syndrome — the
forebrain fails to cleave (holoprosencephaly), which drags the midface with it (cleft
lip/palate, hypotelorism, cyclopia at the extreme).

**MUST KNOW:**

| | Edward (18) | Patau (13) |
|---|---|---|
| Hands | **Overlapping fingers** (index over 3rd, 5th over 4th) | **Polydactyly** |
| Feet | **Rocker-bottom** | Rocker-bottom also possible |
| CNS | Microcephaly, prominent occiput | **Holoprosencephaly** |
| Skin/face | Micrognathia, small mouth, **low-set ears** | **Cleft lip/palate, cutis aplasia** of scalp |
| Other | VSD, horseshoe kidney, **short sternum**, scoliosis | **Polycystic kidneys**, microphthalmia, omphalocele |
| Incidence | 1:6000–8000 (2nd commonest trisomy) | 1:10,000 |

- **Both usually die in the first year**; median survival is days to weeks.
- **Down (21)** is the trisomy associated with **presenile Alzheimer disease** (APP gene on chromosome 21) — a favourite oblique way of naming Down in a stem.
- Screening: Trisomy 18 gives a **quadruple test with LOW AFP, LOW hCG, LOW oestriol** (everything low) — contrast Down (low AFP and oestriol, **high** hCG and inhibin A).

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The option list often mislabels deliberately ("Trisomy 18 (Patau
syndrome)") to catch candidates who match the number without reading the name. Check
**both halves** of every option.

**REMEMBER:** *18 = overlapping fingers; 13 = midline splits (brain, lip, extra digits).*

`Q9536, Q9543`

---

## 103. Turner vs Noonan

**PEARL:** Turner-like features **in a boy, or in both a brother and a sister**, is
**Noonan syndrome** — autosomal dominant, **PTPN11** gene on **chromosome 12**, with a
**normal karyotype** and **pulmonary stenosis**.

**UNDERSTAND:** Turner (45,XO) is a sex-chromosome monosomy, so it occurs **only in
phenotypic females** and is essentially always **sporadic** — siblings are not affected.
Noonan is a RASopathy inherited **autosomal dominantly**, so it affects **both sexes and
runs in families**. That single logic — sex and family history — resolves every version
of this question.

**MUST KNOW:**

| | Turner | Noonan |
|---|---|---|
| Genetics | **45,XO** (or mosaic/isochromosome) | **PTPN11** (chr 12), AD, karyotype **normal** |
| Sex | Females only | Both sexes |
| Heart | **Bicuspid aortic valve, coarctation of aorta** (left-sided) | **Pulmonary stenosis, HOCM** (right-sided) |
| Intellect | Usually normal (poor visuospatial/maths) | Mild intellectual disability common |
| Gonads | **Streak ovaries**, primary amenorrhoea, infertile | Cryptorchidism in males; fertility often preserved |
| Other | Lymphoedema of hands/feet at birth, horseshoe kidney | Bleeding diathesis (factor XI deficiency), lymphatic dysplasia |

- Shared: **short stature, webbed neck, low posterior hairline, cubitus valgus, shield chest, widely spaced nipples, downslanting palpebral fissures, dislocated patella**.
- **Turner management:** growth hormone for height, oestrogen replacement at puberty; **gonadectomy is NOT indicated** — the streak gonads of a pure 45,XO carry no tumour risk. **Prophylactic gonadectomy is only for Y-chromosome material** (Turner mosaic with Y, or complete androgen insensitivity) because of **gonadoblastoma/dysgerminoma** risk.
- Turner is the commonest cause of **primary amenorrhoea with absent secondary sexual characteristics and a normal uterus**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Two traps live here — "laparoscopic gonadectomy" offered for plain
Turner (wrong; **reassurance/hormone replacement**), and Turner offered for an affected
**male sibling** (wrong; **Noonan**).

**REMEMBER:** *Turner is left-sided (coarctation) and female-only; Noonan is right-sided
(pulmonary stenosis) and runs in families.*

`Q9537, Q9544, Q9545`

---

## 104. Anticipation, Sherman's Paradox and Fragile X

**PEARL:** A trinucleotide-repeat disorder becoming **more severe and earlier in onset in
successive generations** is **anticipation**; in Fragile X the specific term for this
non-Mendelian pattern of increasing penetrance down the pedigree is **Sherman's paradox**
— both terms are correct.

**UNDERSTAND:** Repeat tracts are unstable during gametogenesis, so a **premutation (55–200
CGG)** expands to a **full mutation (>200 CGG)** when transmitted. Full expansion
**methylates and silences FMR1**, abolishing FMRP and causing the syndrome. Because the
expansion accumulates over generations, grandsons of a transmitting male are affected
while he is not — the paradox Sherman described.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Fragile X = commonest inherited cause of intellectual disability** (Down is the commonest genetic cause overall but is usually sporadic). **X-linked, CGG repeat in FMR1 at Xq27.3.**
- Phenotype: **long face, large everted ears, prominent jaw, macro-orchidism after puberty**, hyperextensible joints, mitral valve prolapse, autistic features, hand-flapping.
- **In Fragile X, expansion occurs only on MATERNAL transmission** (contrast **Huntington and myotonic dystrophy**, where the biggest expansions come through the **father** — paternal anticipation).
- **Premutation carriers are not normal:** males → **FXTAS** (fragile X tremor/ataxia syndrome); females → **premature ovarian insufficiency**.
- Diagnosis is by **PCR + Southern blot / methylation analysis**, not karyotype.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Both A and B" is the answer when anticipation and Sherman's paradox
are offered separately — the examiner is testing that you know they describe the same
phenomenon.

**REMEMBER:** *Each generation adds repeats — the disease gets younger and worse.*

`Q9538`

---

## 105. Growth, Teeth and Fontanelle Milestones

**PEARL:** **Weight doubles by 5 months, triples by 1 year, quadruples by 2 years,
×5 by 3 years.** **Rule of six for teeth:** primary dentition begins at **6 months**,
permanent dentition at **6 years**. **Anterior fontanelle closes last (18 months).**

**UNDERSTAND:** These are consensus reference points rather than derived facts, but they
work as a coherent picture: growth velocity is highest in infancy and decelerates, which
is why doubling takes 5 months and the next doubling takes another 19.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Fontanelle closure order:** posterior **2–3 months** → sphenoidal **6 months** → mastoid **6–18 months** → **anterior 12–18 months (last)**. Delayed anterior closure: **hypothyroidism, rickets, Down syndrome, achondroplasia, raised ICP, osteogenesis imperfecta**.
- **Teeth:** eruption starts ~6 months (lower central incisors); **20 deciduous teeth complete by 2½–3 years**; 32 permanent teeth. **Delayed eruption = no teeth by 13 months**; **commonest cause is idiopathic/familial**, then hypothyroidism, hypoparathyroidism, rickets. **Natal/prenatal teeth** with congenital syphilis; **Hutchinson incisors and mulberry molars** are the syphilitic permanent-teeth stigmata.
- **Height:** birth 50 cm → 75 cm at 1 yr → 87–90 cm at 2 yr → doubles birth length (100 cm) at 4 yr; ~5–6 cm/yr from 4 yrs to puberty.
- **Upper:lower segment ratio:** **1.7 at birth → 1.3 at 3 years → 1.0 at 7 years → 0.9 in adults** (adult proportions by ~10 years). **High U/L ratio = short-limb dwarfism** (achondroplasia, untreated hypothyroidism, Turner); **low U/L ratio = long limbs** (Marfan, Klinefelter, eunuchoidism).
- **Anthropometry:** weigh to the nearest **100 g**; **standing height is about 0.7–1 cm LESS than recumbent length** (spinal compression) — the classic wrong-number distractor is 1.7 cm; skinfold sites include triceps and **subscapular**.
- **Head circumference:** 35 cm at birth → 47 cm at 1 yr → 49 cm at 2 yr → 55–56 cm adult.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Almost always a single-line numeric recall. The traps are
substituting the U/L ratio number (1.7) for the height-vs-length difference, and
confusing "doubles at 5 months" with "triples at 1 year".

**REMEMBER:** *5 months double, 1 year triple, 2 years quadruple; six months for milk
teeth, six years for permanent.*

`Q9546, Q9547, Q9548, Q9549, Q9550, Q9551, Q9552`

---

## 106. Prenatal Diagnosis — Choosing the Test by Gestational Age

**PEARL:** **CVS 11–14 weeks; amniocentesis ≥15 weeks; cordocentesis 18–20 weeks.** At
**15 weeks** for karyotyping, the answer is **amniocentesis**.

**UNDERSTAND:** Each test is limited by what tissue is safely accessible at that
gestation. Before 11 weeks the chorion frondosum is too small and sampling causes **limb
reduction defects**; before 15 weeks there is too little amniotic fluid, so early
amniocentesis raises the risk of **talipes and fetal loss**; the umbilical vessels are
not reliably cannulable until ~18 weeks.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **CVS:** earliest definitive karyotype, transabdominal or transcervical; risks: **confined placental mosaicism**, ~1% loss, limb defects if <10 weeks. Cannot diagnose **neural tube defects** (no AFP).
- **Amniocentesis:** gold standard; gives karyotype/FISH/QF-PCR **and amniotic AFP + acetylcholinesterase** for NTDs. Loss rate ~0.5–1%.
- **Cordocentesis (PUBS):** fetal blood — for **fetal anaemia, thrombocytopenia, rapid karyotype, intrauterine transfusion**; highest loss rate (1–2%).
- **Cell-free fetal DNA (NIPT)** from **maternal blood** from **10 weeks** — a screening test with very high sensitivity for trisomy 21/18/13; a positive result **must be confirmed** by CVS or amniocentesis. **Maternal urine has no role.**
- Recurrence risk of trisomy 21 after one affected child ≈ **1%** (higher if translocation) — hence the offer of testing.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The stem always states a **gestational age**; match it to the window.
15 weeks → amniocentesis, 12 weeks → CVS, 19 weeks with fetal anaemia → cordocentesis.

**REMEMBER:** *11–14 chorion, 15+ fluid, 18+ cord.*

`Q9542`

---

## 107. Breastfeeding and Maternal Infection

**PEARL:** Almost no maternal viral infection contraindicates breastfeeding — but
**active herpes simplex lesions ON THE BREAST require temporary cessation from that
side.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The route of transmission decides the rule. HSV is spread by **direct
contact with the lesion**, not through milk, so milk from the unaffected breast (and
expressed milk not contaminated by the lesion) is safe. Hepatitis B and C are
blood-borne and not transmitted in milk in any meaningful quantity — which is why they
are not contraindications.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Hepatitis B:** breastfeed, provided the baby gets **HBIG + hepatitis B vaccine within 12 hours** of birth.
- **Hepatitis C:** breastfeed; **interrupt only if nipples are cracked and bleeding**.
- **Varicella:** breastfeed; if maternal rash appears **5 days before to 2 days after delivery**, separate mother and baby and give **VZIG**, but **expressed milk is safe**.
- **Absolute contraindications:** **HIV (in settings where safe formula is available — WHO advises breastfeeding with ART where it is not)**, **HTLV-1/2**, active untreated **tuberculosis** (separate for 2 weeks of therapy, expressed milk fine), maternal **chemotherapy/radioisotopes/amiodarone/lithium**, and in the **infant, galactosaemia** (an absolute infant-side contraindication).
- Continue breastfeeding in maternal **CMV** (term babies), hepatitis A, and mastitis/breast abscess (feeding actually aids drainage).

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Asked as "which infection requires **temporary** cessation" —
temporary is the key word, pointing to HSV rather than the permanent HIV/HTLV
contraindications.

**REMEMBER:** *Milk is safe; the lesion is not — cover it or feed from the other side.*

`Q9535`

---

## 108. Russell–Silver Syndrome and Imprinting

**PEARL:** **Small-for-gestational-age + failure to thrive + relative macrocephaly with a
prominent forehead + small triangular jaw + clinodactyly + body asymmetry =
Russell–Silver syndrome** (hypomethylation of **11p15**, or **maternal uniparental disomy
of chromosome 7**).

**UNDERSTAND:** 11p15 carries the imprinted **IGF2 (paternal, growth-promoting)** and
**H19/CDKN1C (maternal, growth-restraining)** genes. Losing paternal IGF2 expression
produces **undergrowth (Russell–Silver)**; gaining it produces **overgrowth
(Beckwith–Wiedemann)** — the two are opposite ends of the same locus.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Beckwith–Wiedemann (11p15, paternal UPD/gain of IGF2):** **macroglossia, omphalocele, gigantism/hemihypertrophy, neonatal hypoglycaemia**, ear creases; increased risk of **Wilms tumour and hepatoblastoma** → screen with **3-monthly abdominal USS and AFP**.
- Other classic imprinting pair: **15q11–13 — paternal deletion = Prader–Willi** (neonatal hypotonia and poor feeding, then hyperphagia and obesity, hypogonadism, almond eyes); **maternal deletion = Angelman** ("happy puppet" — ataxia, seizures, inappropriate laughter, absent speech).
- **Uniparental disomy** = both copies of a chromosome from one parent; it causes disease only where genes are **imprinted** or where it unmasks a recessive mutation.
- Russell–Silver management: nutritional support and **growth hormone**; watch for hypoglycaemia and metabolic syndrome later.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Distinguish by size — Russell–Silver is the **small** child, Prader–Willi
the **hypotonic then obese** child, Beckwith–Wiedemann the **big** newborn with a big tongue.

**REMEMBER:** *Same locus, opposite parents: Russell–Silver shrinks, Beckwith–Wiedemann
overgrows.*

`Q9553`

---

## 109. Cri du Chat Syndrome

**PEARL:** **High-pitched, cat-like cry + microcephaly + hypertelorism + severe
intellectual disability = Cri du Chat, deletion of the short arm of chromosome 5 (5p−).**

**UNDERSTAND:** The cry is not metaphorical — the deletion produces a small, floppy,
abnormally shaped **larynx and epiglottis** together with CNS immaturity, giving a
mewing monotone cry that is present from birth and usually fades after infancy. That is
why the cry is the diagnostic sign only in the neonate/infant.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Features: **microcephaly** (OFC >3 SD below mean for age and sex), round "moon" face, **hypertelorism**, epicanthic folds, **micrognathia**, low-set ears, single palmar crease, cardiac defects (VSD).
- Behaviour: hyperactivity, aggression, short attention span, self-injury; profound speech delay with better receptive than expressive language.
- Most are **de novo terminal deletions of paternal origin**; ~10–15% arise from a **parental balanced translocation** → **karyotype both parents** for recurrence risk.
- Survival into adulthood is common — unlike trisomies 13 and 18.
- Other deletion syndromes to keep separate: **4p− Wolf–Hirschhorn** ("Greek warrior helmet" facies, seizures), **22q11 DiGeorge**, **7q11 Williams**, **15q11 Prader–Willi/Angelman**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Any stem containing the phrase "cry like a cat" is answered before
you read the options; the actual question is usually an **associated feature** —
**microcephaly**, never macrocephaly.

**REMEMBER:** *5p minus — the cat cries because the larynx is small.*

`Q9541`

---

## 110. Phototherapy: How It Actually Works

**PEARL:** The **chief mechanism of phototherapy is STRUCTURAL isomerisation** —
irreversible conversion of bilirubin to **lumirubin**, which is water-soluble and excreted
in bile and urine **without conjugation**.

**UNDERSTAND:** Blue light (**460–490 nm**, the absorption peak of bilirubin) rearranges
the molecule in three ways, but they are not equal. **Configurational (photo)
isomerisation** to 4Z,15E-bilirubin is fast but **reversible** — the isomer converts back
in the gut, so it contributes little net clearance. **Structural isomerisation to
lumirubin is irreversible**, so it is rate-limiting for actual bilirubin removal.
**Photo-oxidation** to colourless polar products is minor. Hence: fastest ≠ most
important.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Effectiveness depends on: wavelength (blue-green 460–490 nm), irradiance (≥30 µW/cm²/nm = intensive), and SURFACE AREA exposed** — so undress the baby, use double-surface/LED units, and keep the light close (~15–20 cm for LED).
- **Cover the eyes** (retinal damage); leave the nappy area minimal; monitor temperature and fluid balance (**increased insensible water loss**, loose greenish stools).
- **Absolute contraindication: congenital erythropoietic porphyria** → severe blistering.
- **Bronze baby syndrome** occurs with phototherapy in the presence of **conjugated (direct) hyperbilirubinaemia** — a reminder that phototherapy treats **unconjugated** jaundice only.
- Exchange transfusion is for levels above the exchange line or any sign of **acute bilirubin encephalopathy**; use the **AAP/NICE charts by age in HOURS, gestation and risk factors** — never a single fixed number.
- **Kernicterus** targets the **basal ganglia (globus pallidus), hippocampus and brainstem nuclei** → choreoathetoid cerebral palsy, **sensorineural hearing loss**, upward gaze palsy, dental enamel dysplasia.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The examiner distinguishes **"chief/main mechanism" (structural
isomerisation → lumirubin)** from "fastest mechanism" (configurational isomerisation).
Read the qualifier.

**REMEMBER:** *Lumirubin leaves for good — the reversible isomer comes back.*

`Q9576`

---

## 111. Human Milk vs Cow's Milk

**PEARL:** **Lactose is higher in human milk**; **protein, calcium, phosphate, sodium and
all electrolytes are higher in cow's milk**. Total **fat is roughly similar** — it is the
composition, not the amount, that differs.

**UNDERSTAND:** Human milk is designed for a brain-growing infant with immature kidneys:
it is rich in **lactose (galactose for cerebroside synthesis, and energy)** and low in
solute so that the **renal solute load** stays within what a neonatal kidney can excrete.
Cow's milk is designed for a calf that doubles its weight in half the time and must grow
bone and muscle, hence the protein and mineral load — which is exactly why unmodified
cow's milk before 1 year causes **hypernatraemic dehydration, occult GI blood loss and
iron deficiency anaemia**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Human milk:** lactose ~7 g/dL, protein ~1.1 g/dL with **whey:casein 60:40 (70:30 in colostrum)**, **sodium ~7 mEq/L**, and rich in **lactoferrin, lysozyme, secretory IgA, oligosaccharides, taurine, cysteine and long-chain PUFAs (DHA/ARA)**.
- **Cow's milk:** lactose ~4.5 g/dL, protein ~3.3 g/dL with **whey:casein 20:80** (curd is hard to digest), sodium ~22 mEq/L, calcium ~4× higher but **less bioavailable**.
- Human milk is **deficient in vitamin K (give 1 mg IM at birth), vitamin D (supplement 400 IU/day) and iron after 6 months** — hence complementary feeding at 6 months.
- **Colostrum** (first 3–5 days): more protein, vitamin A and **IgA**, less fat and lactose. **Foremilk** is watery and lactose-rich; **hindmilk** is fat-rich — so do not switch breasts too soon.
- **Preterm milk** has more protein, sodium and immunoglobulin than term milk — biologically matched to the preterm baby.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Which is HIGHER in human milk?" — the answer is essentially always
**lactose** (and, if offered, whey, cystine, taurine, vitamin C, lactoferrin, IgA).

**REMEMBER:** *Human milk feeds a brain (lactose); cow's milk builds a calf (protein and
calcium).*

`Q9574, Q9573`

---

## 112. Vitamin A in Severe Acute Malnutrition

**PEARL:** Vitamin A on **day 1** of SAM management: **<6 months → 50,000 IU; 6–12 months
→ 1,00,000 IU; >12 months → 2,00,000 IU** — but a child **>12 months weighing <8 kg gets
the 1,00,000 IU dose**.

**UNDERSTAND:** The dose is scaled to hepatic storage capacity, which tracks body size
rather than age; a severely wasted toddler under 8 kg has the liver mass of an infant, so
the age-based dose would risk **acute hypervitaminosis A** (bulging fontanelle, vomiting,
raised ICP). This weight exception is precisely what the exam tests.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **SAM definition (6–59 months):** **weight-for-height < −3 SD**, or **MUAC <11.5 cm**, or **bilateral pitting oedema** (oedema alone qualifies, whatever the weight).
- **The 10 steps of WHO SAM management** — treat/prevent: **hypoglycaemia, hypothermia, dehydration, electrolyte imbalance, infection**; then **micronutrients, cautious feeding, catch-up growth, sensory stimulation, follow-up**.
- **Do NOT give iron in the stabilisation phase** — free iron promotes bacterial growth and oxidative stress; start **3 mg/kg/day only after appetite returns and weight gain begins** (rehabilitation phase).
- Other micronutrients from day 1: **folic acid 5 mg on day 1 then 1 mg/day, zinc 2 mg/kg/day, copper 0.2–0.3 mg/kg/day, potassium 3–4 mmol/kg/day, magnesium**.
- Rehydrate with **ReSoMal** (lower sodium, higher potassium) — standard ORS has too much sodium for these children; never use IV fluids unless in shock.
- **Refeeding syndrome** — the killer in week 1: hypophosphataemia, hypokalaemia, hypomagnesaemia, cardiac failure. Feed **F-75 in the stabilisation phase**, then **F-100/RUTF** for catch-up.
- Routine broad-spectrum antibiotics for all SAM children (infection is often afebrile and silent).

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The stem gives an age **and** a weight — the weight is there for a
reason. **>12 months but <8 kg → 1 lakh IU**, not 2 lakh.

**REMEMBER:** *Half a lakh, one lakh, two lakh by age — but weigh under 8 kg and drop a
step.*

`Q9575`

---

## 113. Bardet–Biedl Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Retinitis pigmentosa (rod–cone dystrophy) + obesity + POSTAXIAL POLYDACTYLY +
hypogonadism + renal anomalies + learning difficulty = Bardet–Biedl syndrome.**
**Syndactyly is NOT a feature** — the digital anomaly is **extra** digits, not fused ones.

**UNDERSTAND:** Bardet–Biedl is a **ciliopathy**: the primary cilium is defective, and
every affected organ is one that depends on cilia. Photoreceptor outer segments are
modified cilia (→ retinal dystrophy), renal tubular cells are ciliated (→ cystic renal
disease, the commonest cause of death), and hypothalamic ciliary signalling regulates
satiety (→ obesity) and GnRH release (→ hypogonadism).

**MUST KNOW:**
- Autosomal recessive; >20 **BBS** genes. Presenting complaint is usually **night blindness/progressive visual loss in the first decade**, with legal blindness by the second or third.
- **Renal disease is the main determinant of prognosis** — calyceal cysts, dysplasia, chronic kidney failure.
- **Laurence–Moon syndrome** is the closely related condition classically listed with **spastic paraparesis but WITHOUT polydactyly and obesity**; many now regard them as a spectrum.
- Differentiate obesity syndromes: **Prader–Willi** (hypotonia then hyperphagia, almond eyes, small hands/feet, 15q11 paternal deletion); **Alström** (retinal dystrophy + obesity + **deafness + diabetes**, no polydactyly); **Cohen** (microcephaly, prominent incisors); **leptin/MC4R** deficiency (isolated severe early obesity).
- **Postaxial polydactyly** (extra digit on the **ulnar/fibular** side) also occurs in **Ellis–van Creveld** and **Patau (trisomy 13)**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Asked as "which is NOT a feature" — the odd one out is **syndactyly**
(or macrocephaly). If **polydactyly + obesity + retinal dystrophy** appear together, the
answer is Bardet–Biedl.

**REMEMBER:** *A ciliopathy: bad cilia in the retina, the kidney and the hypothalamus —
plus one finger too many.*

`Q9572`

---

## 114. Cleidocranial Dysostosis

**PEARL:** A child who can **approximate both shoulders in front of the chest**, with a
**soft/wide-open skull with delayed fontanelle closure** and **supernumerary teeth with
delayed eruption**, has **cleidocranial dysplasia** — a disorder of **intramembranous
ossification** caused by **RUNX2 (CBFA1)** mutation.

**UNDERSTAND:** RUNX2 is the master transcription factor for osteoblast differentiation,
and it matters most where bone forms **directly in membrane** rather than from a cartilage
model. The clavicle (largely membranous), the cranial vault and the teeth are therefore
the affected structures, while long bones — which ossify **endochondrally** — are
essentially normal. That single fact predicts the whole phenotype.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Clavicles absent or hypoplastic** (usually the lateral part) → hypermobile shoulders that meet in the midline; **X-ray shows the absent lateral clavicle**.
- **Skull:** brachycephaly, frontal and parietal bossing, **wide-open fontanelles and sutures persisting into adulthood**, **wormian bones**.
- **Teeth:** retained deciduous teeth, **delayed eruption of permanent teeth, supernumerary teeth**, high-arched palate.
- Also: short stature, wide pubic symphysis, coxa vara, scoliosis, hypoplastic maxilla with relative mandibular prognathism.
- **Autosomal dominant**, ~⅓ sporadic; **intelligence is normal**; management is dental and orthopaedic — life expectancy is normal.
- Compare: **osteogenesis imperfecta** (blue sclerae, fractures, dentinogenesis imperfecta, **COL1A1/2**) and **osteopetrosis** (**CLCN7**, dense brittle bones, marrow failure, cranial nerve compression).

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The "touches both shoulders together" image or description is
pathognomonic; the associated question is usually the **type of ossification affected
(membranous)** or the **gene (RUNX2)**.

**REMEMBER:** *No clavicles, no fontanelle closure, too many teeth — membrane bone fails.*

`Q9578`

---

## 115. Palliative Shunts in Cyanotic Heart Disease

**PEARL:** **Blalock–Taussig: subclavian artery → pulmonary artery** (modified uses a
Gore-Tex graft). **Waterston: ASCENDING aorta → RIGHT pulmonary artery. Potts: DESCENDING
aorta → LEFT pulmonary artery.**

**UNDERSTAND:** All three create a systemic-to-pulmonary connection to increase pulmonary
blood flow in a duct-dependent cyanotic lesion. Their differences are purely anatomical
proximity — the ascending aorta lies next to the right PA, the descending aorta next to
the left PA. Waterston and Potts are now **abandoned** because a direct aorto-pulmonary
window cannot be calibrated: too much flow causes **pulmonary hypertension and PA
distortion**. The BT shunt survives because the **small-calibre subclavian artery (or
graft) self-limits the flow**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Modified BT shunt** is the standard palliation today; it is placed **opposite the side of the aortic arch** and produces a **continuous murmur** and an absent/reduced pulse in that arm.
- Before surgery, keep the duct open with **prostaglandin E1** in any duct-dependent lesion.
- **Glenn shunt** (SVC → right PA) and **Fontan** (IVC → PA) are the staged palliations for **single-ventricle** physiology, not for TOF.
- Classic indication for palliation: **Tetralogy of Fallot** in a small/symptomatic infant unsuitable for primary repair, pulmonary atresia, tricuspid atresia.
- **Tet spells** (hypercyanotic episodes): **knee–chest position**, oxygen, morphine, IV fluids, **phenylephrine** (raises SVR), **propranolol** (relieves infundibular spasm); **avoid inotropes**, which worsen the spasm.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Pure matching, and the wrong option is usually a swap — "Potts: left
PA to **ascending** aorta". **Potts goes to the descending aorta.**

**REMEMBER:** *Waterston is up front (ascending–right); Potts is down the back
(descending–left); BT comes off the arm.*

`Q9600`

---

## 116. Intraventricular Haemorrhage: The Prognostic Complication of Prematurity

**PEARL:** Of the complications of prematurity, **severe (Grade III–IV) intraventricular
haemorrhage** correlates best with **long-term neurodevelopmental outcome** — RDS, apnoea
of prematurity and stage 1 ROP largely resolve without lasting sequelae.

**UNDERSTAND:** IVH originates in the **germinal matrix**, a highly vascular, fragile
region with immature basement membranes that involutes by 32–34 weeks — which is why the
risk is confined to the very preterm. These vessels sit in a **pressure-passive cerebral
circulation**, so any swing in blood pressure, PaCO₂ or venous pressure ruptures them.
Grade IV is a **parenchymal venous infarction** of brain tissue itself, and destroyed
brain does not regenerate — hence the durable deficit.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Papile grading:** **I** germinal matrix only; **II** intraventricular without dilatation; **III** intraventricular **with ventricular dilatation**; **IV** **intraparenchymal** haemorrhagic infarction. **III and IV predict cerebral palsy, epilepsy and cognitive impairment**; I–II usually do not.
- Timing: **>90% occur within the first 72 hours**; screen with **cranial ultrasound through the anterior fontanelle** on day 3–7 in babies <32 weeks or <1500 g, repeated at 28 days and at term.
- Risk factors: **extreme prematurity, RDS with fluctuating ventilation, pneumothorax, hypercarbia, rapid bicarbonate or volume infusion, absent antenatal steroids**.
- Prevention: **antenatal corticosteroids**, delayed cord clamping, minimal handling, avoiding hypotension and wide CO₂ swings.
- Complication: **post-haemorrhagic hydrocephalus** → serial head circumference and USS; may need a reservoir or shunt.
- Contrast **periventricular leukomalacia** — ischaemic white-matter injury, later onset, the classic substrate of **spastic diplegia**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Which complication best correlates with long-term outcome?" —
brain injury always beats lung injury in these stems.

**REMEMBER:** *Lungs recover; germinal matrix bleeds do not.*

`Q9601`

---

## 117. CSF Physiology in Hydrocephalus

**PEARL:** CSF is produced at about **20 mL/hour (~500 mL/day)** by the **choroid plexus**,
against a total volume of ~150 mL in an adult — so the whole volume **turns over 3–4 times
a day**. Production is **near-constant and largely independent of intracranial pressure**,
which is why obstruction inevitably raises pressure.

**UNDERSTAND:** Because secretion does not switch off when pressure rises (it is an active
process driven by **carbonic anhydrase and Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase** in the choroid epithelium),
any block in the circulation or absorption pathway causes relentless accumulation. That
is the entire pathophysiology of hydrocephalus — and the reason **acetazolamide** (a
carbonic anhydrase inhibitor) has a limited temporising role.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Pathway:** lateral ventricles → **foramen of Monro** → third ventricle → **aqueduct of Sylvius** → fourth ventricle → **foramina of Luschka (lateral) and Magendie (median)** → subarachnoid space → absorbed by **arachnoid granulations** into the dural venous sinuses.
- **Obstructive (non-communicating)** — block **within** the ventricular system: **aqueductal stenosis (commonest congenital cause)**, Dandy–Walker, Chiari malformation, posterior fossa tumour, colloid cyst of the third ventricle.
- **Communicating** — block at absorption: post-meningitic (especially **TB meningitis**), post-subarachnoid haemorrhage, choroid plexus papilloma (the only true **overproduction** cause).
- Infant signs: **rapidly increasing head circumference crossing centiles, tense bulging fontanelle, dilated scalp veins, split sutures, "setting sun" sign, irritability, vomiting**. In infants the open sutures allow expansion, so papilloedema is often absent.
- Treatment: **ventriculoperitoneal shunt** (complications — blockage, infection, over-drainage/subdural haematoma) or **endoscopic third ventriculostomy** for obstructive hydrocephalus.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Numeric recall (20 mL/hr, 500 mL/day, 150 mL total, 3–4 turnovers)
or a foramen-naming question along the circulation pathway.

**REMEMBER:** *Half a litre a day into a 150 mL space — the tap never turns off.*

`Q9571`

---

## 118. Congenital Hypothyroidism: Goitre or No Goitre

**PEARL:** **Thyroid DYSGENESIS is the commonest cause of congenital hypothyroidism but
NEVER produces a goitre** — there is no thyroid tissue to enlarge. A **palpable goitre**
means **dyshormonogenesis, maternal antithyroid drugs, or iodine deficiency**.

**UNDERSTAND:** A goitre is TSH-driven hypertrophy of **existing** thyroid tissue. If the
gland is absent, hypoplastic or ectopic (dysgenesis), high TSH has nothing to act on.
If the gland is present but **cannot make hormone** — an enzyme block, a drug blocking
the enzyme, or absent substrate (iodine) — TSH drives it into a goitre. So the presence
of a goitre tells you the defect is **functional, not anatomical**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Without goitre:** **thyroid dysgenesis (agenesis, hypoplasia, ectopic — 80–85%, commonest overall)**, maternal **TSH-receptor blocking antibodies**, **central (hypopituitary) hypothyroidism** (low TSH with low T4).
- **With goitre:** **dyshormonogenesis** (~10–15%, autosomal recessive — TPO defect commonest; **Pendred syndrome** = organification defect + **sensorineural deafness**), **maternal antithyroid drugs** crossing the placenta (transient), **iodine deficiency or excess (Wolff–Chaikoff)**.
- Clinical picture is **subtle at birth** (maternal T4 crosses the placenta) — **prolonged jaundice, constipation/delayed meconium, poor feeding, hypotonia, large fontanelle, umbilical hernia, macroglossia, hoarse cry, coarse facies, hypothermia**.
- **Universal newborn screening at 48–72 hours (TSH on heel-prick)** exists because treatment delay costs IQ points; sampling before 48 hours gives false positives from the physiological TSH surge.
- **Treat immediately with levothyroxine 10–15 µg/kg/day**, ideally within 2 weeks — the outcome is normal development if started early, irreversible intellectual disability if not.
- **Radionuclide scan and ultrasound** determine the cause (absent uptake = dysgenesis; increased uptake with goitre = dyshormonogenesis; **perchlorate discharge test** for organification defects), but must never delay treatment.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "All can present with congenital hypothyroidism AND a goitre EXCEPT"
— the answer is **dysgenesis**, the commonest cause, which is exactly why it looks like
the wrong answer.

**REMEMBER:** *No gland, no goitre — a goitre means the gland is there but blocked.*

`Q10843`

---

## 119. Infantile Hypertrophic Pyloric Stenosis

**PEARL:** 3–6-week-old, **non-bilious projectile vomiting**, a palpable **olive** and a
**"target sign"** on ultrasound = **hypertrophic pyloric stenosis**, with **hypochloraemic,
hypokalaemic METABOLIC ALKALOSIS and PARADOXICAL ACIDURIA**. **Erythromycin** exposure
(pre- or postnatal) is a recognised risk factor.

**UNDERSTAND:** The paradoxical aciduria is the elegant part. Losing gastric HCl causes
alkalosis with hypochloraemia; the resulting volume depletion drives **aldosterone**,
which reabsorbs Na⁺ in exchange for **K⁺ and then H⁺**. Once potassium is depleted, the
kidney has no choice but to excrete **H⁺** to retain sodium — so the urine turns **acidic
in an alkalotic patient**. Erythromycin is implicated because it is a **motilin receptor
agonist**, driving intense antral and pyloric contraction.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Epidemiology: **firstborn males**, 2–8 weeks, family history; the baby is **hungry and feeds avidly after vomiting** (distinguishing it from sepsis or raised ICP).
- Examination: visible **left-to-right gastric peristalsis**, palpable olive in the right upper quadrant after a test feed.
- **Ultrasound is diagnostic:** **pyloric muscle thickness >3 mm, channel length >15–17 mm** ("target"/"doughnut" in cross section, "cervix sign" longitudinally).
- **This is a MEDICAL emergency, not a surgical one — never operate until the electrolytes and alkalosis are corrected** (alkalosis causes postoperative apnoea). Resuscitate with **0.9% saline + potassium**, aim chloride >100 mmol/L and bicarbonate <30.
- Definitive treatment: **Ramstedt pyloromyotomy** (the mucosa is deliberately left intact); feeds within hours.
- Distinguish from **duodenal atresia** (bilious vomiting, **double bubble**, Down syndrome), **malrotation with volvulus** (bilious vomiting — a surgical emergency), and **GERD** (non-projectile, no olive, no alkalosis).

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The stem hands you all four clues — age, projectile non-bilious
vomiting, target sign, low potassium with acidic urine. The question is often the **drug**
(erythromycin) or the **first step** (fluids and electrolytes, not surgery).

**REMEMBER:** *Vomiting acid, alkalotic blood, acid urine — fix the salt before the scalpel.*

`Q10859`

---

## 120. The Five Stages of Grief

**PEARL:** **Kübler-Ross: Denial → Anger → Bargaining → DEPRESSION → Acceptance.**
Withdrawal, sleep disturbance, hopelessness and suicidal ideation = the **fourth stage,
depression**.

**UNDERSTAND:** The stages describe a **process, not a sequence with a timetable** —
Kübler-Ross herself stressed that patients skip stages, return to earlier ones, or
experience several at once. The exam asks the ordinal position, but the clinical point is
different: distinguishing normal anticipatory grief from **major depressive disorder**,
which needs treatment.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Normal grief:** preserved self-esteem, sadness in waves triggered by reminders, wishes to die "to be with" the deceased rather than active suicidal planning, illusions or fleeting hallucinations of the deceased **with intact reality testing**; usually improves over weeks to months.
- **Major depression complicating grief:** **pervasive anhedonia, worthlessness and guilt unrelated to the loss, psychomotor retardation, active suicidal ideation, mood-congruent psychosis** → treat with **antidepressants, psychotherapy, or ECT** if severe or suicidal.
- **Prolonged grief disorder** (DSM-5-TR): intense yearning/preoccupation with the deceased persisting **>12 months in adults (6 months in children)** with functional impairment.
- **DSM-5 removed the "bereavement exclusion"** — a depressive episode may be diagnosed within 2 weeks of a loss.
- Breaking bad news: **SPIKES** — Setting, Perception, Invitation, Knowledge, Emotions with empathy, Strategy/Summary.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Simple stage-matching, but read for the **suicidal ideation**, which
both fixes the stage as depression and flags the need for active treatment.

**REMEMBER:** *"DABDA" — Denial, Anger, Bargaining, Depression, Acceptance.*

`Q10842`

---

## 121. Down Syndrome: Associations and the Non-Association

**PEARL:** Down syndrome is associated with **congenital heart disease, GI atresias,
chronic serous otitis media with CONDUCTIVE hearing loss, hypothyroidism, short stature,
atlantoaxial instability, leukaemia and early Alzheimer disease**. **CAUDAL REGRESSION
SYNDROME is NOT a feature — it belongs to the INFANT OF A DIABETIC MOTHER.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Every Down association traces to **trisomy 21 gene dosage**: the **AVSD**
from endocardial cushion genes, **Alzheimer disease in the 30s–40s** from the **APP gene on
chromosome 21**, and the leukaemia risk from **GATA1/RUNX1**. Caudal regression, by
contrast, is a **teratogenic** lesion of the caudal mesoderm caused by **maternal
hyperglycaemia in the first weeks of gestation** — a different mechanism entirely, and one
that would appear regardless of karyotype.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Cardiac (~40–50%): AVSD (endocardial cushion defect) is the most characteristic**; VSD is the commonest overall. **Eisenmenger** develops early — refer for echocardiography **in every newborn with Down syndrome**.
- **GI: duodenal atresia (double bubble), annular pancreas, Hirschsprung disease, TOF, imperforate anus**; also **coeliac disease**.
- **>50% have chronic serous otitis media → conductive hearing loss**; also refractive errors, **cataract, keratoconus, Brushfield spots**; **obstructive sleep apnoea** in ~50%.
- **Hypothyroidism** (congenital and acquired autoimmune) — screen annually; also type 1 diabetes.
- **Atlantoaxial instability (~15%)** — screen before general anaesthesia or sports participation; can cause cord compression.
- **Haematology: transient abnormal myelopoiesis in the newborn** (usually resolves), then a **10–20× risk of leukaemia — AML (especially megakaryoblastic, M7) in under-3s and ALL later**.
- **Cytogenetics: nondisjunction trisomy 21 (95%, risk rises with MATERNAL AGE), Robertsonian translocation (4% — karyotype the PARENTS; recurrence risk is high and independent of age; a 14;21 carrier mother has ~10–15% risk), mosaicism (1%, milder).**
- Antenatal screening: **first-trimester — increased NT, ↑β-hCG, ↓PAPP-A**; **second-trimester quadruple test — ↓AFP, ↓oestriol, ↑hCG, ↑inhibin A**; **NIPT (cell-free fetal DNA)** for high sensitivity, confirmed by CVS or amniocentesis.
- **Infant of a diabetic mother:** **caudal regression (most characteristic)**, cardiac defects (**transposition, VSD, hypertrophic cardiomyopathy**), neural tube defects, **macrosomia, hypoglycaemia, hypocalcaemia, polycythaemia, hyperbilirubinaemia, RDS**, small left colon syndrome.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Which is NOT associated?" — the odd one out belongs to a **different
syndrome**, most often the diabetic embryopathy. Learn the two lists side by side.

**REMEMBER:** *Trisomy 21 is a gene-dosage disease; caudal regression is a sugar disease.*

`Q10952`

---

## 122. Estimating FiO₂ from Nasal Cannula Flow

**PEARL:** **FiO₂ ≈ 21% + (4 × flow in L/min)** for a nasal cannula. At **3 L/min → about
30–33%**.

**UNDERSTAND:** Each litre per minute of oxygen adds roughly **4 percentage points** because
it partially fills the **nasopharyngeal reservoir** between breaths, and that stored oxygen
is then diluted by the room air the patient entrains around it. The relationship holds only
up to about **6 L/min**: beyond that the anatomical reservoir is already full, so extra flow
adds nothing but dries and irritates the mucosa. This is also why a cannula is a
**variable-performance (low-flow) device** — the actual FiO₂ falls as the patient's minute
ventilation rises.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Low-flow (variable performance): nasal cannula (24–44%), simple face mask (40–60%), partial rebreathing and non-rebreathing masks (60–90%+), face tent.** Delivered FiO₂ depends on the patient's respiratory pattern.
- **High-flow (fixed performance): VENTURI mask (24–60%, precise), high-flow nasal cannula (up to 60 L/min and ~100%, humidified, provides some PEEP and washes out nasopharyngeal dead space), mechanical ventilation.**
- Flows **above 4 L/min by nasal cannula should be humidified**; nasal cannulae are well tolerated and allow eating and speaking — the practical reason they are first choice in children.
- **Paediatric caution:** in **duct-dependent congenital heart disease**, high FiO₂ closes the ductus — give oxygen judiciously and start **prostaglandin E1**. In **preterm neonates**, target **SpO₂ 90–95%** to limit **retinopathy of prematurity and bronchopulmonary dysplasia**.
- Escalation in paediatric respiratory failure: **nasal cannula → face mask → non-rebreathing mask → HFNC/CPAP → intubation**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** A one-line calculation. **21 + 4 × flow.** Options are usually
adjacent (25/30/45/60), so the arithmetic must be exact.

**REMEMBER:** *Four per litre, on top of twenty-one.*

`Q10953`

---

## 123. Criteria for Perinatal Asphyxia

**PEARL:** All four essential criteria must be present: **umbilical ARTERIAL pH <7.0
(prolonged metabolic or mixed acidaemia), an APGAR score of 0–3 persisting BEYOND 5 minutes,
NEUROLOGICAL manifestations in the immediate neonatal period (HYPOTONIA, seizures, coma or
HIE), and MULTI-ORGAN dysfunction.** A **cord pH of 7.2 or an Apgar of 4–7 does NOT
qualify.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The criteria exist to **restrict the diagnosis to genuine intrapartum
hypoxic-ischaemic injury**, because "birth asphyxia" is the most litigated label in
obstetrics. Each element rules out a mimic: the **pH proves a real metabolic insult**, the
**prolonged low Apgar proves it was severe and sustained**, the **neurological signs prove
the brain was affected**, and the **multi-organ involvement proves it was a global perfusion
failure** rather than an isolated neurological disorder. A low Apgar alone can be caused by
prematurity, sedation, infection or a congenital anomaly — which is why it is never
sufficient by itself.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Apgar (at 1 and 5 minutes): Appearance, Pulse, Grimace, Activity, Respiration**, 0–2 each. **It guides nothing in resuscitation — never delay resuscitation to score it** — and is a poor predictor of long-term outcome on its own.
- **Hypoxic-ischaemic encephalopathy (SARNAT staging): I — hyperalert, normal tone, no seizures, excellent prognosis; II — lethargy, hypotonia, SEIZURES, intermediate; III — coma, flaccidity, absent reflexes, poor prognosis.**
- **THERAPEUTIC HYPOTHERMIA (33–34 °C for 72 hours, started WITHIN 6 HOURS of birth) is the only intervention proven to improve death or disability** in moderate-to-severe HIE in babies **≥36 weeks**. Cool, then rewarm slowly.
- **Multi-organ effects: HIE and seizures, acute tubular necrosis, myocardial dysfunction with hypotension, necrotising enterocolitis, DIC, meconium aspiration, persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn, and SIADH.**
- Resuscitation: the **golden minute** — dry, stimulate and warm; if not breathing or gasping, **positive pressure ventilation with ROOM AIR in term infants (21%; use 21–30% in preterms)**; chest compressions at **3:1** if the heart rate remains <60 after 30 seconds of effective ventilation; **adrenaline** if it stays <60. **Routine suctioning of vigorous meconium-stained babies is NOT recommended.**
- **Delayed cord clamping (30–60 seconds)** improves iron stores and outcomes in babies who do not need resuscitation.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Options offer near-miss values (**pH 7.2 instead of 7.0; Apgar 4–7
instead of 0–3**). Match against the exact criteria; **hypotonia** is the one genuinely
listed neurological sign among the choices.

**REMEMBER:** *pH under 7, Apgar under 4 past 5 minutes, an abnormal brain and a failing
body — all four, or it is not asphyxia.*

`Q11018`

---

## 124. McCune–Albright Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Peripheral (GnRH-INDEPENDENT) precocious puberty + segmental CAFÉ-AU-LAIT
macules with IRREGULAR ("coast of MAINE") borders + POLYOSTOTIC FIBROUS DYSPLASIA =
McCUNE–ALBRIGHT SYNDROME**, from a **post-zygotic activating (mosaic) mutation in GNAS
(Gsα)**. **Basal LH is LOW and does NOT rise on GnRH stimulation, while oestradiol is very
high.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The mutation locks **Gsα in the "on" state**, so any cell using a
**Gs-coupled receptor secretes autonomously without its trophic hormone** — ovarian follicles
make oestrogen without LH, thyrocytes make thyroxine without TSH, somatotrophs make growth
hormone, adrenal cells make cortisol. Because the autonomous oestrogen **suppresses the
pituitary by negative feedback**, LH is low and unresponsive — the exact opposite of
**central** precocious puberty, where the axis is switched on and GnRH stimulation produces
a brisk LH rise. The mutation is **post-zygotic and mosaic**, so the lesions are
**SEGMENTAL and stop abruptly at the midline** — hence the irregular café-au-lait borders.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Classic triad: precocious puberty (recurrent unilateral autonomous ovarian CYSTS, painless vaginal bleeding), café-au-lait macules, and fibrous dysplasia (bone pain, deformity, pathological fracture, cranial nerve compression — "SHEPHERD'S CROOK" deformity of the femur).** Other endocrinopathies: **hyperthyroidism, acromegaly, Cushing syndrome, hyperprolactinaemia and hypophosphataemic rickets (FGF23).**
- **Café-au-lait borders: McCune–Albright = irregular, "COAST OF MAINE"; NEUROFIBROMATOSIS 1 = smooth, "COAST OF CALIFORNIA".**
- **Treatment: AROMATASE INHIBITORS (letrozole) or TAMOXIFEN to block peripheral oestrogen action — GnRH analogues do NOT work** (there is no GnRH drive to suppress), unless secondary central puberty develops later.
- **Central (true) precocious puberty: pubertal LH response to GnRH stimulation; causes — idiopathic (commonest, especially in girls), CNS lesions (hypothalamic HAMARTOMA — classically with gelastic seizures), hydrocephalus, cranial irradiation. Treat with a GnRH ANALOGUE (leuprolide).**
- **Other causes of peripheral precocity: congenital adrenal hyperplasia (VIRILISATION, raised 17-OHP), adrenal or gonadal tumours, exogenous steroids, primary HYPOTHYROIDISM (Van Wyk–Grumbach — precocity WITH delayed bone age), and PEUTZ–JEGHERS syndrome (Sertoli cell tumours).**
- **Precocious puberty = secondary sexual characteristics before 8 years in girls, 9 years in boys.** Investigate with **bone age (ADVANCED in true precocity), LH/FSH ± GnRH stimulation, oestradiol/testosterone, pelvic ultrasound and MRI brain.**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The flat LH response to GnRH plus high oestradiol proves the source is
**peripheral**; the skin and bone findings then name the syndrome.

**REMEMBER:** *The ovary works on its own, so the pituitary switches off — and the skin spots
have a ragged coastline.*

`Q11825`

---

## 125. Wiskott–Aldrich Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Recurrent infections + ECZEMA + THROMBOCYTOPENIA WITH ABNORMALLY SMALL PLATELETS
(microthrombocytopenia), presenting with bloody diarrhoea and bruising in an infant BOY =
WISKOTT–ALDRICH SYNDROME** — X-linked recessive, from mutations in **WASP (Xp11.23)**.

**UNDERSTAND:** WASP regulates **ACTIN CYTOSKELETON reorganisation** in all haematopoietic
cells. Losing it explains every feature at once: **platelets cannot form properly and are
small and rapidly destroyed** (bleeding); **T cells cannot form the immunological synapse or
migrate** (recurrent infections and progressive T-cell loss); and disordered immune
regulation produces **eczema, autoimmunity and lymphoma risk**. One structural protein, three
clinical problems — hence the classic triad.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Mnemonic: "WATER" — Wiskott–Aldrich: Thrombocytopenia, Eczema, Recurrent infections.**
- **Immunology: LOW IgM, HIGH IgA and IgE, normal or low IgG; poor antibody responses to POLYSACCHARIDE antigens (hence encapsulated organisms); progressive lymphopenia.** **Small platelet volume (low MPV) is the key laboratory clue** — in ITP the platelets are normal or large.
- **Complications: bleeding (including intracranial), infection, AUTOIMMUNE disease (haemolytic anaemia, vasculitis, IBD) and B-CELL LYMPHOMA.**
- **Treatment: HAEMATOPOIETIC STEM CELL TRANSPLANTATION is the only cure** (gene therapy in trials); IVIG, antimicrobial prophylaxis and **splenectomy** (raises the platelet count but adds sepsis risk). **Platelet transfusion for bleeding; avoid aspirin.**
- **Contrast the other platelet disorders: BERNARD–SOULIER — GIANT platelets, defective GPIb (adhesion), thrombocytopenia; GLANZMANN thrombasthenia — normal-sized platelets, normal count, defective GPIIb/IIIa (aggregation), absent aggregation to ALL agonists except RISTOCETIN; ITP — normal-sized or large platelets, isolated thrombocytopenia, otherwise well child after a viral illness.**
- **Other X-linked immunodeficiencies to keep separate: BRUTON agammaglobulinaemia (BTK — absent B cells, no tonsils/nodes, infections after 6 months); X-linked hyper-IgM (CD40L); chronic granulomatous disease (NADPH oxidase — catalase-positive organisms, abnormal DHR/NBT test); SCID (γc chain or ADA).**

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The **small platelet size** is the discriminating laboratory detail —
it separates WAS from ITP and from Bernard–Soulier.

**REMEMBER:** *Eczema, bleeding and infections in a boy — and the platelets are tiny.*

`Q11838`

---

## 126. Grading Variable Decelerations

**PEARL:** **SEVERE variable deceleration = FHR falls BELOW 70 bpm AND lasts at least 60 seconds.** Both the depth and the duration must be met.

**UNDERSTAND:** Variable decelerations are caused by **cord compression**. Compressing the **thin-walled umbilical VEIN first** reduces venous return and triggers a reflex tachycardia; further compression occludes the **artery**, causing a sharp baroreceptor-mediated bradycardia. The **deeper and longer** the deceleration, the more complete and prolonged the arterial occlusion — hence severity is defined by exactly those two variables.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Definition of a variable deceleration: **abrupt** fall of **≥ 15 bpm below baseline**, lasting **≥ 15 seconds but < 2 minutes**, variable in timing relative to contractions.
- **Mild:** > 80 bpm at any duration; or < 30 s at any depth; or 70–80 bpm for 60 s. **Moderate:** > 70 bpm for 30–60 s; or 70–80 bpm for > 60 s. **Severe:** **< 70 bpm for ≥ 60 s**.
- Management: **change maternal position (left lateral), stop oxytocin, give oxygen and IV fluids**; consider **amnioinfusion** for recurrent variables; deliver if persistent with loss of variability.
- Contrast the other patterns: **early decelerations** — mirror the contraction, caused by **head compression**, benign; **late decelerations** — begin after the peak, caused by **uteroplacental insufficiency**, always concerning; **sinusoidal** — fetal anaemia.
- The most important reassuring feature on any trace is **good beat-to-beat variability**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** All four options give a duration of 60 seconds, so the answer turns entirely on the **rate**: below 70.

**REMEMBER:** *Seventy for sixty is severe.*

`MC9536`

---

## 127. Type II Pneumocytes and Respiratory Distress Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Type II pneumocytes** are the cells lost or immature in **respiratory distress syndrome** — they secrete **surfactant** and are also the **stem cells for alveolar repair**.

**UNDERSTAND:** Type I pneumocytes are terminally differentiated flat gas-exchange cells that **cannot divide**. When alveolar epithelium is injured, only the **cuboidal type II cell can proliferate and then differentiate into type I cells**. So the same cell that prevents alveolar collapse also rebuilds the alveolus — which is why its loss produces both the acute collapse of RDS and the delayed repair of ARDS.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Type I** — thin, flat, **95% of alveolar surface area**, joined by tight junctions, forming the blood–gas barrier. **Type II** — rounded, granular, microvilli, **lamellar bodies containing surfactant**, ~5% of surface but ~60% of alveolar cells.
- **Surfactant is dipalmitoyl phosphatidylcholine (lecithin)**; production begins ~24 weeks and surges after **34–35 weeks**. Assessed by the **lecithin:sphingomyelin ratio ≥ 2** and the presence of **phosphatidylglycerol**.
- **Antenatal corticosteroids (betamethasone/dexamethasone) between 24 and 34 weeks** accelerate type II cell maturation — the single most effective intervention for RDS.
- RDS radiology: **fine reticulogranular "ground-glass" lungs with air bronchograms and low volumes**.
- Treatment: **surfactant replacement plus CPAP/ventilation**. Complications: **pneumothorax, PDA, bronchopulmonary dysplasia, IVH**.
- Type II cells also **transport sodium and clear alveolar fluid** at birth — impaired in **transient tachypnoea of the newborn**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Labelled histology image. Type II are the **rounded cells at alveolar corners**; type I are the **flat lining cells**.

**REMEMBER:** *The cell that makes surfactant is also the cell that rebuilds the alveolus.*

`MF2557`

---

## 128. Congenital Infections — Risk of Perinatal Transmission

**PEARL:** **Rubella has the LOWEST risk of perinatal (intrapartum) transmission** — it is an **intrauterine, first-trimester** infection. **CMV is the commonest congenital infection**; **hepatitis B and HSV** carry high perinatal transmission risk.

**UNDERSTAND:** "Congenital" and "perinatal" are different routes. Rubella damages by **transplacental infection during organogenesis**, so its risk is defined by **gestational age at maternal infection** (highest < 12 weeks) — by the time of delivery, transmission is essentially irrelevant. Hepatitis B and HSV, by contrast, are transmitted mainly by **contact with maternal blood and genital secretions during delivery**, which is why intrapartum measures prevent them.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Congenital rubella triad: sensorineural deafness (commonest), cataract, congenital heart disease (PDA, peripheral pulmonary stenosis)**; plus "**blueberry muffin**" rash and **salt-and-pepper retinopathy**.
- **CMV** — commonest congenital infection; only **~10% symptomatic at birth**; features **hepatosplenomegaly, petechiae, jaundice, chorioretinitis, microcephaly and PERIVENTRICULAR calcification**. **Sensorineural hearing loss** is the key late sequel — in **~50% of symptomatic** but only **~13.5% of asymptomatic** infants. **Diagnosis: PCR on urine, saliva or blood WITHIN THE FIRST 2–3 WEEKS** (after 3 weeks, a positive result cannot distinguish congenital from postnatally acquired infection). Treat symptomatic disease with **valganciclovir**.
- **Toxoplasma** — **hydrocephalus, DIFFUSE intracranial calcification, chorioretinitis**.
- **Hepatitis B** — prevented by **HBIG plus vaccine within 12 hours of birth**; **breastfeeding is then permitted**.
- **HSV** — three patterns: **skin-eye-mouth, CNS (encephalitis), disseminated**; caesarean delivery if active genital lesions.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Read whether the question says **congenital/intrauterine** or **perinatal/intrapartum** — rubella and CMV swap places between the two.

**REMEMBER:** *Rubella strikes in the first trimester, hepatitis B at delivery.*

`MF9137, MG1226`

---

## 129. Neonatal Thermal Bands

**PEARL:** Normal axillary temperature is **36.5–37.5 °C**. **COLD STRESS = 36.0–36.4 °C**; **moderate hypothermia 32–35.9 °C**; **severe hypothermia < 32 °C**; hyperthermia **> 37.5 °C**.

**UNDERSTAND:** Neonates lose heat rapidly because of a **high surface area to volume ratio, thin skin, little subcutaneous fat, and an inability to shiver**. They generate heat only by **non-shivering thermogenesis in brown adipose tissue**, which consumes oxygen and glucose — so hypothermia rapidly causes **hypoglycaemia, hypoxia, metabolic acidosis and increased mortality**. Cold stress is defined as a separate, mild band precisely because intervention at that stage prevents this cascade.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Measure in the **axilla for at least 3 minutes**.
- Heat is lost by **conduction, convection, radiation and evaporation** — evaporation is the largest immediate loss at birth, hence **immediate drying and removing the wet linen**.
- **Warm chain** measures: warm delivery room (25–28 °C), immediate drying, **skin-to-skin contact**, early breastfeeding, delayed bathing, **Kangaroo Mother Care**, warm transport.
- **KMC** is indicated for stable low-birth-weight babies and reduces mortality, infection and hypothermia.
- **Peripheral IV lines can take a maximum of 12.5% dextrose** — anything stronger is too acidic/hyperosmolar and causes **thrombophlebitis**; **25% dextrose needs a central line**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The bands are asked as pure recall, and cold stress (**36.0–36.4**) is easily confused with the lower end of normal.

**REMEMBER:** *Below 36.5 is abnormal; 36.0 to 36.4 is the warning band.*

`MA4262, MC0775`

---

## 130. Managing Dehydration by IMNCI Plan

**PEARL:** **Restless/irritable + drinks eagerly + sunken eyes + skin pinch returning slowly (but < 2 s) = SOME dehydration → Plan B: ORS plus zinc.** Not IV fluids, and not just home advice.

**UNDERSTAND:** The IMNCI plans are graded by **how much circulating volume is lost**, judged from four bedside signs. **"Some" dehydration (3–9% loss)** means the child is still **conscious and able to drink**, so the gut can be used — oral rehydration is as effective as IV and far safer. IV fluid is reserved for **severe dehydration (> 9%)**, where the child is **lethargic and cannot drink**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Plan A (no dehydration)**: extra fluids at home, **zinc for 14 days**, continued feeding, and counselling on **danger signs**.
- **Plan B (some dehydration)**: **ORS 75 mL/kg over 4 hours** at the facility, then reassess; plus **zinc**.
- **Plan C (severe dehydration)**: **IV Ringer's lactate 100 mL/kg** — in infants < 12 months, **30 mL/kg over 1 hour then 70 mL/kg over 5 hours**; in older children, **30 mL/kg over 30 minutes then 70 mL/kg over 2½ hours**.
- **Zinc: 10 mg/day under 6 months, 20 mg/day over 6 months, for 14 days** — reduces duration, severity and recurrence.
- **Low-osmolarity ORS (245 mOsm/L)**: sodium 75, glucose 75, potassium 20, citrate 10 mmol/L.
- **In SEVERE ACUTE MALNUTRITION the rules change: use ReSoMal (low sodium, high potassium) 5 mL/kg every 30 minutes for the first 2 hours**, then 5–10 mL/kg in alternate hours; **avoid IV fluids unless in shock** (risk of heart failure), and **do not continue aggressive hydration once dehydration has resolved**. Hypoglycaemia in SAM: **10% glucose 50 mL orally if conscious; 10% glucose 5 mL/kg IV if unconscious** (never 50% dextrose).

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Match the signs to the plan. The trap is over-treating "some" dehydration with IV fluids, or applying standard ORS rules to a severely malnourished child.

**REMEMBER:** *If the child can drink eagerly, use the mouth.*

`MC0768, MA0492`

---

## 131. MUAC and Shakir's Tape

**PEARL:** **Shakir's tape measures MID-UPPER ARM CIRCUMFERENCE** to screen for acute malnutrition in **children 6–59 months** by frontline workers. **RED < 11.5 cm = severe acute malnutrition; YELLOW 11.5–12.5 cm = moderate; GREEN > 12.5 cm = normal.**
