**UNDERSTAND:** MUAC works as a screening tool because **between 1 and 5 years the mid-arm circumference is almost constant (~16 cm) in healthy children** — so a single cut-off applies across the whole age range **without needing to know the child's age, weight or height**. That age-independence is exactly what makes it usable by a village health worker with nothing but a strip of tape.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Measured on the **LEFT arm**, at the **midpoint between the acromion and olecranon**, with the arm hanging relaxed (the midpoint is marked with the elbow flexed to 90°).
- **SAM in 6–59 months is diagnosed by ANY ONE of: weight-for-height < −3 SD, MUAC < 11.5 cm, or BILATERAL PITTING OEDEMA.**
- MUAC predicts **mortality risk better than weight-for-height**, which is why it drives admission to nutrition programmes.
- **Bangle test** and the **Shakir tape** are the field equivalents.
- Distinguish **wasting (acute — low weight-for-height)** from **stunting (chronic — low height-for-age)** and **underweight (composite — low weight-for-age)**.
- Ten steps of SAM management begin with treating/preventing **hypoglycaemia, hypothermia, dehydration, electrolyte imbalance and infection**, then **micronutrients**, then **cautious then catch-up feeding**, then stimulation and follow-up.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The multi-statement version tests the **name (Shakir, not Shelter)** and the **user (frontline field workers)**; the 13.5–14.5 cm figure is a fabricated distractor.

**REMEMBER:** *Under 11.5 is red and severe.*

`MF7403`

---

## 132. Biotin Deficiency and Valproate

**PEARL:** **Alopecia with "intermittent constrictions" of the hair shaft + periorificial scaly dermatitis in a child on long-term VALPROATE = biotin (vitamin B7 / vitamin H) deficiency.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Valproate is metabolised as a **branched-chain fatty acid and depletes carnitine and biotinidase activity**, reducing free biotin. Biotin is the cofactor for the **carboxylases**, and the tissues that fail first are those with the **highest turnover — hair matrix and skin** — producing the alopecia and dermatitis. The hair shaft constrictions correspond to intermittent periods of deficiency.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Other features: **periorificial dermatitis, atrophic glossitis, conjunctivitis, paraesthesia, hypotonia, lethargy, ataxia, seizures, lactic acidosis and organic aciduria**.
- Other causes of biotin deficiency: **raw egg white (avidin), prolonged TPN, other anticonvulsants (phenytoin, carbamazepine)**, and **BIOTINIDASE DEFICIENCY** — a treatable, newborn-screened cause of **seizures, hypotonia, alopecia and sensorineural deafness**, corrected by oral biotin.
- Valproate's other toxicities: **hepatotoxicity (highest risk under 2 years and in mitochondrial disease/POLG mutations), hyperammonaemic encephalopathy, pancreatitis, weight gain, tremor, thrombocytopenia** and **neural tube defects with reduced IQ if used in pregnancy**.
- Related water-soluble vitamin deficiencies: **B1 beriberi/Wernicke; B2 cheilosis and magenta tongue; B3 pellagra; B5 burning feet; B6 microcytic anaemia and convulsions (isoniazid); B9/B12 megaloblastic anaemia**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Vitamin H" is deliberately used instead of biotin or B7 in the options — recognise the synonym.

**REMEMBER:** *Valproate strips the biotin, and the hair goes first.*

`MC1806`

---

## 133. Immune Reconstitution Inflammatory Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Fever and paradoxical WORSENING of symptoms within the first weeks of starting antiretroviral therapy, in a patient with a low CD4 count = IRIS.**

**UNDERSTAND:** ART does not cause a new infection — it **restores the immune response to an infection that was already there**. While CD4 counts were very low, subclinical organisms (mycobacteria, cryptococcus, CMV) provoked no inflammation. As immunity recovers, the newly competent immune system attacks them vigorously, and it is the **inflammation, not the organism**, that makes the patient worse.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Two forms: **paradoxical** (a known, treated infection flares) and **unmasking** (a previously undiagnosed infection becomes apparent).
- Risk factors: **CD4 < 100/µL at initiation, a high viral load, a rapid fall in viral load, and starting ART soon after treating an opportunistic infection**.
- Commonest associations: **tuberculosis, Cryptococcus, MAC, CMV retinitis, PML, hepatitis B/C, Kaposi sarcoma**.
- Management: **CONTINUE ART**, treat the underlying infection, and give **corticosteroids** for severe inflammation (established benefit in TB-IRIS).
- Prevention: treat the opportunistic infection first and **delay ART** — usually 2 weeks for most infections, but **4–6 weeks in cryptococcal meningitis** (early ART increases mortality there).
- Infant HIV diagnosis: **HIV DNA/total nucleic acid PCR on a DRIED BLOOD SPOT** — maternal antibody persists up to 18 months, so **antibody tests are useless in infancy**; confirm every positive on a second sample.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The timing ("first week of treatment") plus the low CD4 count is the whole question. The distractor options are invented syndrome names.

**REMEMBER:** *The immune system wakes up and attacks what was already there.*

`MB5796, MF5385`

---

## 134. Bone Age Assessment

**PEARL:** **X-ray of the LEFT HAND AND WRIST is the standard for assessing skeletal (bone) age** — used in disputes over a child's chronological age and in evaluating short stature.

**UNDERSTAND:** The hand and wrist contain **around 30 separate ossification centres maturing in a predictable sequence across the whole of childhood**, all captured in **one low-dose radiograph of a non-dominant, easily positioned part**. No other region offers that density of sequential maturation markers, which is why it is the universal standard.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Methods: **Greulich and Pyle atlas** (compare with a standard atlas), **Tanner–Whitehouse (TW2/TW3) scoring**, and automated **BoneXpert** software.
- Bone age reflects **physiological rather than chronological** age; the **elbow is used in adolescents (13–16 years)** and the **knee in infants**, when hand centres are less informative.
- **Delayed bone age**: constitutional delay of growth and puberty, **hypothyroidism, growth hormone deficiency, chronic illness, malnutrition**. **Advanced bone age**: **precocious puberty, congenital adrenal hyperplasia, hyperthyroidism, obesity**.
- **Bone age equals height age in familial short stature; bone age is delayed and matches height age in constitutional delay** — the classic discriminator.
- Related growth facts: **birth weight doubles at 5 months, triples at 1 year, quadruples at 2 years and quintuples at 3 years**; a child speaking **2–3 word sentences and stacking a tower of 6 cubes is 2 years old**.
- **Head control:** marked head lag at 2 months, partial at 3 months, **no head lag when pulled to sit by 4 months**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The forensic/sport-dispute stem always points to the **hand and wrist**. Milestones are used in the stem to establish the child's age before a weight calculation.

**REMEMBER:** *Thirty ossification centres in one small film.*

`MC1583, MA4766, MA4743`

---

## 135. Coagulation Factor Eponyms

**PEARL:** **Factor V — parahaemophilia (Owren disease); Factor VIII — haemophilia A (classic); Factor IX — haemophilia B (Christmas disease); Factor X — Stuart–Prower; Factor XI — HAEMOPHILIA C.**

**UNDERSTAND:** The eponyms track the **order of discovery, each named after the index patient or family**, not after any biological logic — so there is no shortcut. What *is* logical is the inheritance: **haemophilia A and B are X-linked** (both genes lie on the X chromosome), while **haemophilia C (factor XI) is autosomal recessive**, which is why it affects both sexes and clusters in **Ashkenazi Jews**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Haemophilia C bleeds mildly and unpredictably** — often only after **surgery or dental extraction**, and the bleeding severity correlates poorly with the factor level (unlike A and B).
- All three prolong the **aPTT with a normal PT and normal bleeding time**.
- Treatment: **factor VIII concentrate or desmopressin for mild haemophilia A**; **factor IX concentrate for B**; **fresh frozen plasma or factor XI concentrate for C** (desmopressin does not work in B or C).
- **Emicizumab** (bispecific antibody mimicking factor VIII) has transformed prophylaxis in haemophilia A, including in patients with **inhibitors**.
- Factor **XIII deficiency** is the exception — **normal PT, aPTT and BT**, with **delayed umbilical stump bleeding and poor wound healing**; diagnosed by **urea clot solubility**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Pure matching, and the options are deliberately scrambled. Anchor **Christmas = IX** and **Stuart–Prower = X**, then the rest follow.

**REMEMBER:** *Christmas is the ninth; Stuart-Prower is the tenth.*

`MC3264`

---

## 136. Tyrosinaemia Type II (Richner–Hanhart)

**PEARL:** **Painful hyperkeratotic plaques on palms and soles + herpetiform CORNEAL ULCERS + developmental delay = tyrosinaemia type II** (**tyrosine aminotransferase** deficiency), also called **oculocutaneous tyrosinaemia**.

**UNDERSTAND:** Excess tyrosine **crystallises within cells**, and the crystals damage the tissues where they concentrate — **corneal epithelium and the thick keratin of palms and soles**. The lesions therefore mimic herpes on the cornea but are **sterile, bilateral and resistant to antivirals**, which is the diagnostic clue: they respond only to **dietary tyrosine restriction**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Onset between **2 and 4 years**; treat with a **diet low in tyrosine and phenylalanine**, aiming for plasma tyrosine **< 500 µmol/L** — skin and eye lesions resolve.
- Contrast **tyrosinaemia type I (hepatorenal, fumarylacetoacetate hydrolase deficiency)** — liver failure, renal Fanconi syndrome, **cabbage-like odour**, succinylacetone in urine, high risk of **hepatocellular carcinoma**; treated with **nitisinone (NTBC) plus diet**.
- **Type III** (4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate dioxygenase) — neurological only.
- **Transient tyrosinaemia of the newborn** is the commonest cause of raised tyrosine and is benign, responding to vitamin C.
- The palmoplantar lesions are **painful out of proportion to their appearance** — the feature that distinguishes them from ordinary keratoderma.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Herpetiform corneal ulcer" baits an HSV answer. The **palmoplantar keratoderma plus developmental delay** makes it metabolic.

**REMEMBER:** *Tyrosine crystals scar the cornea and thicken the palms.*

`MA7680`

---

## 137. Parvovirus B19

**PEARL:** **Slapped-cheek rash = erythema infectiosum (fifth disease) = parvovirus B19**, the cause of **PURE RED CELL APLASIA** and **transient aplastic crisis**.

**UNDERSTAND:** Parvovirus B19 binds the **P antigen (globoside)** on **erythroid progenitors** and lyses them, halting red cell production for about a week. A healthy child tolerates this because red cells survive 120 days; but in **chronic haemolysis**, where survival is only a few days, the same brief arrest causes a **catastrophic fall in haemoglobin** — the aplastic crisis.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Aplastic crisis** occurs in **sickle cell disease, thalassaemia, hereditary spherocytosis, G6PD and pyruvate kinase deficiency** — reticulocytopenia with profound anaemia.
- **The rash is immune-complex mediated and appears AFTER viraemia ends** — so the child is **no longer infectious once the rash appears**.
- Other presentations: **arthropathy** (symmetrical small joints, especially in adult women), **papular-purpuric gloves-and-socks syndrome**, **chronic pure red cell aplasia in the immunocompromised** (treated with IVIG).
- **In pregnancy: fetal anaemia → high-output failure → NON-IMMUNE HYDROPS FETALIS** — the commonest infectious cause; monitor with **middle cerebral artery peak systolic velocity** and treat with **intrauterine transfusion**.
- **Non-enveloped single-stranded DNA virus** — the only ssDNA human pathogen; resistant to alcohol-based disinfectants.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The image identifies the virus; the question then asks for another disease it causes. **Pure red cell aplasia** is the answer to look for.

**REMEMBER:** *It stops red cell production — harmless unless your red cells are already dying fast.*

`MF9129`

---

## 138. Congenital Diaphragmatic Hernia — What Not To Do

**PEARL:** In congenital diaphragmatic hernia, **NEVER use bag-and-mask ventilation.** **Intubate immediately and pass a nasogastric tube on continuous suction.**

**UNDERSTAND:** Bag-and-mask forces gas into the **oesophagus and stomach**, and in CDH the stomach and bowel lie **inside the chest**. Distending them further compresses the already **hypoplastic lung and shifts the mediastinum**, converting respiratory distress into cardiorespiratory collapse. Intubation delivers gas directly to the lungs and the nasogastric tube decompresses the herniated viscera.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Presentation: **respiratory distress at birth, SCAPHOID abdomen, barrel chest, absent breath sounds and displaced apex beat** (usually **left-sided Bochdalek** — posterolateral).
- Types: **Bochdalek (posterolateral, ~85–90%, usually left)**, **Morgagni (anterior, retrosternal, right)**, eventration, and central tendon defects.
- The lethal problems are **pulmonary HYPOPLASIA and pulmonary HYPERTENSION**, not the hernia itself — hence surgery is **delayed until the infant is physiologically stable**, not performed as an emergency.
- Management: **intubate → NG tube on suction → gentle ventilation with permissive hypercapnia → treat pulmonary hypertension (iNO, sildenafil) → ECMO if needed → delayed surgical repair**.
- **Avoid high peak pressures** — the hypoplastic lung is easily barotraumatised.
- Antenatal prognosis is estimated from the **lung-to-head ratio** and **liver position** on ultrasound/MRI.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Asked in both directions — "initial intervention" (**intubate + NG tube**) and "which cannot be used" (**bag and mask**).

**REMEMBER:** *Never blow air into a chest that already contains bowel.*

`MA0437, MG1228`

---

## 139. Paediatric DKA — Fluids Before Insulin

**PEARL:** In paediatric diabetic ketoacidosis, give **normal saline FIRST and start the insulin infusion about 1 HOUR later**. Never give an insulin bolus.

**UNDERSTAND:** Children are at high risk of **cerebral oedema**, and rapid osmotic shifts are the trigger. Fluid alone **restores perfusion and begins lowering glucose gently**; adding insulin immediately would drop the glucose and effective osmolality too fast, pulling water into brain cells. Delaying insulin also avoids the abrupt **intracellular potassium shift** in a total-body-potassium-depleted child.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Diagnostic triad: **hyperglycaemia (> 200 mg/dL), ketosis, and metabolic acidosis (pH < 7.3 or bicarbonate < 15)**.
- Insulin: **0.05–0.1 units/kg/hour infusion, NO bolus**. Add **dextrose** to the fluid once glucose falls to ~250–300 mg/dL, and continue insulin until the **acidosis clears** (ketones, not glucose, guide the endpoint).
- **Potassium** must be added once urine output is established, even if the initial level is normal — total body potassium is always depleted.
- **Bicarbonate is not given** except for life-threatening acidosis — it worsens cerebral oedema and causes paradoxical CNS acidosis.
- **Cerebral oedema** — headache, falling heart rate, rising blood pressure, altered consciousness — occurs 4–12 h into treatment; treat with **mannitol or hypertonic saline** and slow the fluids.
- Clinical features: **Kussmaul breathing, dehydration, abdominal pain mimicking a surgical abdomen, drowsiness**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** All four options contain saline and insulin — the discriminator is the **sequence and the delay**.

**REMEMBER:** *Fluid first, insulin an hour later, never a bolus.*

`MG1232`

---

## 140. Neuronal Migration Disorders

**PEARL:** **Schizencephaly — a full-thickness cleft through the cerebral hemisphere lined by grey matter — is a NEURONAL MIGRATION disorder.** Myelomeningocele is a neural tube defect, and plagiocephaly a skull-shape deformity.

**UNDERSTAND:** Between 8 and 24 weeks, neurons born in the periventricular germinal matrix **migrate outward along radial glia** to build the six-layered cortex. If migration is interrupted along a whole radial column, no cortex forms there and the defect extends **from ventricle to pial surface** — a schizencephalic cleft. Because the cleft is **lined by grey matter (misplaced neurons)** rather than gliotic white matter, it is distinguished from a destructive porencephalic cyst.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Types: **open-lip** (cleft walls separated, CSF-filled) and **closed-lip** (walls apposed); **unilateral or bilateral**.
- Presentation: **seizures, developmental delay, microcephaly, spastic quadriparesis**; severity tracks with bilaterality and open-lip morphology. **MRI is the investigation of choice.**
- Other migration disorders: **lissencephaly (agyria — smooth brain, "figure-of-eight" on axial MRI, LIS1/DCX genes, Miller–Dieker syndrome)**, **pachygyria, polymicrogyria, heterotopia (subependymal or band/"double cortex"), and hemimegalencephaly**.
- Distinguish by timing: **neural tube defects (3–4 weeks) → anencephaly, myelomeningocele, encephalocele**; **proliferation defects (2–4 months) → micrencephaly, megalencephaly**; **migration defects (3–5 months) → lissencephaly, schizencephaly, heterotopia**; **organisation/myelination defects (later)**.
- **Plagiocephaly** is postural skull flattening — no brain malformation.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** The phrase "neuronal migration disorder" is the discriminator; three of the four options are malformations from entirely different embryological stages.

**REMEMBER:** *A grey-matter-lined cleft means neurons never finished their journey.*

`MA6600`

---

## 141. West Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Infantile spasms + developmental regression + HYPSARRHYTHMIA on EEG = West syndrome.** The drug of choice is **ACTH** (or **vigabatrin**, especially in tuberous sclerosis).

**UNDERSTAND:** West syndrome is an **epileptic encephalopathy** — the abnormal electrical activity itself causes the developmental regression, not merely the underlying lesion. That is why treatment aims to **abolish both the spasms and the hypsarrhythmia**, and why conventional anticonvulsants (phenytoin, phenobarbitone, levetiracetam) are ineffective: they suppress seizures without abolishing the interictal chaos.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Onset **3–12 months**; spasms are **clusters of sudden flexion (salaam/jack-knife attacks) on waking**, each lasting 1–2 seconds.
- **Hypsarrhythmia** = **high-voltage, chaotic, disorganised slow waves with multifocal spikes**.
- **Vigabatrin is FIRST LINE when the cause is TUBEROUS SCLEROSIS**; its major toxicity is **irreversible peripheral visual field constriction**. **ACTH/prednisolone** is first line otherwise.
- Causes: **symptomatic (~70–80%) — hypoxic-ischaemic injury, tuberous sclerosis, malformations, metabolic disease, Down syndrome; cryptogenic** in the rest. Cryptogenic cases have the better prognosis.
- **Always examine the skin under Wood's lamp for ash-leaf macules** — tuberous sclerosis changes the drug choice.
- May evolve into **Lennox–Gastaut syndrome** (slow spike-and-wave, multiple seizure types, intellectual disability).

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Drug of choice is the question. Hypsarrhythmia is the giveaway word; the distractors are standard anticonvulsants.

**REMEMBER:** *Chaotic EEG needs a hormone, not an anticonvulsant.*

`MF5349`

---

## 142. Upper : Lower Segment Ratio

**PEARL:** **U:L ratio is 1.7 at birth, 1.3 at 3 years, 1.0 at 7 years, and ~0.9 in adults.** A **LOW U:L ratio for age = short trunk** → **spondyloepiphyseal dysplasia** or vertebral anomalies. A **HIGH ratio = short limbs** → **achondroplasia, rickets, untreated congenital hypothyroidism**.

**UNDERSTAND:** The ratio falls with age because **the limbs grow faster than the trunk throughout childhood**. Deviation therefore tells you **which compartment is abnormal**: a disproportionately short trunk lowers the ratio (spine disease), while disproportionately short limbs raise it (long bone disease). This is why the measurement localises the dysplasia without any imaging.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Lower segment = pubic symphysis to floor**; **upper segment = height − lower segment**.
- Worked example: height 90 cm, lower segment 45 cm → upper segment 45 → **U:L = 1.0** at 3 years, when it should be **1.3** → **low for age → short trunk → spondyloepiphyseal dysplasia**.
- **Arm span**: about **2.5 cm less than height at birth**, **equals height at ~11 years**, and slightly exceeds it thereafter. **Arm span much greater than height** → **Marfan syndrome, homocystinuria, Klinefelter**; **arm span much less than height** → short-limbed dysplasias.
- Proportionate short stature: **constitutional delay, familial, endocrine (GH deficiency, hypothyroidism), chronic disease, malnutrition**. Disproportionate: **skeletal dysplasias, rickets**.
- **Mid-parental height** predicts genetic target: **(father's + mother's height ± 13 cm) ÷ 2**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Compute the ratio, compare it with the age-appropriate value, then decide **trunk vs limb**. The numbers in the stem are always sufficient.

**REMEMBER:** *Low ratio means short spine; high ratio means short limbs.*

`MA4771`

---

## 143. Suspected VP Shunt Infection

**PEARL:** In a child with a ventriculoperitoneal shunt and suspected meningitis, obtain CSF by **SHUNT TAP (reservoir aspiration)** plus blood cultures — **not by lumbar puncture**.

**UNDERSTAND:** The infection lives **on the shunt hardware as a biofilm**, and the shunt diverts CSF away from the lumbar sac — so **lumbar CSF may be normal or sterile** even in florid shunt infection. Sampling directly from the reservoir tests the compartment that is actually infected, and avoids the risk of LP in a child who may have **raised pressure from shunt obstruction**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Technique: aspirate from the **subcutaneous reservoir with a 25-gauge needle** under strict asepsis.
- Commonest organisms: **coagulase-negative staphylococci (Staph. epidermidis, ~50%)**, then **Staph. aureus**, Gram-negative bacilli, and **Cutibacterium acnes** — skin flora introduced at surgery, so most infections occur **within the first 1–2 months**.
- Treatment: **IV antibiotics (vancomycin + a third-generation cephalosporin) PLUS removal or externalisation of the shunt** — hardware infection rarely clears with antibiotics alone.
- Distal (peritoneal) end infection may present as **abdominal pain, pseudocyst or peritonitis**; ventriculoatrial shunts can cause **shunt nephritis**.
- **Shunt obstruction** presents with features of raised intracranial pressure — **headache, vomiting, drowsiness, bulging fontanelle, sunsetting eyes**; assess with a **shunt series X-ray and CT/MRI**, and radionuclide study for patency.
- In infants, **Kernig and Brudzinski signs are usually absent** — rely on **irritability, poor feeding, temperature instability and a bulging fontanelle**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Next best step" — the answer pairs **blood culture with a shunt tap**. The lumbar puncture option is designed to attract the standard meningitis reflex.

**REMEMBER:** *Sample the compartment the shunt is draining, not the one it bypasses.*

`MF1356`

---

## 144. Vitamin K Deficiency Bleeding

**PEARL:** **Vitamin K deficiency bleeding occurs in BREASTFED infants, not formula-fed ones** — formula is **fortified** with vitamin K. Fat malabsorption (**biliary obstruction, cystic fibrosis, biliary atresia**) and **broad-spectrum antibiotics** are the other causes.

**UNDERSTAND:** Newborns start with almost no vitamin K because it **crosses the placenta poorly, the gut is sterile at birth, and liver stores are low**. Human milk contains only **~2 µg/L**, far below requirement, whereas formula contains **~50 µg/L**. Vitamin K is also **fat-soluble**, so any condition blocking bile flow or fat absorption prevents its uptake, and gut flora producing **menaquinone (K2)** are wiped out by antibiotics.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Three forms: **EARLY (within 24 h)** — from maternal drugs crossing the placenta (**phenytoin, phenobarbitone, rifampicin, isoniazid, warfarin**); **CLASSIC (1–14 days)** — low stores plus inadequate intake; **LATE (2–12 weeks)** — exclusively breastfed infants and those with **malabsorption**, with a high rate of **intracranial haemorrhage**.
- Prevention: **vitamin K 1 mg IM (0.5 mg if < 1 kg) as a single dose at birth** — this is why late VKDB is now rare.
- Labs: **prolonged PT (first, because factor VII has the shortest half-life), then prolonged aPTT; normal platelets and fibrinogen**; **PIVKA-II** is the specific marker.
- Vitamin K–dependent factors: **II, VII, IX, X, protein C and protein S** — γ-carboxylated by **γ-glutamyl carboxylase**.
- Treatment of active bleeding: **IV vitamin K plus fresh frozen plasma** for immediate factor replacement.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Would NOT prompt suspicion" → **artificial/formula feeds**. Every other option impairs absorption or synthesis.

**REMEMBER:** *Formula is fortified; breast milk is not.*

`MA0459`

---

## 145. Radiological Signs of Scurvy

**PEARL:** **Frankel's line = the DENSE white zone of provisional CALCIFICATION at the metaphysis.** The **lucent band just beneath it is the Trümmerfeld zone**; the **ring around the epiphysis is Wimberger's sign**; the **metaphyseal spur is Pelkan's**.

**UNDERSTAND:** In scurvy, **osteoblasts cannot lay down osteoid**, but the **cartilage cells continue to calcify normally**. So calcified cartilage accumulates unresorbed at the growth plate as a **dense white line (Frankel)**, while the region just proximal — where new bone should have formed but did not — becomes **weak and lucent (Trümmerfeld)** and is the site where fractures and haemorrhage occur.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Full radiological list: **generalised osteopenia, "pencil-point" cortical thinning, ground-glass osteoporosis, subperiosteal haemorrhage with periosteal reaction on healing, scorbutic rosary, and haemarthrosis**.
- **Wimberger's RING sign (scurvy)** must be distinguished from the **Wimberger's SIGN of congenital syphilis** — symmetrical **medial metaphyseal destruction of the proximal tibia**.
- Clinical scurvy: **perifollicular haemorrhage, corkscrew hairs, swollen bleeding gums (only if teeth have erupted), pseudoparalysis from subperiosteal bleeding (child lies frog-legged, refuses to move), poor wound healing, anaemia**.
- Mechanism: vitamin C is the cofactor for **prolyl and lysyl hydroxylase** — without it collagen cannot cross-link.
- **Rickets** is the differential — but rickets gives **cupping, fraying and widening of the metaphysis with a RAREFIED (not dense) zone**, plus a **rounded rachitic rosary**, **craniotabes, frontal bossing, delayed fontanelle closure and craniosynostosis, Harrison's sulcus, genu varum/valgum**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Sign-to-description matching. Each eponym describes a **different anatomical zone** — learn them as a sequence from epiphysis to shaft.

**REMEMBER:** *Cartilage keeps calcifying while bone stops forming — dense line above, weak zone below.*

`MC0259, MA0452`

---

## 146. Treacher Collins Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Treacher Collins (mandibulofacial dysostosis) spares INTELLIGENCE.** Features are **downslanting palpebral fissures, coloboma of the LOWER eyelid, malar/zygomatic hypoplasia, micrognathia, malformed pinnae and conductive deafness**.

**UNDERSTAND:** The defect (**TCOF1**, autosomal dominant) impairs **neural crest cell proliferation and survival destined for the FIRST and SECOND pharyngeal arches**. Those arches build the **maxilla, mandible, zygoma, external and middle ear** — and nothing else. The brain derives from different precursors, so **cognition is normal**, and the deafness is **conductive (ossicular) rather than sensorineural**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Downslanting palpebral fissures** (contrast **Down syndrome, which upslants**) and **lower-lid coloboma with absent lower lashes** are the most distinctive signs.
- Cleft palate in ~30%; **choanal atresia and airway obstruction** may be life-threatening in infancy.
- **Hearing aids (bone conduction) early** are critical to prevent speech delay in a child with normal intelligence.
- Compare: **Pierre Robin sequence** — micrognathia, glossoptosis, U-shaped cleft palate, airway obstruction (a sequence, not a syndrome); **Goldenhar (oculo-auriculo-vertebral)** — **hemifacial microsomia, epibulbar dermoid, vertebral anomalies**, usually **unilateral**; **Nager syndrome** — Treacher Collins features plus radial limb defects.
- Other conditions where intelligence is characteristically normal: **Turner syndrome, Marfan syndrome, neurofibromatosis type 1 (usually), achondroplasia**.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** "Not associated with" → **low intelligence**. The facial features are all genuine.

**REMEMBER:** *First and second arch only — the face is affected, the brain is not.*

`MB0256`

---

## 147. Short Stature — Definition

**PEARL:** Height **below the 3rd percentile** or **less than −2 SD** for age and sex.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Familial short stature:** **bone age = chronological age**, normal growth velocity, short parents, normal final height for the family.
- **Constitutional delay:** **bone age < chronological age**, delayed puberty, *normal* final adult height ("late bloomer").
- **Pathological:** low growth velocity (<5 cm/yr) — hypothyroidism, GH deficiency, Turner, chronic disease.

**REMEMBER:** *Bone age = age → familial; bone age behind → constitutional or pathological.*

---

## 148. Upper/Lower Segment Ratio

**PEARL:** U/L ratio ≈ **1.7 at birth**, **1.3 at 3 years**, **1.0 after 7 years**.

**MUST KNOW:** **High U/L ratio** = short limbs — achondroplasia, **Turner syndrome**, untreated hypothyroidism, rickets. **Low U/L ratio** = long limbs — **Marfan syndrome** and **hypogonadism** (delayed epiphyseal fusion lets legs grow on).

**REMEMBER:** *Ratio falls with age to 1; high = short limbs, low = long limbs.*

---

## 149. Developmental Milestones

**PEARL:**
- **Transferring an object from hand to hand: ~5.5–6 months** (the beginning of comparing objects).
- **Cruising** — pulling to stand and shuffling along furniture: **10–11 months**.

**MUST KNOW:** Related anchors: sits with support 5 mo, sits without support 6–8 mo, **pincer grasp 9–10 mo**, stands alone 11–12 mo, **walks independently 12–15 mo**.

**REMEMBER:** *Hand-to-hand at 6 months, cruise at 10, walk at 12.*

---

## 150. Severe Acute Malnutrition (SAM) — WHO Definition

**PEARL:** Any ONE of: **weight-for-height/length < −3 SD**, **bilateral pitting oedema**, or **MUAC < 11.5 cm**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Stunting = height-for-age < −2 SD** → **chronic** malnutrition. **Wasting = weight-for-height < −2 SD** → **acute**.
- MUAC is used from **6–59 months**; oedema alone makes it SAM regardless of weight.
- The **10 steps** of SAM management: treat/prevent hypoglycemia, hypothermia, dehydration (**ReSoMal**, low sodium/high potassium), electrolytes, infection, micronutrients (**no iron in the stabilization phase**), cautious feeding, catch-up growth, sensory stimulation, follow-up.

**REMEMBER:** *SAM = <−3SD or oedema or MUAC <11.5; stunted = chronic, wasted = acute.*

---

## 151. Folic Acid in SAM

**PEARL:** **5 mg on day 1**, then **1 mg daily until day 14**.

**REMEMBER:** *5 once, then 1 daily for a fortnight.*

---

## 152. Iron in Low-Birth-Weight Infants

**PEARL:** LBW infants need oral iron **2 mg/kg/day from ~1 month until 1 year** to prevent iron-deficiency anaemia.

**MUST KNOW:** Term infants start at **6 months** (1 mg/kg/day) when maternal stores run out. Preterms are born with lower stores because most iron transfer occurs in the **third trimester**.

**REMEMBER:** *LBW: iron at 1 month; term: iron at 6 months.*

---

## 153. First-Day Feeding & Preterm Feeding Method

**PEARL:**
- A **term** baby needs **60 mL/kg** of fluid/milk in the **first 24 hours** (increasing ~15–20 mL/kg/day to 150 mL/kg).
- A **stable 33-week preterm** should be fed **orally by spoon, paladai or cup** first; if intake is inadequate, move to **orogastric/nasogastric tube**.

**UNDERSTAND:** Coordinated **suck–swallow–breathe develops at ~34 weeks** — below that, direct breastfeeding is unreliable, but a stable baby can still take expressed milk by cup/spoon. Bottles are avoided (nipple confusion).

**REMEMBER:** *Day 1 = 60 mL/kg; <34 weeks = cup/paladai, then tube — never bottle.*

---

## 154. Breastfeeding Contraindications

**PEARL:**
- **Maternal tuberculosis:** avoid breastfeeding for the **first 2 weeks of ATT**, until the mother is non-infectious (expressed milk may be given; the infant gets INH prophylaxis and BCG afterwards).
- **Active herpes simplex lesions on the breast:** stop feeding **on the affected side only**.
- Most other maternal viral infections do **not** contraindicate breastfeeding.

**MUST KNOW:** Absolute contraindications: **galactosemia** (infant), maternal HIV (where safe replacement feeding is feasible — in India exclusive breastfeeding with ART is advised), active untreated TB (as above), chemotherapy/radioactive isotopes.

**REMEMBER:** *TB = 2 weeks off; HSV = only that breast; galactosemia = never.*

---

## 155. Neonatal Jaundice — Conjugated vs Unconjugated

**PEARL:** **Congenital hypothyroidism** causes prolonged **UNconjugated** hyperbilirubinaemia. **Dubin–Johnson, Rotor and galactosaemia** cause **CONJUGATED** hyperbilirubinaemia.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Other prolonged unconjugated causes: breast-milk jaundice, Crigler–Najjar, Gilbert, haemolysis, pyloric stenosis.
- **Conjugated bilirubin >20% of total (or >2 mg/dL) is ALWAYS pathological** — think biliary atresia, choledochal cyst, neonatal hepatitis, sepsis, metabolic.
- Hypothyroidism delays bilirubin conjugation (reduced UGT activity) and slows gut motility.

**REMEMBER:** *Hypothyroid = unconjugated; the "storage/metabolic" trio (Dubin-Johnson, Rotor, galactosaemia) = conjugated.*

---

## 156. Biliary Atresia

**PEARL:** Suspect biliary atresia when **jaundice persists beyond 2 weeks** (with pale stools and dark urine).

**MUST KNOW:** Any jaundice past 2 weeks → **fractionate the bilirubin**. Investigation: ultrasound (**triangular cord sign**, absent/small gallbladder), HIDA scan, and **liver biopsy** (definitive). **Kasai portoenterostomy must be done before 60 days** for the best outcome; late cases need transplant.

**REMEMBER:** *Jaundice after 2 weeks + pale stool = check conjugated bilirubin; Kasai before 60 days.*

---

## 157. Cephalhaematoma

**PEARL:** A **subperiosteal** collection of blood — **limited by suture lines** because the periosteum is bound to bone edges.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Appears **hours after birth**, resolves in weeks, may **calcify** and can cause **jaundice** on resorption.
- **Caput succedaneum** = oedema, crosses sutures, present at birth, resolves in days.
- **Subgaleal haemorrhage** crosses sutures, is **fluctuant and can hold the entire blood volume** → shock; the dangerous one, classically after ventouse.

**REMEMBER:** *Cephalhaematoma respects sutures; caput and subgaleal don't. Subgaleal kills.*

---

## 158. Mongolian Spots

**PEARL:** A **self-limited neural-crest migration defect** in normal neonates — melanocytes trapped in the **dermis** on their way to the epidermis. They **fade with age**, need **no treatment**, and have **no malignant potential**. **Extensive spots occur in Hurler syndrome** (and other mucopolysaccharidoses).

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Their bruise-like appearance is frequently mistaken for **child abuse** — document them.

**REMEMBER:** *Dermal melanocytes, fade away, never malignant; extensive = Hurler.*

---

## 159. Neonatal Pupils

**PEARL:** Newborn pupils are **physiologically constricted** because of immature autonomic (sympathetic) innervation of the iris — **parasympathetic predominance** — and they are **difficult to dilate**.

**MUST KNOW:** The dilator pupillae is poorly developed; pupillary **light reflex appears at ~30–32 weeks** of gestation and is consistent by 34 weeks — used to estimate gestational age.

**REMEMBER:** *Newborn = small, stubborn pupils.*

---

## 160. Subtle Seizures

**PEARL:** **Subtle seizures are the commonest type of neonatal seizure**.

**MUST KNOW:** Manifestations: eye deviation/blinking, chewing/sucking (oro-buccal-lingual), cycling/boxing/swimming limb movements, apnoea, autonomic changes. Commonest **cause** of neonatal seizures overall = **hypoxic-ischaemic encephalopathy**. **Tonic-clonic (generalized) seizures are rare in neonates** — incomplete myelination and cortical organization prevents generalization.

**REMEMBER:** *Neonates seize subtly; HIE is the usual cause.*

---

## 161. Neonatal Polycythemia

**PEARL:** A haematocrit of **72%** in a 2-day-old = polycythemia (venous **Hct >65%**). With symptoms (feeding intolerance, abdominal distension, lethargy, jitteriness), treatment is **partial exchange transfusion** with normal saline to reduce viscosity and improve perfusion.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Symptomatic → PET at **Hct ≥65%**; asymptomatic → usually **PET at ≥75%**, hydration below that.
- Causes: **infant of a diabetic mother**, IUGR/placental insufficiency, delayed cord clamping, twin-twin transfusion, trisomies.
- Complications: **hypoglycemia, NEC, renal vein thrombosis, stroke, hyperbilirubinaemia**.

**REMEMBER:** *Hct >65% + symptoms = partial exchange with saline.*

---

## 162. Infant of a Diabetic Mother

**PEARL:** Monitor **serum calcium along with blood glucose** — **hypocalcemia** is common and neurologically significant if missed.

**UNDERSTAND:** Maternal hyperglycemia → fetal hyperinsulinism → **hypoglycemia within hours of birth**; magnesium loss from maternal diuresis → fetal hypomagnesemia → functional **hypoparathyroidism** → hypocalcemia (typically at 24–72 h).

**MUST KNOW:** Also: macrosomia + birth trauma, **polycythemia**, hyperbilirubinaemia, **RDS** (insulin delays surfactant), hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (septal), and **caudal regression syndrome**.

**REMEMBER:** *IDM: sugar low early, calcium low at 1–3 days.*

---

## 163. Neonatal Resuscitation — Adrenaline & the Pop-off Valve

**PEARL:**
- Give **adrenaline** when the heart rate remains **<60/min after 60 seconds** of effective bag-and-mask (positive pressure) ventilation — i.e. after ventilation ± chest compressions have failed.
- The **pop-off (pressure release) valve** of the self-inflating bag opens at **30–40 cm H2O** to prevent barotrauma/pneumothorax.

**MUST KNOW:** Adrenaline dose **0.01–0.03 mg/kg IV (1:10,000)**, preferably umbilical venous. **Ventilation is the single most important step** in neonatal resuscitation — the "golden minute".

**REMEMBER:** *HR <60 after a minute of good ventilation → adrenaline; pop-off at 30–40 cm H2O.*

---

## 164. Congenital Diaphragmatic Hernia — Prognosis

**PEARL:** The **degree of pulmonary hypertension** is the most critical determinant of prognosis.

**UNDERSTAND:** Herniated bowel compresses the developing lung → **pulmonary hypoplasia** with an abnormally muscularized pulmonary vasculature → high pulmonary vascular resistance → right-to-left shunting and refractory hypoxaemia.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Do NOT bag-mask ventilate** — it distends the bowel and worsens compression; **intubate immediately** and pass a nasogastric tube.
- Surgery is **not** an emergency — stabilize first (gentle ventilation, iNO, ECMO), then repair.
- 85–90% are **left-sided (Bochdalek, posterolateral)**.

**REMEMBER:** *CDH kills through pulmonary hypertension, not the hernia — intubate, don't bag.*

---

## 165. Rubinstein–Taybi Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Broad thumbs and broad great toes** + intellectual disability + **down-slanting palpebral fissures and beaked nose**.

**MUST KNOW:** *CREBBP* (CBP) mutation on 16p13.3.

**REMEMBER:** *Broad thumbs + beaked nose = Rubinstein–Taybi.*

---

## 166. Prader–Willi Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Neonatal hypotonia and poor feeding**, later **hyperphagia and obesity**, with **almond-shaped eyes, small hands and feet**, hypogonadism.

**MUST KNOW:** Loss of the **paternal** 15q11-13 (deletion or maternal uniparental disomy). The **maternal** counterpart is **Angelman syndrome** — happy puppet, ataxia, seizures, no speech.

**REMEMBER:** *Floppy baby who later eats everything = Prader–Willi (paternal 15q).*

---

## 167. Bardet–Biedl Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Early-onset obesity + retinitis pigmentosa + polydactyly + intellectual disability** (+ renal anomalies and hypogonadism).

**MUST KNOW:** A **ciliopathy**, autosomal recessive. **Renal failure is the main cause of death** — the feature that separates it from Prader–Willi (which lacks retinitis pigmentosa and polydactyly). Laurence–Moon has spastic paraplegia but no polydactyly/obesity.

**REMEMBER:** *Obese + blind + extra fingers = Bardet–Biedl (watch the kidneys).*

---

## 168. Pierre Robin Sequence

**PEARL:** **Micrognathia + glossoptosis + U-shaped cleft palate** → airway obstruction and feeding difficulty.

**UNDERSTAND:** It is a **sequence**: the small mandible displaces the tongue backwards, which physically prevents the palatal shelves from fusing.

**MUST KNOW:** Management is **prone positioning** first; associated with **Stickler syndrome** (commonest) and 22q11 deletion.

**REMEMBER:** *Small jaw → tongue back → palate can't close; nurse prone.*

---

## 169. Pendred Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Bilateral sensorineural hearing loss + goitre + positive perchlorate discharge test** — from defective **iodine organification** and a cochlear malformation.

**MUST KNOW:** ***SLC26A4* (the PDS gene) on chromosome 7**, coding a **chloride/iodide transporter (pendrin)**; autosomal recessive. Imaging shows **Mondini dysplasia / enlarged vestibular aqueduct**. It is the commonest syndromic cause of congenital deafness; patients may be euthyroid or **hypothyroid with goitre**.

**REMEMBER:** *Deaf + goitre + perchlorate discharge = Pendred.*

---

## 170. Patau Syndrome (Trisomy 13)

**PEARL:** **Cleft lip and palate, polydactyly, microphthalmia, holoprosencephaly and cardiac defects**.

**MUST KNOW:** Also cutis aplasia of the scalp and rocker-bottom feet; most die within the first year. **Trisomy 13 = "13 letters in Patau's midline defects"** — think midline face and brain.

**REMEMBER:** *Cleft + extra digits + small eyes = Patau (13).*

---

## 171. Edwards Syndrome (Trisomy 18)

**PEARL:** **Clenched fists with overlapping fingers**, prominent **occiput**, low-set malformed ears, **micrognathia**, and **rocker-bottom feet**.

**MUST KNOW:** Second commonest autosomal trisomy; VSD is near-universal; **death usually within weeks to months**. Screening: **all four markers low** (AFP, hCG, estriol, and inhibin low-ish) — versus Down syndrome, where **hCG and inhibin are high**.

**REMEMBER:** *Overlapping fingers + rocker-bottom feet = Edwards (18).*

---

## 172. Holt–Oram Syndrome

**PEARL:** Autosomal dominant — **congenital heart defect (usually ASD, also VSD) + upper limb anomaly (absent/hypoplastic or triphalangeal thumb)**.

**MUST KNOW:** *TBX5* mutation; the **radial ray** is affected, so it is always the **upper** limb. Conduction disease (progressive AV block) is common even with a small defect.

**REMEMBER:** *"Heart–hand syndrome": ASD + funny thumb = Holt–Oram (TBX5).*

---

## 173. Ellis–van Creveld Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Short stature + polydactyly + narrow thorax + congenital heart defect**, with **short tubular bones and cone-shaped epiphyses**.

**MUST KNOW:** Chondroectodermal dysplasia — also **nail and dental hypoplasia**; the classic cardiac lesion is a **single atrium/ASD**. Autosomal recessive (*EVC*), famously in the Amish.

**REMEMBER:** *Short + extra finger + narrow chest + single atrium = Ellis–van Creveld.*

---

## 174. Alagille Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Paucity of intrahepatic bile ducts** + **peripheral pulmonary artery stenosis** + characteristic facies (**broad prominent forehead, deep-set eyes, pointed chin**).

**MUST KNOW:** *JAG1* (Notch pathway), autosomal dominant. Also **butterfly vertebrae** and **posterior embryotoxon** (eye). Presents as **neonatal cholestasis** — the key differential from biliary atresia, but with **extrahepatic ducts patent**.

**REMEMBER:** *Cholestasis + pulmonary stenosis + butterfly vertebrae = Alagille.*

---

## 175. Kenny–Caffey Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Cortical thickening of long bones with medullary stenosis**, growth retardation, **hypocalcemic seizures** and **high serum phosphate** — due to **hypoparathyroidism**.

**REMEMBER:** *Thick bones + low calcium + high phosphate = Kenny–Caffey (hypoparathyroid).*

---

## 176. Young Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Obstructive azoospermia + chronic sinusitis + bronchiectasis**, with *Pseudomonas* colonization — caused by **inspissated (thick) mucus** in respiratory and reproductive tracts.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** Differentiate from **Kartagener syndrome** (situs inversus + sinusitis + bronchiectasis, ciliary dysmotility, **sperm are immotile but present**) and **cystic fibrosis** (**congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens**, sweat chloride positive). In Young syndrome, cilia and sweat chloride are **normal**.

**REMEMBER:** *Young = thick mucus blocks both the lungs and the epididymis; normal cilia, normal sweat test.*

---

## 177. Noonan Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Pulmonary stenosis** (often with a dysplastic valve) is the characteristic cardiac defect in Noonan syndrome.

**MUST KNOW:** *PTPN11* (RAS-MAPK pathway), autosomal dominant; short stature, webbed neck, low posterior hairline, pectus, **normal karyotype in both sexes**. Also hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.

**EXAM CONNECTION:** **Turner syndrome (45,XO) is associated with LEFT-sided lesions — coarctation and bicuspid aortic valve**; Noonan ("male Turner", normal karyotype) is **right-sided — pulmonary stenosis**.

**REMEMBER:** *Turner = left heart (coarctation); Noonan = right heart (pulmonary stenosis).*

---

## 178. Ataxia Telangiectasia

**PEARL:** **ATM** gene mutation. Triad: **progressive cerebellar ataxia + oculocutaneous telangiectasia + sinopulmonary infections**. The immunodeficiency is a **deficiency of IgA (and IgG2)** — **IgM is normal**, not absent.

**MUST KNOW:**
- ATM is a **DNA double-strand break repair** gene → **radiosensitivity** (avoid diagnostic irradiation) and a high risk of **lymphoma/leukaemia**.
- **Raised alpha-fetoprotein** is a useful diagnostic marker.
- Autosomal recessive; ataxia appears first (toddler), telangiectasia later (school age).

**REMEMBER:** *ATM: ataxia, telangiectasia, low IgA, high AFP, cancer risk.*

---

## 179. Osteopetrosis

**PEARL:** **CLCN7** mutation causes osteopetrosis — increased bone density with abnormal, brittle bone.

**MUST KNOW:** Defective **osteoclast** function (carbonic anhydrase II deficiency in one form) → failure of bone resorption. Marrow space is obliterated → **pancytopenia and extramedullary haematopoiesis**; cranial nerve compression (blindness, deafness); paradoxical **fractures**. Radiology: **"bone-in-bone", "Erlenmeyer flask", sandwich vertebra**. Treatment of the severe infantile form: **haematopoietic stem cell transplant**.

**REMEMBER:** *Osteoclasts fail → dense brittle bones + pancytopenia + cranial nerve palsies.*

---

## 180. Neurocutaneous Distinction

**PEARL:** **Lisch nodules (iris hamartomas) belong to NF1**; **facial angiofibromas belong to tuberous sclerosis**, not NF1.

**MUST KNOW:** Tuberous sclerosis (TSC1/TSC2): **ash-leaf macules** (earliest, Wood's lamp), **shagreen patch**, **subungual fibromas**, **cardiac rhabdomyoma**, renal angiomyolipoma, **infantile spasms** + cortical tubers. NF1: café-au-lait macules, axillary freckling, neurofibromas, **optic glioma**, sphenoid dysplasia.

**REMEMBER:** *Lisch = NF1; angiofibroma + ash-leaf = tuberous sclerosis.*

---

## 181. Trigonocephaly

**PEARL:** Premature fusion of the **metopic** suture.

**MUST KNOW:** **Sagittal** fusion → **scaphocephaly/dolichocephaly** (commonest craniosynostosis); **unilateral coronal** → anterior plagiocephaly; **bilateral coronal** → brachycephaly; **all sutures** → oxycephaly/turricephaly (raised ICP).

**REMEMBER:** *Metopic = triangular forehead = trigonocephaly.*

---

## 182. Transposition of the Great Arteries

**PEARL:** A cyanotic lesion with **ventriculo-arterial discordance** (aorta from RV, pulmonary artery from LV) — two parallel circuits, needing urgent mixing. Chest X-ray shows the **"egg-on-a-string"** appearance (narrow superior mediastinum from the AP-aligned great vessels); **pulmonary venous hypertension is NOT a feature** — pulmonary vascularity is increased, not congested.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Commonest cyanotic heart disease presenting on day 1**; survival depends on a PDA/PFO/VSD.
- Management: **prostaglandin E1 infusion** to keep the duct open ± **balloon atrial septostomy (Rashkind)** → **arterial switch** operation.
- Associated with **infants of diabetic mothers**.

**REMEMBER:** *Egg on a string, cyanosis on day 1 → PGE1 and septostomy.*

---

## 183. Kawasaki Disease

**PEARL:** Fever ≥5 days + **strawberry tongue, non-purulent conjunctival congestion, rash, extremity changes with periungual peeling, cervical node** — treat promptly with **IVIG + aspirin** to prevent **coronary artery aneurysms**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- IVIG is most effective **within 10 days** of fever onset.
- Kawasaki disease is the one situation where **aspirin is given to children** despite Reye syndrome concerns (high anti-inflammatory dose, then low antiplatelet dose).
- Follow up with **2D echo** at diagnosis, 2 weeks and 6–8 weeks.
- Commonest cause of **acquired heart disease in children** in developed countries.

**REMEMBER:** *Fever 5 days + red everything → IVIG within 10 days or the coronaries pay.*

---

## 184. Henoch–Schönlein Purpura

**PEARL:** Treatment for **significant gastrointestinal involvement or severe symptoms** is **glucocorticoids (prednisolone)**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **IgA vasculitis**: palpable purpura on **buttocks and extensor legs**, arthritis, colicky abdominal pain, nephritis; **platelets are normal or raised** (key differentiator from ITP).
- Steroids relieve abdominal pain and arthritis and reduce **intussusception** risk (classically **ileo-ileal**) — but do **not** prevent nephritis.
- Prognosis is determined by **renal involvement** — follow urinalysis and BP for 6 months.

**REMEMBER:** *Purpura with normal platelets = HSP; steroids for the gut, not the kidney.*

---

## 185. Measles — Commonest Complication

**PEARL:** **Otitis media** is the commonest complication of measles (Eustachian tube blockage → secondary bacterial infection).

**MUST KNOW:** **Pneumonia** is the commonest **cause of death**; **diarrhoea** is the commonest GI complication. Give **vitamin A** to all children with measles. Rare late complication: **SSPE**.

**REMEMBER:** *Otitis media commonest complication; pneumonia commonest killer.*

---

## 186. Subacute Sclerosing Panencephalitis

**PEARL:** Diagnosis rests on **elevated anti-measles IgG antibody in the CSF**.

**MUST KNOW:** Occurs **7–10 years** after measles (higher risk if infected before age 2); **myoclonic jerks + progressive dementia**; EEG shows **periodic burst-suppression (Radermecker) complexes**. Uniformly fatal; prevented only by **measles vaccination**.

**REMEMBER:** *Myoclonus + dementia + CSF measles antibody = SSPE.*

---

## 187. Pertussis

**PEARL:** In infants with paroxysmal cough and a **markedly raised leukocyte count with lymphocytosis**, the drug of choice is **azithromycin**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Azithromycin is preferred in infants **<1 month** because erythromycin is linked to **hypertrophic pyloric stenosis**.
- Antibiotics reduce **transmission** but change the illness little once the paroxysmal stage begins.
- **Absolute lymphocytosis** correlates with severity; complications: apnoea, seizures, pulmonary hypertension.
- Give **prophylaxis to all household contacts** regardless of vaccination status.

**REMEMBER:** *Whoop + high lymphocyte count = azithromycin (for the family too).*

---

## 188. Acute Epiglottitis

**PEARL:** A **cherry-red epiglottis** is the classic finding — a life-threatening airway emergency.

**MUST KNOW:**
- ***Haemophilus influenzae* type b**; now rare due to Hib vaccination.
- **4 D's**: Drooling, Dysphagia, Dysphonia, Distress; child sits in the **tripod position**, **no cough** (unlike croup).
- **Do NOT examine the throat or lie the child down** — secure the airway first in theatre.
- X-ray (only if stable): **thumb sign**; croup shows the **steeple sign**.

**REMEMBER:** *Drooling, tripod, no cough → don't touch the throat, call for the airway.*

---

## 189. Neonatal Chlamydial Infection

**PEARL:** *Chlamydia trachomatis* causes neonatal **conjunctivitis** and, at **1–3 months**, an **afebrile pneumonia** with insidious **staccato cough, tachypnoea and peripheral eosinophilia**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Conjunctivitis timing:** chemical (silver nitrate) day 1; **gonococcal days 2–5 (hyperacute, purulent)**; **chlamydial days 5–14**.
- Treatment of both eye and lung disease is **oral erythromycin/azithromycin** — topical therapy alone does not clear nasopharyngeal carriage.

**REMEMBER:** *Afebrile, wheezy, eosinophilic pneumonia at 6 weeks + conjunctivitis = Chlamydia.*

---

## 190. Meningococcal Exposure

**PEARL:** After exposure to meningococcal meningitis, **antibiotic chemoprophylaxis** gives immediate protection; **vaccination is also recommended but does not act immediately**.

**MUST KNOW:** Prophylaxis: **rifampicin (2 days), or single-dose ciprofloxacin/ceftriaxone** — ceftriaxone is preferred in pregnancy. Give within 24 hours to close/household contacts. **Complement (terminal C5–C9) deficiency** predisposes to recurrent *Neisseria* infection.

**REMEMBER:** *Antibiotics now, vaccine for later.*

---

## 191. Live Vaccines and Immunodeficiency

**PEARL:** Live vaccines are **contraindicated in DiGeorge syndrome, Wiskott–Aldrich syndrome and ataxia telangiectasia** — but are **NOT contraindicated in complement deficiency**.

**UNDERSTAND:** Complement deficiency leaves T- and B-cell function intact, so a live organism can still be controlled; the T-cell defects above cannot.

**MUST KNOW:** Also avoid live vaccines in SCID, high-dose steroids, and symptomatic HIV with low CD4. Complement-deficient patients specifically **need** meningococcal, pneumococcal and Hib vaccination.

**REMEMBER:** *No T cells, no live vaccine; complement deficiency needs MORE vaccines, not fewer.*

---

## 192. HIV Diagnosis in Infants

**PEARL:** The next best step in an infant born to an HIV-positive mother is **HIV DNA PCR on a dried blood spot**.

**UNDERSTAND:** **Maternal IgG antibodies persist up to 18 months**, so antibody (ELISA) tests cannot distinguish infection from passive transfer — a **virological** test is essential.

**MUST KNOW:** Test at **6 weeks** (or at birth for high-risk), repeat as per protocol; a positive test needs a second sample for confirmation. Antibody testing becomes valid only **after 18 months**.

**REMEMBER:** *Under 18 months = DNA PCR, not antibody.*

---

## 193. Dengue Fluid Therapy

**PEARL:** In dengue with warning signs, use **crystalloids (NS/RL)**:
- **With documented hypotension: 10–20 mL/kg/hour**
- **Without hypotension: 7 mL/kg/hour** (then taper 5 → 3 mL/kg/h as the patient improves)

**MUST KNOW:** The critical (leakage) phase begins with **defervescence**; monitor **haematocrit + platelets + urine output**. A **rising haematocrit with falling platelets** signals plasma leakage; a **falling haematocrit** may mean bleeding. Colloids are reserved for refractory shock. Avoid NSAIDs/aspirin.

**REMEMBER:** *No hypotension = 7 mL/kg/h; hypotension = 10–20 mL/kg/h.*

---

## 194. Paediatric DKA — Initial Management

**PEARL:** ABC first, then **0.9% normal saline 20 mL/kg bolus** and an **insulin infusion at 0.1 U/kg/hr**.

**MUST KNOW:**
- **Start insulin only after 1–2 hours of fluid** — and **never give an insulin bolus** in children (cerebral oedema risk).
- **Cerebral oedema is the leading cause of death** in paediatric DKA — treat with mannitol/hypertonic saline; rehydrate over **48 hours**.
- Add **dextrose** when glucose falls to ~250–300 mg/dL, continuing insulin until ketosis clears; replace **potassium** early (total body K is depleted despite a normal serum level).

**REMEMBER:** *Fluid first, no insulin bolus, watch for cerebral oedema.*

---

## 195. Fluid of Choice in Children

**PEARL:** **DNS (dextrose normal saline)** — providing water, electrolytes and glucose.

**MUST KNOW:** **Isotonic** fluid is preferred for maintenance in children; hypotonic fluids risk **hyponatraemia** (SIADH is common in ill children). **Holliday–Segar** maintenance: 100 mL/kg for the first 10 kg, 50 mL/kg for the next 10, 20 mL/kg thereafter.

**REMEMBER:** *Isotonic + dextrose = DNS; hypotonic fluid drops sodium.*

---

## 196. Tumour Lysis Syndrome

**PEARL:** **Aggressive hydration** is the first-line management, to prevent renal failure and flush out released intracellular contents.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Biochemistry: **high potassium, high phosphate, high uric acid, LOW calcium** (bound by phosphate).
- **Rasburicase** for high-risk/established TLS (**contraindicated in G6PD deficiency**); allopurinol for prophylaxis.
- **Urinary alkalinization is no longer recommended** (worsens calcium phosphate precipitation).
- Commonest with **Burkitt lymphoma and T-ALL** (high tumour burden).

**REMEMBER:** *Hydrate hard; K, PO4, urate up — calcium down.*

---

## 197. Febrile Seizures — Recurrence Risk

**PEARL:** Recurrence risk is increased by: **age under 1 year**, a **relatively low peak temperature (38–39°C)**, and a **short duration of fever (<24 hours) before the seizure**.

**UNDERSTAND:** All three mean a **low seizure threshold** — the child fits with only a small fever provocation, so ordinary illnesses will do it again.

**MUST KNOW:** Also: family history of febrile seizures. Overall recurrence ~30–35%. **Simple** febrile seizure = generalized, <15 min, once in 24 h, age 6 months–5 years — needs no EEG, imaging or long-term antiepileptics.

**REMEMBER:** *Young age + low fever + fits early = it will happen again.*

---

## 198. Juvenile Myoclonic Epilepsy

**PEARL:** **GABRA1** is a key gene in juvenile myoclonic epilepsy.

**MUST KNOW:** Early-morning **myoclonic jerks** (dropping the toothbrush/teacup) + generalized tonic-clonic seizures ± absences in a teenager; precipitated by **sleep deprivation, alcohol and photic stimulation**. EEG: **4–6 Hz polyspike-and-wave**. **Valproate** is most effective (levetiracetam/lamotrigine in women of childbearing age); **treatment is usually lifelong** — relapse is the rule on withdrawal. **Carbamazepine and phenytoin worsen it.**

**REMEMBER:** *Morning jerks in a teenager = JME — valproate, and don't stop it.*

---

## 199. EHPVO

**PEARL:** Haematemesis, melaena and splenomegaly in a **child** = portal hypertension, and **extrahepatic portal vein obstruction** is the commonest cause in children/adolescents.

**MUST KNOW:** **Liver function is preserved** (well-grown child, normal LFTs, no ascites or encephalopathy) — the block is **pre-hepatic**. Ultrasound Doppler shows a **portal cavernoma**. Treatment: **endoscopic variceal ligation/sclerotherapy**; shunt surgery (Meso-Rex) if it fails.

**REMEMBER:** *Bleeding + big spleen + normal liver in a child = EHPVO.*

---

## 200. Hirschsprung Disease

**PEARL:** **Suction rectal biopsy** is the standard diagnostic test — it confirms the **absence of enteric ganglion cells** (with hypertrophied nerve trunks and raised acetylcholinesterase).

**MUST KNOW:** Suspect with **delayed passage of meconium (>48 h)**, constipation and abdominal distension; **rectal examination gives an explosive gush of stool**. Barium enema shows a **transition zone** with a **reversed rectosigmoid index**. **Anorectal manometry shows absent recto-anal inhibitory reflex**. Definitive treatment: pull-through surgery. Dreaded complication: **enterocolitis**.

**REMEMBER:** *No ganglion cells on suction biopsy = Hirschsprung.*

---

## 201. Thalassaemia — Investigations

**PEARL:** **HPLC and peripheral blood smear** are the key initial investigations; **globin gene sequencing is the definitive (confirmatory) test**.

**MUST KNOW:** HPLC shows **raised HbA2 (>3.5%)** in β-thalassaemia trait and **raised HbF** in major. Smear: microcytic hypochromic anaemia with **target cells, basophilic stippling, nucleated RBCs**, and a **normal/high RBC count with a low Mentzer index (MCV/RBC <13)** — distinguishing it from iron deficiency.

**REMEMBER:** *Screen with HPLC, confirm with gene sequencing; Mentzer <13 = thalassaemia.*

---

## 202. Hypophosphataemic Rickets

**PEARL:** **Low serum phosphate + NORMAL calcium and PTH + increased urinary phosphate** (renal phosphate wasting), usually from **PHEX** mutation (X-linked dominant).

**UNDERSTAND:** PHEX loss → excess **FGF23** → phosphaturia and suppressed 1,25-(OH)2 vitamin D.

**MUST KNOW:**
- Treatment: **oral phosphate + calcitriol** (not plain vitamin D); **burosumab** (anti-FGF23) is the newer targeted therapy.
- Contrast **nutritional rickets**: **low calcium, HIGH PTH and high alkaline phosphatase**, low vitamin D — responds to vitamin D.

**REMEMBER:** *Normal calcium and PTH + low phosphate = renal phosphate leak, not vitamin D deficiency.*

---

## 203. Cystic Fibrosis — Electrolytes

**PEARL:** **Hyperkalaemia is NOT seen in cystic fibrosis** — patients develop **hypokalaemia, hyponatraemia and hypochloraemic metabolic (contraction) alkalosis** from salt loss in sweat.

**UNDERSTAND:** Defective CFTR prevents chloride (and hence sodium) reabsorption in the sweat duct → salt-losing state, especially in hot weather, with secondary hyperaldosteronism driving potassium loss.

**MUST KNOW:** Diagnosis: **sweat chloride >60 mmol/L**. This "pseudo-Bartter syndrome" presentation can be the first clue in an infant.

**REMEMBER:** *CF loses salt in sweat → hypokalaemic hypochloraemic alkalosis.*

---

## 204. Fetal Adrenal Gland

**PEARL:** The fetal adrenal produces large amounts of **DHEA(-S)**, the **precursor for placental estrogen synthesis**.

**UNDERSTAND:** The placenta lacks 17α-hydroxylase, so it cannot make androgens itself — it depends on **fetal adrenal DHEAS**, which it aromatizes to estriol. Hence **maternal estriol reflects fetal wellbeing**, and is low in anencephaly and placental sulfatase deficiency.

**REMEMBER:** *Fetal adrenal makes DHEAS → placenta makes estriol → estriol measures the fetus.*

---

## 205. Lower Limb Perforators

**PEARL:**
- **Cockett's perforators** — **distal calf/ankle**, connecting the **posterior arch vein to the posterior tibial vein**.
- **Boyd's perforator** — **proximal calf**, connecting the **great saphenous vein to the posterior tibial vein**.

**MUST KNOW:** Incompetence causes venous hypertension and varicose veins.

**REMEMBER:** *Boyd is high (proximal), Cockett is low (ankle).*

---

## 206. Venous Ulcers

**PEARL:** Venous ulcers form in the **gaiter region** — between the calf muscles and the ankle — most often on the **medial side below the malleolus**, where the **Cockett perforators** join.

**MUST KNOW:** Features: shallow, sloping edge, granulation base, **painless**, with **lipodermatosclerosis, haemosiderin pigmentation and atrophie blanche**. Treatment: **graduated compression bandaging** (only after excluding arterial disease with an **ABPI >0.8**).

**EXAM CONNECTION:** **Arterial ulcers** are punched-out, painful, and occur over pressure points/toes; **neuropathic ulcers** are painless and on the sole/heads of metatarsals.

**REMEMBER:** *Medial gaiter area = venous; compress it.*

---

## 207. Axillary Temperature

**PEARL:** Keep the thermometer in the axilla for **3 minutes** in toddlers.

**MUST KNOW:** Axillary temperature reads about **0.5°C lower** than core; **rectal** temperature is the gold standard but avoided routinely (perforation, discomfort, neutropenia).

**REMEMBER:** *Axilla 3 minutes, and add 0.5°C.*

---

## 208. World Rabies Day

**PEARL:** **28th September** — the death anniversary of **Louis Pasteur**.

**REMEMBER:** *Rabies Day = Pasteur's death day, 28 September.*

---

## 209. Vitamin A Prophylaxis Dosing

**PEARL:**
- **<6 months: 50,000 IU**
- **6–12 months: 1 lakh (100,000) IU**
- **>12 months: 2 lakh (200,000) IU**
- **>12 months but weighing <8 kg: 1 lakh IU**

**MUST KNOW:** Under the national programme, **9 doses** are given — the first with measles vaccine at **9 months (1 lakh IU)**, then **2 lakh IU 6-monthly up to 5 years**. Also give vitamin A in **measles, severe malnutrition and xerophthalmia** (days 1, 2 and 14). Overdose → **bulging fontanelle/pseudotumor cerebri**.

**REMEMBER:** *50k under 6 months, 1 lakh in infancy, 2 lakh after 1 year — but weight <8 kg means halve it.*

---

## 210. Sodium in Breast Milk

**PEARL:** Breast milk has a **low sodium content (~7 mEq/L)** — matched to the infant's limited renal concentrating capacity.

**UNDERSTAND:** The newborn kidney cannot excrete a large solute load, so a low-sodium, low-solute feed meets nutritional needs without straining it. This is exactly why **cow's milk (≈3× the sodium and protein) is unsuitable under 1 year** — it risks hypernatraemic dehydration and renal solute overload.

**REMEMBER:** *7 mEq/L — breast milk is designed for the immature kidney.*

---

## 211. CSF Production Rate in a Child

**PEARL:** CSF is produced at approximately **20 mL/hour** in a child (≈500 mL/day in adults, ~0.35 mL/min).

**MUST KNOW:** Total CSF volume ≈ **150 mL in adults** (about 50 mL in infants) — so the entire volume is **replaced 3–4 times a day**. Produced mainly by the **choroid plexus**, absorbed by the **arachnoid villi**. **Acetazolamide reduces production**; obstruction anywhere along the pathway causes **hydrocephalus** — which in an infant enlarges the head, since the sutures are open.

**REMEMBER:** *20 mL/hour, turned over 3–4 times daily.*

---

## 212. Russell–Silver Syndrome

**PEARL:** Most cases are **sporadic**, caused by **hypomethylation at chromosome 11p15** or **maternal uniparental disomy of chromosome 7**.

**MUST KNOW:** Features: **severe intrauterine and postnatal growth restriction with a relatively large head ("pseudohydrocephalus")**, a **triangular face with a prominent forehead**, **body asymmetry/hemihypotrophy**, **clinodactyly of the 5th finger**, and feeding difficulty with hypoglycaemia. Treated with nutritional support and **growth hormone**. It is an **imprinting disorder** — the mirror image of **Beckwith–Wiedemann syndrome**, which involves the *same* 11p15 region but with overgrowth.

**REMEMBER:** *Small body, big head, triangular face, asymmetry — 11p15 or maternal UPD 7.*

---
